family, R-050/R-054 screened *listed* closed-end-fund/BDC price-to-NAV and *unlisted* interval-fund
repurchase families, R-051 screened catastrophe bonds, R-053 screened electricity-derivative contract pricing,
and the earlier template screens (A–I) were state-kind screens, not family screens. The municipal market is
also, uniquely in the register's corpus, a market where the **venue itself publishes the cost measure that
would define the candidate** (S-369), where the **published prior art is an open-access journal synthesis of
exactly this family** (S-372), and where the **regulator's own rule is a closed-list anti-omission rule whose
wording is the source of one of the four named elements** (S-366). It was the last untried family of the
register's standing "named absent families" list.

**Sources.** **S-365** (MSRB Rule G-17 and its 2021 Interpretive Notice, **line-verified** from the MSRB's own
rule page); **S-366** (17 CFR 240.15c2-12, **line-verified** from the eCFR's current text); **S-367** (the
MSRB's own data-product and subscription pages, **line-verified**: the RTRS historical product, its **three-year
lag** and its **anonymised dealer identifiers**); **S-368** (MSRB, *Third Quarter 2026 Municipal Securities
Market Summary*, **line-verified**: the BVAL benchmark yields, the published Muni/TSY ratio and its definition,
the bid-wanted totals, the fund-flow and ATS statistics); **S-369** (Wu & Ostroy, *Transaction Costs for
Municipal Securities Odd-Lot Trades*, MSRB Research Paper, April 2026, **line-verified**); **S-370** (Bagley &
Vieira, *Convergence of Individual and Institutional Trading Dynamics in Small Size Trades*, MSRB Research
Paper, February 2025, **line-verified**); **S-371** (Bagley, Vieira & Wu, *Analysis of Municipal Securities
Trade Reporting Times, 2022–2025*, MSRB Research Paper, August 2026, **line-verified**); **S-372** (Griffin,
Hirschey & Kruger, *Journal of Economic Perspectives* 39(2), Spring 2025, 235–260, **line-verified** from the
University of Nebraska–Lincoln institutional-repository copy of the published version); **S-373** (MSRB,
*Glossary of Municipal Securities Terms*, Third Edition, August 2013, **line-verified**); **S-374** (Ostroy,
Vieira & Bagley, *Major Trends in the Municipal Securities Primary Market*, MSRB Research Paper, September 2025,
**line-verified**); **S-375** (MSRB press release on the draft amendments to Rule G-32, 10 September 2026,
**line-verified** — a **dated watch item only**).

**Reason 1 — the effect is published, measured and quantified; a candidate here is a trade specification on a
published relation, not a discovery (rediscovery guard).** The customer cost premium in this market is not a
hypothesis; it is a number that the **venue itself measures and publishes, quarterly, in its own research
series**. The MSRB's own paper reports that the average effective spread for municipal transactions "shot up
to **63 basis points** in May 2022" and that "As of late 2025, the average effective spread was approximately
**50 basis points**"; that "the effective spread for odd-lot customer trades is **2.4 times** that for block
customer trades"; and that "Both the average and median effective spreads for odd-lot customer trades
executed by other dealers are nearly **2.7 times** as much as those for odd-lot customer trades executed by
institutional dealers" (S-369). The peer-reviewed synthesis puts the same fact in round-trip terms: "retail
investors' trading costs for municipal bonds were 2.3 percent in the early 2000s … they were still **1.1
percent in 2023**", and "we estimate that **5 percent of small bond transactions have markups over 3.29
percent** in 2023" (S-372). And the venue publishes the **decay** as well: institutional customers' share of
odd-lot customer trades "rose from 10% in 2015 to **44% in 2024**", with the paper's own conclusion that "it
is highly likely that at least 50% of odd-lot customer transactions occurred with institutional clients"
(S-370). A candidate in this family would therefore be a **rule for trading a published spread and a published
erosion trend** — exactly the shape the register has already refused six times (R-004, R-006, R-007, R-008,
R-041, R-050) — and it would be *re-presenting* the finding rather than discovering it. The register's
rediscovery guard is engaged at the first step.

**Reason 2 — there is no published decision-time state: the pre-trade quote that would reveal a dealer's
one-way bid is not consolidated, the market's transactions are disclosed only *after* they occur, and the
trades in which a concession is most extreme are the ones that leave no record.** The published synthesis is
explicit: "Electronic trading platforms exist only in the interdealer market, with no widely used platform
for individuals to trade directly with each other. **Municipal bond transactions are publicly disclosed after
they occur, but individual investors have limited pre-trade information, particularly with respect to quoted
prices**" (S-372). The MSRB's own research says the same of the market it regulates: "In fixed-income
securities markets, **pre-trade quote data are not universally available or nationally consolidated**"
(S-369). What *is* available is a product: real-time municipal trade data is a **paid subscription** ("MSRB
Transaction Subscription Service: All municipal trade data reported to the MSRB by municipal securities
dealers"), and the historical academic product carries a **three-year lag** and **anonymised dealer
identifiers** (S-367). The benchmark that prices the whole market is a **proprietary** series — "yields on
**Bloomberg Valuation Services (BVAL)**" — and even the *bid-wanted* state is observed on a proprietary
terminal ("the amount of bid-wanteds from institutional customers on **Bloomberg** rose dramatically in
September"; "September 29 registered $3.8 billion in bid-wanteds") (S-368). Worse, the record is
**systematically absent exactly where the concession would be largest**: the exempt trade classes are "trades
flagged as being executed at the **List Offering or Takedown Transactions**, trades in short-term instruments
maturing in nine months or less, **Auction Rate Securities, Variable Rate Demand Obligations**, trades in
commercial paper, and trades '**away from market**'" (S-371). Two limbs of the register's own bar therefore
fail together: **G-12's sign-and-existence limb** (the state that would decide whether a dealer's bid is
"one-way" in the tradeable sense is not publicly observable) and **G-13's published-before-the-decision limb**
(the transaction record is post-trade by construction, and the academic product is three years late).

**Reason 3 — the "zero-omission rating disclosure" framing fails in the direction that helps a candidate: the
permitted omissions are a **closed list**, the rating's changes are **disclosable events**, and the rating's
price effect is already published and quantified.** The framing came from the rule's own words, but the rule
says the opposite of what the framing needs. 15c2-12(b)(1) requires that the underwriter "obtain and review
an official statement that an issuer of such securities deems final as of its date, **except for the omission
of no more than the following information**: The offering price(s), interest rate(s), selling compensation,
aggregate principal amount, principal amount per maturity, delivery dates, any other terms or provisions
required by an issuer of such securities to be specified in a competitive bid, **ratings**, other terms of the
securities depending on such matters, and the identity of the underwriter(s)" (S-366). The list is **closed**:
there is no unregulated disclosure gap in which a rating could be "zero-omitted". And the rating is not a
hidden state at all: 15c2-12(b)(5)(i)(C)(11) lists "**Rating changes**" among the events the issuer must
report "in a timely manner not in excess of **ten business days** after the occurrence of the event"
(S-366). A published, dated, pre-announced state is a **baseline** under the register's standing precedence,
not a concession. And the *effect* of the rating on the price is itself published: after the 2010 recalibration
produced "upgrades of zero to four credit rating notches on $2.2 trillion in municipal debt", the literature
"led to an economically large **10 to 33 basis point** decrease in credit spreads for the affected bonds",
and "the literature indicates that investors rely heavily on credit ratings, **even when changes in ratings
convey relatively little new information**" (S-372). The rating element therefore yields a **published
baseline with a published sign**, not an unclaimed state.

**Reason 4 — the tax-equivalent arithmetic is a deterministic transform of two published yield series and a
named, long-studied published puzzle; conditioning on it adds no information beyond the prices.** The venue
publishes the comparison and defines it: "The **Muni/TSY ratio** is the benchmark tax-exempt yield for a
maturity divided by the yield on Treasury bonds for the same maturity" (S-368). The arithmetic itself is
published as a long-standing puzzle with a name: "A longstanding puzzle in the municipal bond literature is
that **short-term municipal yields are consistent with the tax rates we would expect for investors in the top
income tax brackets, whereas longer-term municipal yields are higher than we would expect**" — with the
"inconsistent implied marginal tax rates" listed among the market's documented puzzles, alongside the finding
that "the **median marginal tax rate** of households that own municipal bonds is **25 percent**, and at least
one-fourth of households that own municipal bonds have a **zero percent** marginal tax rate", and the
state-tax clientele result that "bonds in high-tax states are priced with lower yields" (S-372). The tax
channel's economics are published too: "the tax subsidy to municipal debt **increases underwriter
competition**, which in turn leads to **lower underwriting fees**" (S-372). This is the register's existing
R-046 / R-035 pattern — a **published function of published prices**, and a **published literature** — and the
family's tax-equivalent conditional is a re-presentation of the municipal bond puzzle, not a discovery.

**Reason 5 — the tradeable object is a *published compensation* for a *published service*, the dated flow is
the **obligor's own** and pre-announced, and the "no-bid" state is a discretionary risk-management practice
rather than a published obligation.** The concession and the mark-up are **defined terms in the venue's own
glossary**: "**CONCESSION** – A price expressed in terms of a net offering price (in basis or dollar price
terms) minus a differential (in points or dollars per bond) granted between professionals"; "**MARK-UP** – A
form of remuneration received by a municipal securities dealer when selling securities as principal to a
customer"; "**BID WANTED** – The process by which an investor or municipal securities dealer actively
solicits bids on a position of securities from the marketplace"; "**PRINCIPAL TRADE** – A securities
transaction in which a municipal securities dealer effects the transaction for its own account" (S-373). A
mark-up is therefore **what the dealer is paid for doing the intermediation**, and the register's own cost
reason (R-052, R-053) applies: a published remuneration is a **cost, not a state**. The primary market's flow
is the **issuer's own** and overwhelmingly **negotiated**: "In 2015, negotiated offerings accounted for 68% of
the total par amount issued. In 2024, that number increased to **77%**" (S-374) — and the compensation
arrangement is itself a **disclosed term of the transaction**, because the Interpretive Notice requires that
"The sole underwriter or syndicate manager must disclose to issuers whether underwriting compensation will be
contingent on the closing of a transaction" and that the standard disclosures on the underwriter's role and
compensation be made "at or before the time the underwriter is engaged to perform underwriting services"
(S-365), so even the primary-market concession is a **published term of a published document**. And the
dealer-withdrawal state that the family would trade is, by construction, **unpublished and discretionary**: a
dealer's willingness to quote or to bid is a risk-management decision, and the rule that binds it binds
*conduct* — "Requires dealers and municipal advisors to deal fairly with all persons and not to engage in any
deceptive, dishonest or unfair practice", and "(v) the underwriter has a duty to purchase securities from the
issuer at a fair and reasonable price, but must balance that duty with its duty to sell municipal securities to
investors at prices that are fair and reasonable" — without publishing a price (S-365). The register's own bar is explicit: a state that is neither published in advance nor conditioned on a
priced, published quantity is not a state, and the dealer's bid is the **counterparty's risk appetite**, which
is exactly the class of state R-010 (agency credit) and IDEA-002 (funding) were built around, not a published
quantity.

**An honest finding, recorded as a framing correction and *not* as a sixth reason.** The Cycle-93 handoff
named "the **Muni Monday** no-bid window" as one of the four elements of this family. **It could not be
corroborated.** The phrase does not occur in the indexed research literature (OpenAlex full-text search for the
exact phrase returns one unrelated paper; the phrase with "municipal bond" returns zero), nor in the
peer-reviewed 26-page synthesis of this market (S-372), nor in the MSRB's own 120-page term list (S-373), nor
in any of the nine MSRB/regulator documents fetched in this cycle (zero occurrences of "Monday", "day of the
week" or "weekday"). The element is therefore treated as an **unverified label**, and **no reason in this
record rests on it**. The limitation is stated plainly: general web search was unavailable in this environment,
so **trade press** and dealer commentary — the most likely home of a phrase like this — were **not** searched;
that route remains open. This is recorded in the ledger's Cycle-94 honest-negatives table, and it is a
**correction to the handoff**, not a debt.

**Cycle-98 annotation — the open trade-press route was run this cycle, and it returned the same framing
correction.** The limitation named above ("general web search was unavailable in this environment, so
**trade press** and dealer commentary … were **not** searched; that route remains open") is now
**discharged as a route, and the result is a negative**: with the general web-search provider **restored**
this cycle, one bounded search was run for a day-of-week periodicity in the tax-exempt municipal market,
restricted to trade-press, venue and published sources. **No trade-press or venue source establishing a
dated municipal market periodicity was found.** The nearest published object is a **monthly** seasonality —
Cusatis & Tawatnuntachai, "Seasonality in the Municipal Bond Market", *Municipal Finance Journal* 31(4):
1–17 (2011), whose abstract (read in an index rendering, **not fetched**) reports "negative seasonal
returns for the municipal market in March from 1980 to 2007" and the January effect, and attributes the
March pattern to "an increase in municipal issuance in March and a lack of participation by individual
investors as buyers in anticipation of the April 15 tax deadline" — i.e. the published municipal
seasonality is a **monthly, tax-deadline** phenomenon, and no source found states a **weekly or Monday**
municipal effect. The day-of-the-week literature returned concerns **other** markets (GNMA and Treasury
futures, T-bills, junk bonds), not the tax-exempt municipal market. **Consequences, stated precisely:**
the "Muni Monday" element **remains an unverified label**, and it **remains a framing correction and not
a sixth reason**; the search result is a **route outcome, not evidence** (it was an index rendering, and
the monthly-seasonality abstract was not fetched, so it is used as a verification basis for nothing);
**R-055's re-screen condition (d) is NOT satisfied**, so **no re-screen is triggered**; and **R-055's
verdict, its five reasons, its known-baseline row 92 and its dated conditions (a)–(d) are unchanged**.

**Dated re-screen condition.** Re-screen only if (a) the MSRB, the SEC or Congress adopts **public,
real-time or intraday dissemination of municipal trade data**, or the other reform proposals in the published
synthesis are adopted — "improvements in the availability of information about prices and competitiveness of
price quotes for potential buyers" (S-372) — which would create the **published, decision-time quote state**
that Reason 2 shows to be absent; (b) a **dealer-level, publicly published** inventory or quoting state
appears, which the current academic product's **anonymised dealer identifiers** preclude (S-367); (c) the
**Rule G-32** dealer-disclosure amendment cycle completes — comments were due **14 December 2026** (S-375) —
in a way that changes the *published* dealer-disclosure regime; or (d) a **trade press** source establishes a
genuine, dated municipal market periodicity (the route left open by this cycle's honest negative). Absent
(a)–(d), the family stays a baseline.

**Decision:** **bounded negative (R-055).** The tax-exempt municipal bond market is a **baseline family, not a
frontier**. It is the register's clearest case of an *identified, published, quantified* inefficiency — the
venue measures it, the literature explains it, and the reform proposals are published too — which is the
opposite of an unclaimed, decision-time-state-determined, cost-surviving concession. The four elements named
in the Cycle-93 handoff are each disposed of: the **one-way bid** is real as market structure but is not
publicly observable at decision time (Reason 2) and the dealer's compensation is a published term (Reason 5);
the **"Muni Monday" no-bid window** is **uncorroborated** (framing correction above); the **zero-omission
rating disclosure** is a **closed-list rule** whose product is a published, dated state with a published price
effect (Reason 3); and the **tax-equivalent arithmetic** is a **published transform of published prices** and a
named published puzzle (Reason 4). **No `IDEA-` identifier is minted, no validation specification is written,
no gap is opened, and no score changes.** The family is written to the known-baseline table as **row 92**.

**Cycle-96 annotation — the prior art is strengthened; the verdict, the five reasons and row 92 are unchanged.**
The Cycle-96 provenance operation ("complete the municipal scholarship") obtained the paper this record has been
carrying as an **honest negative** since Cycle 94: **Griffin, Hirschey and Kruger (2023), "Do Municipal Bond Dealers
Give Their Customers 'Fair and Reasonable' Pricing?", *The Journal of Finance* 78(2), 887–934 (S-378)** — the
**published version of record**, fetched and text-extracted (**48 pages; 147,579 characters**). A second entry,
**S-379**, carries the **deposited abstract** of the Green, Hollifield and Schürhoff working paper and is
**abstract-level evidence only**. Both **strengthen existing reasons** and add **no sixth reason**:

- **Reason 1** is strengthened at the peer-reviewed, dealer level: "53% (47%) of dealers charge median markups of
  more than 1% in the new (seasoned) issue market"; "In the new issue market, the difference between the 10% most
  expensive and 10% least expensive purchase prices for small trades is at least 0.5% on 61% of trading days";
  and "Over the 6.5-year sample, the total value of small trade markups (for purchase transactions) in excess of
  2% is $479 million".
- **Reason 2** is strengthened: dealer-level markups are measurable **only** from the **regulator's proprietary
  data set with dealer identifiers** for July 2011 through December 2017, through the regulator's own access; the
  public tape is **15-minute lagged** ("Since January 2005, all municipal bond transactions have been publicly
  posted with a 15-minute lag"); and the paper itself records "Practical challenges to accessing pricing
  information for typical retail investors".
- **Reason 5** is strengthened: the compensation is a **regulated, published object** — "MSRB Rule G-30 requires
  that commissions and markups for transactions with dealers must be 'fair and reasonable'" — and its level is
  **published, measured and explained**, including the published null that "We find no evidence that median or
  extreme markups decreased following MSRB rule changes requiring best execution in March 2016".

**Nothing else changed.** The **verdict** (bounded negative), the **five reasons**, the **known-baseline row 92**,
the **dated re-screen conditions (a)–(d)** and the **"Muni Monday" framing correction** are unchanged. The
**known limitation on reason 5 is narrowed, not discharged**: the *content* of the canonical opaque-market
statement is now inside the register's corpus (S-379 at abstract level, and quoted inside the fetched S-378), but
the **bodies** of Harris & Piwowar (2006) and Green, Hollifield & Schürhoff (2007) remain closed and **neither is
used as a verification basis for any reason in this record**. The failed routes are recorded in the ledger's
Cycle-96 honest-negatives table. **No `IDEA-`, `V-`, `R-`, `P-` or `G-` identifier was consumed and no score
changed.**

## R-056 — Accelerated share repurchases: the announcement-date public-observability of the discount and of the intermediary's covering window (Cycle 108 pre-registered screen — bounded negative)

**What was screened.** Not a new mechanism family: this is a **state-availability screen of a family the
register already holds as a baseline** (known-baseline **row 44**, "Accelerated share repurchases"). The
question the Cycle-107 handoff named as the register's ASR state's actual load-bearing question is:
**what was publicly knowable, at the announcement date, about (i) the discount and (ii) the intermediary's
covering window — and is the resulting state decision-time and free?** Cycle 107 made the question
answerable for the first time by registering **S-384**, the actual executed ASR contract. Cycle 108
executed the screen.

**The pre-registration (written into `cycle_notes.md` before the fetches, and reproduced here).** Limb A
— *public-observability of the contract's terms*: **POSITIVE** if the executed confirmation is a public
exhibit to the announcement filing, in which case the baseline row's sentence "the discount is
private-contract data" is **too strong** and must be **narrowed in place**. Limb B — *public-observability
of the discount*: **NEGATIVE** if the discount is a **terminal** quantity — defined against a settlement
price struck over a **future** window the contract sets — so that no announcement-time observation
identifies the discount that will be realised; this is itself a registrable result. Limb C — *decision-time
and free?*: positive only if the state is computable from public information at the announcement date
**and** is not already published as a tradeable result. Falsification conditions: (F-1) a filed exhibit
disclosing the realised or contractually fixed discount at the announcement date falsifies Limb B;
(F-2) Rule 10b-18 not being a published *ex ante* constraint on the covering window falsifies the
"published ex ante bound" sub-claim of Limb A; (F-3) the confirmation **not** being a public exhibit
falsifies Limb A. Execution was limited to reading primary legal and regulatory text; **no backtest,
simulation, parameter sweep, significance test, performance or risk metric, market-data acquisition or
processing, strategy or signal implementation, executable infrastructure, workflow or trading.**

**Reason 1 — the contract architecture IS public at the announcement date (Limb A POSITIVE, and this
corrects the baseline rather than killing the family).** The executed instrument is not private: the
**Form 8-K's own Item 1.01** states that "the Accelerated Share Repurchase Master Confirmation, and the
Supplemental Confirmation attached thereto, dated January 19, 2006, **which are attached hereto as
Exhibit 99.1** and are incorporated herein by reference" (S-386), and Exhibit 99.1 is the contract the
register already holds as **S-384**. The **announcement filing body** itself also carries the headline
commercial term: "The initial price paid per common share was **$25.97**" (S-386), and the **announcement
press release** (Exhibit 99.3, S-387) carries the pricing principle. So the register's standing sentence
on row 44 — "the mechanism, the pricing and the announcement effects are published, and **the discount is
private-contract data**" — is **too strong as written** and is **narrowed in place** below. The
*falsification condition F-3* is **not** triggered: the confirmation is a public exhibit.

**Reason 2 — the quantity that carries the economics is TERMINAL, and the *initial* discount is zero by
construction (Limb B NEGATIVE).** The press release states that the shares "are expected to be purchased
from Goldman Sachs **based on the current market price at the time of the contract signing**" (S-387) —
so the $25.97 initial price *is* the prevailing market price and there is **no initial discount** to
observe. The whole economic content of an ASR is the **contingent** adjustment, and the announcement
filing defines it as a future quantity: "The repurchased common shares will be subject to a **future
contingent purchase price adjustment expected to be settled at the end of the purchase period in the first
or second quarter of 2006, based on the volume weighted average share price during the actual repurchase
period**" (S-386). The settlement price is struck over a window the contract itself sets — a "Valuation
Date: A day not exceeding **4 months** after the Trade Date" (S-384) — and the contract's compensation
mechanics (the ex-dividend "Counterparty Additional Payment Amount" and the settlement price "reduced by
the per Share amount of the underwriting discount and/or commissions", S-384) are *rules*, not a number.
**No announcement-time observation identifies the discount that will be realised.** Falsification
condition **F-1 is not triggered**: no filed instrument discloses the realised or a contractually fixed
discount at the announcement date.

**Reason 3 — the effect's sign and expected magnitude were PUBLISHED BY THE ISSUER, at the announcement
date, in the announcement filing itself.** The 8-K says: "**At the present time, the Company does not
expect that the purchase price adjustment will be material.**" (S-386). The register is not being asked
to discover a magnitude that its own corpus has not already published; here the *issuer* published the
expected sign and size of the very quantity a candidate would need, and published it in the same
document that publishes the trade. The mechanism and the pricing are peer-reviewed (S-166, S-167) and the
announcement effects are now primary-verified (S-386, S-387). This is the register's "already-claimed"
limb in its purest form, and it is fatal to the family as a *discovery*.

**Reason 4 — the covering window is bounded EX ANTE by published law and by the contract's own cap, so
the state is BOUNDED rather than point-identified — and the bound is not the tradeable object.** The
constraint the contract keys itself to is now line-verified in the rule itself (S-388): "**ADTV** means
the average daily trading volume reported for the security during the **four calendar weeks** preceding
the week in which the Rule 10b-18 purchase is to be effected"; the daily cap is "**must not exceed 25
percent of the ADTV** for that security", with a once-a-week block-purchase alternative that the contract
expressly disclaims ("the applicable maximum amount of Shares permitted under Rule 10b-18(b)(4), **without
allowing for any 'block purchases'** (as defined therein)", S-384); and the purchases must be "from or
through only **one broker or dealer** on any single day" at a price that "does not exceed the highest
independent bid" (S-388). So the *bound* on the covering window is **decision-time and public** — a
genuine result, and the positive half of Limb C. But the **realised** path, the realised VWAP and the
realised adjustment are not observable at the announcement date, and the adjustment is expected by the
issuer to be immaterial (Reason 3). The state therefore exists but is **bounded, not point-identified**,
and its *content* is a published rule rather than a discriminating state.

**Reason 5 — the sample and the regime are adverse, and the family is a baseline in its own right.** This
is **one 2006 transaction** by a listed bank holding company, in the pre-crisis regime, with 2.5 million
shares against a 3.0-million-share authorisation "approximately **3.6%** of the Company's outstanding
shares" (S-387) — a scale at which no cross-sectional claim can be built. The family's mechanism,
pricing and announcement effects are already in the register as **published, peer-reviewed** (S-166,
S-167), the contract architecture is now primary-verified (S-384, S-386), and the announcement-date
information set is now primary-verified (S-386, S-387).

**Decision:** **bounded negative (R-056).** The accelerated-share-repurchase family is a **baseline family,
not a frontier**, and the specific question the register had carried for two cycles is now **closed in
both directions**. The **positive** finding is that the contract architecture is **public** — the
executed confirmation was a filed exhibit and the initial price was disclosed in the announcement filing —
so the register's baseline wording is **narrowed in place** rather than left standing. The **negative**
finding is that the quantity with the economics is **terminal**: the initial price is the market price at
signing by construction, the adjustment is settled at the end of a future window on that window's VWAP,
the covering window is bounded ex ante by published law rather than by a discriminating state, and the
issuer published its own expectation of the adjustment at the announcement date. **No `IDEA-` identifier is
minted, no validation specification is written, no gap is opened, no re-screen of any retained candidate
is triggered, and no score changes.** The correction lands in the known-baseline table as an in-place
narrowing of **row 44**.

**What is left open, stated precisely.** Two things, and neither is a research task the register can
execute: (i) the **realised** purchase-price adjustment of the FirstMerit transaction is disclosed
after the fact in the issuer's periodic reports and is **not** used here — the register records the
announcement-date information set, not the outcome; (ii) the screen was executed on **one** transaction
and **one** rule version, so it establishes what is knowable *in the documented class of transactions where
the confirmation is filed*, and it does **not** establish that other ASR issuers file their confirmations.
The second point is the reason no gap was opened: it is a **scope** limitation on an already-bounded
negative, and every one of the five reasons above is independent of it.

## R-057 — IDEA-011's "first adopter versus repeat adopter" classification: is the state available at the adoption date? (Cycle 109 pre-registered screen, P-3 — bounded negative on the prospective arm, bounded positive on the historical comparator)

**What was screened.** The register's rank-1 candidate (IDEA-011, IFRS 18 adoption and the information
environment in the transition year) conditions on a **classification**: the candidate's own claim is a
**comparability clock** — the drift in reported-earnings information quality is larger in **first-time
adopters** of a standard whose transition creates a comparability break than in **repeat (second-time)
adopters**. The question screened is **not** whether the mechanism is published (G-21 closed that: the
literature search is exhausted and the comparator being unrun and unused is settled) but whether the
**classification is determinable at the decision date** — R-056's form, applied to IDEA-011.

**The pre-registered question and falsification condition.** Three limbs: (A) is the classification's
**architecture** observable — does a published instrument name, for the candidate's jurisdictions, which
firms/jurisdictions are first-time adopters and which are repeat adopters? (B) is it **decision-time** — can
first-adopter status be fixed by information available at the decision date, rather than assigned ex post?
(C) is the state **free** — computable at no cost from public information, and not already published as a
tradeable result? The falsification condition fixed in advance: **a published, prospective adoption calendar
that fixes first-adopter status before the effective date for the candidate's jurisdictions** would overturn
a negative; and the pre-registration named, in advance, the three things that do **not** count — a mandatory
application date (a deadline, not a status), a voluntary early-adoption permission (self-selected and
announced firm-by-firm after the fact), and a status directory (status in principle, no dated transition
schedule).

**Verdict — BOUNDED NEGATIVE on the prospective arm, BOUNDED POSITIVE on the historical comparator's
classifier, with one named residual.**

- **Limb A is POSITIVE for the framework-level classification and NEGATIVE for the standard-level one.** The
  **IFRS Foundation's per-jurisdiction profile documents** — reached through a route form the register had not
  tried (the profile *pages* are client-side rendered; each profile's *content* is served as a per-jurisdiction
  **PDF**, `…/jurisdictions/pdf-profiles/<jurisdiction>-ifrs-profile.pdf`, all four fetched directly:
  **HTTP 200** — European Union 149,740 bytes, 8 pages, 19,713 characters; Saudi Arabia 126,760 bytes, 6
  pages, 14,921 characters; Nigeria 119,901 bytes, 6 pages, 12,870 characters; Ghana 115,469 bytes, 4 pages,
  9,175 characters) — carry a **dated first-adoption fact** in their "status of adoption" narrative: the EU
  "adopted IFRS Standards as the required financial reporting standards … **effective in 2005**"; Ghana's
  Institute "adopted IFRS Standards in place of Ghana National Accounting Standards" in **2007**, effective
  **1 January 2007**; Nigeria's Federal Executive Council "approved **1 January 2012** as the effective date
  for adoption of IFRS Standards in Nigeria" on **28 July 2010**. So the *historical* first-time-versus-repeat
  label is computable at the decision date from published, dated, standard-setter material — and it is free.
  But **no profile carries an IFRS 18 date, or even an IFRS 18 mention** ("IFRS 18" **0 hits** in all four),
  and the profiles' own "last updated" dates (**16 June 2016** for Ghana and Nigeria, **28 July 2022** for
  Saudi Arabia, **2 October 2024** for the EU) all **predate or barely postdate IFRS 18's April 2024
  issuance**; the profile PDFs are therefore **not** an IFRS 18 adoption calendar, and the register's Cycle-53
  limb (a) verdict is **confirmed in substance and corrected in scope** (see S-254).
- **Limb B is NEGATIVE for the prospective arm.** The only published instrument that partitions *firms* for
  the candidate's live window is **Saudi Arabia's CMA early-adoption permission** (S-270), and its own text
  settles the question: listed joint-stock companies "**may early adopt** IFRS 18 … during 2026 **by
  announcing financial statements prepared in accordance with that Standard and the effect of such early
  adoption on the Saudi Exchange (Tadawul) website**" — the arm is **self-selected**, the **only** disclosure
  is an **ex-post announcement of the early-adopted financial statements**, and there is **no pre-announcement
  list, registry of intent, or declaration date**. So which firms will early-adopt is **not knowable at any
  decision date before 1 January 2027**. The **supervisor's own instrument** is no better: the ESMA public
  statement (S-267, re-fetched directly this cycle — **HTTP 200, 390,994 bytes, 10 pages, 36,978 characters**)
  is jurisdiction-uniform and contains **no** first-time/repeat-adopter language at all ("first time" 0 hits,
  "first-time" 0, "early adopt" 0, "repeat" 0, "voluntar" 0, "jurisdiction" 0, "calendar" 0): it fixes the
  **deadline** ("effective date of application of the Standard is 1 January 2027 with earlier application
  [permitted]"), which is exactly what the pre-registration said it would do. A second structural fact
  reinforces the negative: where a jurisdiction is a genuine first-time adopter of the IFRS *package* in the
  IFRS 18 era, the whole package arrives at once (S-271, Saudi Arabia's SOCPA), so the **standard-specific**
  comparability break is **not separable** from the package break.
- **Limb C is POSITIVE for the framework-level classifier** (published, dated, standard-setter, no
  construction) **and NEGATIVE for the firm-level voluntary flag** (self-selected, ex-post). The
  *mechanism's* own conditioning state — the enforcement regime and reporting-quality environment in the
  transition year — is separately and already decision-time observable (the venue's inspection and enforcement
  regime, the audit-committee changes in the published 2026 interim reports, S-281 … S-286), so this screen
  does **not** touch the candidate's live state.
- **The historical comparator is bounded positive with one named residual.** The discriminator the
  *unrun* comparison (G-21) needs is the label "had this firm already applied IFRS before the new
  standard arrived?", and for the 2005 event that label is derivable at the decision date from two published
  sources: the **jurisdiction's own dated first-adoption fact** (above) and **each firm's own published
  prior-year basis-of-preparation note** (which states the framework applied in the comparative period). The
  **named residual**, outside this cycle's pre-registered execution bounds and therefore **not examined**:
  the IASB's **published list of entities permitted to apply IFRS early in 2005**, which would fix the
  firm-level "repeat adopter" flag *prospectively* and is the one instrument that could strengthen the
  positive limb.

**Dated re-screen condition.** Re-screen only if (a) the **IFRS Foundation publishes an IFRS 18 adoption
calendar** — per jurisdiction, with application dates, and ideally with the framework's first-adoption date
alongside it — so that the standard-level classification exists prospectively; or (b) a **prospective
early-adoption registry** is published (a list of firms that will apply IFRS 18 early, published before
1 January 2027, by a regulator or a venue), so that the firm-level arm becomes decision-time; or (c) the
**IASB's 2005 early-adopter list** is obtained and read, to convert the historical comparator's positive
limb from "derivable from published accounts" to "published prospectively".

**Decision: bounded negative (R-057).** The screen's product is a **rediscovery guard and a narrowing of
IDEA-011's identification**, not a candidate rejection: **no `IDEA-` identifier is minted, no validation
specification is written, no gap is opened, no re-screen of any retained candidate is triggered, and no score
changes.** The register's own record of the classification's *architecture* is corrected where it was too
pessimistic (S-254 now carries the dated first-adoption facts), and the candidate's residual is **stated
precisely**: the historical comparator is **specifiable without look-ahead** (so the G-21 comparison can be
run without a hindsight-classification objection), while the **prospective group A / group B partition in the
IFRS 18 window is not decision-time** and the register's G-32 limb (a) closure is **confirmed on a stronger
ground** than before. The three audits executed alongside it (P-4, P-5, P-6) are recorded in the Cycle-109
gap-verdict block of `source_ledger.md` and in the three dossiers' Cycle-109 blocks; they confirm existing
positions and consume no identifier.

## R-059 — IDEA-009's prior-art screen: the two decisive questions the Cycle-109 P-5 audit named (Cycle 112 pre-registered screen, P-2 — bounded negative on both questions, with a materially adverse finding that re-scored the candidate)

**What was screened.** The Cycle-109 P-5 audit closed with a sentence that named the only two findings that
would change IDEA-009's verdict: "A published test of the **unconditional** form on a venue other than the
two already covered, or a published result establishing that the **conditional** form's edge is absent." This
entry records the execution of that screen. It is **not** a rejection of the candidate: the candidate is
retained, re-scored down, and its specification is amended. The entry exists so that the negative reasoning
is preserved and so that the family is not re-searched in this form.

**The pre-registered question and falsification condition.** Limb (A): a published test of the unconditional
funding-rate-level reversal on a venue other than Binance or BitMEX (the venues of S-310 and S-320). Limb
(B): a published result that the direction-conditioned form's edge is absent or fails out of sample, beyond
the book-chapter failure already on file as S-319. The falsification rule was fixed before the first fetch: a
new source moves a score **only** if it bears on the **sign** claim or on **out-of-sample failure**; a source
that supports only the mechanism leaves the score untouched (the Cycle-35/52 precedent).

**Routes tried.** (1) web search, "crypto perpetual futures funding rate extreme reversal predictor study 2026
arXiv"; (2) web search, "funding rate unconditional reversal Bybit OKX Deribit out-of-sample test evidence";
(3) direct fetch of the two documents the second search surfaced (`raw.githubusercontent.com` README of
`OctopusTakopi/funding-rate-alpha`, and the `aijmr.com` article page for the DOI
10.62127/aijmr.2026.v04i04.1493); (4) direct fetch of `raw.githubusercontent.com` README of
`DavidAyusoPita/funding-carry-analysis`; (5) a direct Wayback Machine capture of the BitMEX blog page
`xbtusd-funding-mean-reversion-strategy`, which returned **HTTP 404** on the live URL and **HTTP 200** via
the archive.

**Verdict — BOUNDED NEGATIVE on both pre-registered limbs, with materially adverse adjacent findings.**
- **Limb (A) is not satisfied.** No published test of the unconditional form on a **named third venue** was
  located. S-390 is Binance — the venue of S-310 and S-320 — and the portion of S-389 read does not identify
  the three venues in its extended sample, so the register does not credit it as a third-venue test.
- **Limb (B) is not satisfied in its strict form.** No source was found that establishes the conditional
  form's edge to be absent. What *was* found is adjacent and adverse, and the register records it as adverse
  rather than as a refutation: (i) **S-390** — an independent 2026 test of the candidate's own trailing-
  percentile construction finds the effect **asymmetric**, with the negative-funding tail working (+0.506 % at
  24 hours, FDR-robust) and "**extreme positive funding does not exhibit a comparable 24-hour effect**";
  (ii) **S-389** — the raw funding level's next-24-hour information coefficient **changes sign between a
  2020–21 reversal regime and a 2023–26 continuation regime**, the current regime's coefficients being
  positive and significant ("No unconditional level signal survives this flip"), the candidate's percentile
  construction reported **null** (z-score 24-hour information coefficient −0.0007, t = −0.3), the **crowd**
  reading contradicted ("open interest builds into and through high-funding settlements … positions are opened
  into the payment, not unwound before it"), and the effect's excess over the round trip "indistinguishable
  from zero (+2.2 bps, t = 0.68)"; (iii) **S-391** — the **venue itself published the same rule in 2017**
  (per-cell success rates 47.62 %, 53.23 %, 45.68 %, 44.44 % over 524 funding periods), which is the
  rediscovery guard; (iv) **S-392** — the cost and decay anchors (13 bps per round trip; 100–135 hours to
  repay; the spread 20–35 % smaller in the second year; "OKX publishes only three months of history").

**Effect.** IDEA-009 is **re-scored 53.0 → 42.0** (mechanism 3 → 2; falsifiability 3 → 2; resilience 2 → 1;
evidence, novelty and data unchanged, each with its reason recorded in the dossier), drops from rank 3 to
**rank 4**, and takes **two new hard-stop conditions** — *costs likely to overwhelm the hypothesized effect*
and *a claim that merely repackages a known idea*. The candidate is **retained, not parked**; **V-008 is
amended** (regime gate; cost-floor arm against the published 8 bps and 13 bps round trips; the percentile and
raw-level constructions as separate arms); **gap G-37 is opened** on the within-contract regime-transfer
question; and the family is written into the known-baseline table as **row 93**.

---

## R-060 — IDEA-014's state-free existence limb: does any published source estimate a deadline-specific RIN price effect? (Cycle 112 pre-registered screen, P-3 — bounded negative on the literature side; the limb remains inconclusive)

**What was screened.** IDEA-014's conditional version is retired with a hard stop, and the candidate's only
live claim is **state-free**: does the compliance deadline move RIN prices at all, and by more than the
round-trip cost? The screen asked whether **published** evidence answers that question, using two route
forms the register had not used on this candidate in nineteen cycles of work: the **agency's own impact
analyses** and the **legislative-audit literature**, together with the academic RIN-price literature.

**The pre-registered question and falsification condition.** The limb's subject is the *deadline's price
effect* — not the obligation's existence, and not the sign of any state. Fixed in advance: an **absence** of
evidence may not be upgraded to a refutation, and a **mechanism** finding may not be read as a **directional**
finding.

**Routes tried.** (1) web search, "RIN prices Renewable Identification Number compliance deadline price effect
study"; (2) web search, "GAO report RIN price analysis EPA small refinery exemption obligated party demand
deadline"; (3) inspection of the search-surfaced agency documents (GAO's report PDF at `gao.gov`, the EPA
technical memorandum and its EPA landing page). The academic route returned S-134 and S-135, which the
register already held.

**Verdict — BOUNDED NEGATIVE on the published-literature side; the limb remains INCONCLUSIVE.** No published
source located in this cycle estimates a pre-/post-deadline RIN price pattern. What the two new agency
documents do establish is materially useful but is **not** the candidate's claim: (i) **the obligation is
priced** — GAO's analysis of EPA's EMTS data finds the smaller obligated party paying or receiving prices
"2.4 percent higher or lower … on average" and "2.9 percent" between refineries, statistically significant,
with D4 at 0.8 %; EPA's final analysis puts the 24 small refineries at "1.1 % (1.2 ¢) more per RIN" over the
daily average and 0.5 % over the 20 largest refiners (D4: 2.7 %), with EPA's own sensitivity band of
"5.3 % less to 7.5 % more"; and (ii) **the deadline's timing structure** is as the candidate assumes —
compliance dates "generally March 31 of the year following the compliance year", obligations satisfiable with
current- or prior-year vintages, and EPA issuing 89 % of its small-refinery-exemption decisions after the
90-day deadline, so refineries "often do not know whether they need RINs until close to, or after, the March
31 compliance deadline", which "may result in either an excess or a shortfall of RINs". The documented price
differentials are **counterparty-size** effects, not **deadline-timing** effects.

**Effect.** **No score change (52.0)**, under the Cycle-35/52 precedent: the sources bear on the mechanism and
the cost floor, not on the conditional directional claim. The **state-free existence limb stays
inconclusive** — neither a positive finding nor a refutation — and the candidate's conditional version stays
retired with its hard stop. Two consequences: **(a) a published friction anchor now exists** (the observable
dispersion of RIN transaction prices around the daily average — 1.1 % central per EPA, 2.4–2.9 % per GAO) and
is the order-of-magnitude floor any deadline effect must exceed; **gap G-38 is opened** for the one input
still missing (a published bid-ask spread or round-trip cost), since the dispersion figures are a
counterparty-size effect and not a cost estimate; and **(b) V-012 is written** as the candidate's first
*state-free* specification, under a **narrow, recorded amendment** to the Cycle-6 rule — G-15 is closed on
the published-literature side, the state-free test needs no signed state by construction, and the two new
entries supply the public transaction-price dataset and the friction anchor that the Cycle-7 search had
recorded as unavailable. **The bar on any state-conditioned specification is re-affirmed in full.**

---

## R-061 — New mechanism family screen: currency-option implied distribution states (implied volatility, skewness, 25-delta risk reversal) as published, decision-time predictors (Cycle 112 pre-registered screen, P-4 — bounded negative)

**What was screened.** A new mechanism family in a venue the register holds no candidate in (**FX options**)
and a state kind its four live candidates do not use (**a benchmark-administrator- or venue-published implied
distribution series**). The pre-registration required five limbs to be checked, in this order: (i) the
baseline inventory must be consulted first and the family distinguished from the **FX-fixing** baseline
(S-074 … S-081) and the **option-implied-crash** baseline (S-223); (ii) the state must be **published before
the decision** (G-13); (iii) the **sign must be unclaimed** (G-12); (iv) the state must not be a public
real-time by-product of the very flow the candidate would trade (the Cycle-52 third test); and (v) the cost
floor must be survivable.

**Routes tried.** (1) web search, "FX option risk reversal implied skew predicts exchange rate returns
evidence published study"; (2) inspection of the search-surfaced documents (S-395, S-396, S-397).

**Verdict — BOUNDED NEGATIVE on the sign limb (G-12 fails).** The family is published in both directions.
**S-395** (Chen & Gwati): using daily options data for six major currency pairs, "the options-implied FX risk
measures — standard deviation, skewness, and kurtosis — consistently explain subsequent currency excess
returns for horizons between one week to twelve months", with quantile-regression results and adjusted R²
values from 13 % to 28 % at one month and 58 % to 84 % for quarterly movements with the term structure.
**S-396** (Ratcliff, 2010, a journal article): the relative price of out-of-the-money puts and calls
"contains information about the market's estimate of the relative probabilities of appreciation or
depreciation of the euro" and "offer significantly better out-of-sample predictions of tomorrow's exchange
rate than either the simple random walk or an interest rate parity model". **S-397** (Kurbanov, 2010) records
the counterweight for the majors: risk reversals "can explain up to 55 % in variation … currency returns" in
sample, but "the outcome of out of sample predictability test … could not beat benchmark Random Walk model
with **RMSE ratio of 1.06**". The remaining limbs were not reached: the sign limb already fails, and the
register does not credit the family with an unclaimed residual.

**Effect.** **No candidate was written.** The family is written into the known-baseline table as **row 94**,
with the published predictor and the published out-of-sample null recorded together so that the family is not
re-presented as a discovery. **No gap was opened** (nothing is left open that a later cycle could resolve), no
score changed, and no specification was written. The screen's route form — a published implied-distribution
state in an unused venue — is recorded as **exhausted for the FX-options instance**, and the register records
that the family is now **covered**, not merely searched.

## Known baselines (reference set, not candidates)

These are recorded so that future sessions do not re-present them as discoveries and so that new
candidates can be compared against them. Listed without scores; the ranking rubric is not applied
to baselines.

| Baseline family | Why it is a baseline (not a discovery) |
| --- | --- |
| Cross-sectional momentum / reversal | Long-established factor family; well understood mechanisms; the interesting questions are about implementation, crowding and decay, not about the existence of the effect. |
| Value and quality-minus-junk | Established premia with well-known explanations (cash-flow duration, risk, mispricing); the operator's own caution about small samples and concentrated winners applies directly. |
| Time-series momentum / trend following | Established; the mechanism (underreaction to persistent trends, limits to arbitrage) is documented, including in this register via S-024 and S-021. |
| Carry / basis / convenience yield | Established risk-premium family; the interesting boundary is financing and funding structure, which is where IDEA-002 and the next cycle's frontier live. |
| Short-term reversal and liquidity provision | Established; the interesting boundary is the *state* in which the provision is profitable, which IDEA-002 and IDEA-004 address. |
| Variance risk premium harvesting (short volatility) | Established baseline; IDEA-003 is a state-conditioned refinement of it, and must always be described that way. |
| Overnight vs intraday decomposition | Established decomposition with an identified clientele mechanism (S-028); useful as a *control* for other candidates, not as a candidate itself. |
| Index inclusion / reconstitution effect | Established effect (S-001 … S-004); IDEA-001 is a flow-scaled, two-leg refinement and must always be described that way. |
| Crowding as a risk factor | Established (S-026); IDEA-004 is a trade specification built on it, not a discovery of crowding itself. |
| Commodity index roll: order-flow impact and the "financialisation" of commodity futures | Established and heavily studied, with contested results (S-035 supporting; S-032, S-033, S-034, S-036, S-037 contesting). IDEA-005 is a state-conditioned refinement of a *documented and contested* family and must always be described that way; the unconditioned trade is logged as R-004. |
| Liquidity provision around predictable large trades ("sunshine trading") | Established mechanism and evidence (S-032, S-034). Any candidate built on supplying liquidity to a scheduled flow must cite and differentiate from this, not present it as new. |
| Hedging pressure and the inventory state in commodity futures | Established (S-039, S-038); these are the rival explanations for any futures-spread move and the state against which a convenience-yield bound must be measured. |
| Retail / same-day-expiry options flow and market-maker gamma | Established as a market-structure fact and as a narrative (S-043, exchange-published); the size of the index-level channel is estimated by the exchange to be de minimis, and the single-name question is open (G-05). P-006 records the parked candidate. |
| FX end-of-day fixing and closing-clock effects | **Literature read in the G-09 closure pass** (S-074 … S-081). The *fix* is a published, documented effect: the temporary, end-of-month-concentrated price impact of passive hedging demand at the 16:00 London fix (S-075), negative serial correlation around the fix (S-076), negative pre/post-fixing autocorrelation (S-077), elevated volatility and movement size around 4pm (S-074), and the W-shaped dollar pattern around global fixings (S-081). The benchmark's post-2015-revision quality is separately documented (S-080). **The fix is a baseline, not a candidate**: the aggregate version of IDEA-006 was rejected as R-007. The only residual not covered by the located literature is the *settlement-side* half of the deadline cluster — the CLS currency-close and pay-in deadlines and the post-US-close window created by T+1 (S-048, S-049) — which is unsearched (**G-11**) and must be described as an expectation until it is checked. Any candidate in this family must name the published fixing/session rules it depends on and must not present the fix's price impact as a discovery. |
| Funding-currency / global-liquidity channel and FX carry crashes | **Literature read in Cycle 2 and closed in Cycle 5** (S-052 … S-073, S-095 … S-097). The *aggregate* funding-constraint-state → carry-return relation is a published, peer-reviewed, positive baseline (S-055); the dollar-funding-liquidity → dollar-forecast relation is published in working-paper form (S-052) and peer-reviewed form (S-053); the crash mechanism, CIP deviations, hedging demand and the global-liquidity driver are documented in the central-bank and peer-reviewed literature (S-056 … S-060, S-069, S-072). **The aggregate versions were rejected as R-006, and the cross-sectional version was rejected as R-008 in Cycle 5.** The whole family — aggregate, cross-sectional, and the publication-timing variant — is now a baseline, not a candidate. |
| Cross-currency basis as a priced cross-section of currency risk premia | **Published and positive** (S-064, a preprint; G10 currencies 1999–2025). "Currencies with higher dollar funding pressure (higher unconditional level of basis) earn higher expected excess returns"; a high-minus-low basis factor is reported to earn 4–4.5% annually and to subsume carry and other factors. Any FX candidate in this family must be a **new mechanism**, not a new state variable (R-008, G-12). |
| Intermediary specialization, safe-asset scarcity and the cross-section of CIP bases | **Published, peer-reviewed** (S-095, *Journal of Finance* 2026; NBER WP 32707; FEDS 2024-061). Three forces are documented — foreign safe asset scarcity, market power and segmentation of banks by market, and concentration of demand — and their pass-through to prices is quantified (of the order of 7–18 basis points per standard deviation). The state is **confidential supervisory data**, so it is not reproducible from public sources; this is the strongest argument that the family needs a new mechanism rather than a new proxy. |
| **Sovereign bond index inclusion and exclusion** | **Published, peer-reviewed — screened in Cycle 6 as frontier family 4.** Inclusion-driven flows into local-currency sovereign debt indices are a published causal object: sovereign yields fall and the domestic currency appreciates (S-137, *Journal of International Economics* 2021), and rebalancing flows move prices, deepen the market and appreciate the currency with placebo tests around the event (S-138, *Journal of Financial Economics* 2019). The institutional scale and the correlated-yield consequences are documented by the IMF (S-139). The equity-reconstitution analogue is IDEA-001's territory. **No candidate in this family is available without a new mechanism.** |
| **Sovereign issuance calendars (US Treasury quarterly refunding)** | **Published — screened in Cycle 6 as frontier family 2.** The refunding announcement is an issuer-run, published, recurring event (S-143), and its price consequences are already the subject of published work: a paper decomposing "debt supply shocks using Treasury auction announcements" (S-142) and a paper on "pre-refunding announcement gains" in Treasuries (S-144). The announcement is also a *news* event whose surprise component requires expectations that are not published, so the residual is not a state-conditioned concession at all. **No candidate in this family.** |
| **Fast-payment rails and the liquidity-demand channel** | **Published mechanism, no candidate structure — screened in Cycle 6 as frontier family 3.** Instant payments raise banks' liquidity demand because they remove the ability to delay and net payment flows (S-140), and 24/7 settlement is modelled as inducing a precautionary demand for central bank balances with upward pressure on the overnight rate (S-141). The family's decision-time object is a rail's *adoption curve*, not a dated obligation: there is no date-bound, price-insensitive flow to capture, and the effect is a level shift in reserve demand that is repriced as it is learned. **No candidate in this family.** |
| **Physical-delivery calendars and the inventory channel in commodity futures** | **Published mechanics — screened in Cycle 6 as frontier family 5.** The inventory-and-convenience-yield channel that makes a delivery calendar a price determinant is already in this ledger (S-038, S-039); the roll mechanics that generate the delivery-month pattern are peer reviewed (S-145, alongside S-032, S-033, S-034, S-036); and the *published* state the family would need — exchange warehouse stocks and delivery calendars — is the same published-inventory channel already in this table. **No candidate in this family.** |
| **Statutory compliance-deadline concessions: the EU ETS instance** | **Published — the template's baseline, and the reason the US RFS instance is a separate candidate.** The EU ETS surrender-month premium is documented: April returns "averaging more than 10% in Aprils" (S-101). Any candidate in this family must be a **different market with a different regulator and a different obligor population**, and must state how its state decides the *sign* of the effect — the published EU ETS instance has no such state. That is exactly what IDEA-014 does in the US Renewable Fuel Standard. |
| **RIN price formation and pass-through (US biofuel credits)** | **Published — the market object, not the candidate.** The RIN price and its pass-through into wholesale and retail fuels are studied in the peer-reviewed literature (S-134, S-135), and the market's quality, liquidity and transaction costs are documented (S-133). A candidate in this family must be about something the published price literature does not state — the *compliance-deadline* effect, and the state that decides its sign. |
| Dealer balance-sheet constraints as a priced currency-funding spread | **Published, peer-reviewed** (S-096): dealers affected by the U.K. leverage-ratio framework charge about 20 basis points per annum more for synthetic dollar funding. Dealer-level and counterparty-identified, so the state is proprietary. |
| Intermediary leverage as a stochastic discount factor for the cross-section of returns | **Published, peer-reviewed** (S-097). The canonical intermediary-state asset-pricing baseline (R² of 77% on size, book-to-market, momentum and bond portfolios). Any funding-state candidate in any asset class must be measured against it. |
| Trader type and the rate of spot–futures convergence in commodity futures | **Published, peer-reviewed** (S-098): "There is strong evidence that speculators (commodity index traders) increase (reduce) the rate of convergence between spot and futures markets." This is a *component* of IDEA-005's differentiator, not the differentiator itself. |
| Commodity index-trader market share and roll-period returns | **Published, peer-reviewed, and largely null** (S-099): the CIT market share (CIT ÷ open interest) is tested directly as a contributor to commodity returns in roll periods, with risk adjustments, and the reported effect is not significant. This is the closest published test of IDEA-005's state variable and is recorded as **contradicting evidence** against it. |
| Payment-system / CLS settlement deadlines as a price determinant | **Published, peer-reviewed** (S-112): after the introduction of CLS "the value of intraday liquidity has increased during the hours of the CLS settlement cycle", with a structural break at the pay-in schedule and an estimated mark-up of "roughly 1.5bp for the CLS settlement cycle"; the mechanism is "large amounts of reserve balances … transferred during specific time slots". Settlement risk in currency markets is also published (S-082, S-071). **This is a baseline**: any candidate whose mechanism is "settlement deadlines are priced" is re-presenting published work. |
| Securities settlement cycle length as a price-impact and market-quality determinant | **Published, peer-reviewed** (S-113, S-114; plus a preprint in S-115): shortening the cycle *improved* liquidity — US securities became more liquid relative to matched London-listed ones after T+3 → T+2, and T+1 "significantly improved liquidity, measured by bid-ask spreads, Amihud illiquidity and price impact measures". The published sign is the opposite of a harvestable concession, and any candidate in this family must explain that sign. |
| The post-US-close FX window (the T+1 funding window) as a market-quality object | **Documented, institutionally** (S-117, S-118, S-119, S-120): the New York Fed's FX Committee names "NY 4-6pm currency liquidity" as the transition's priority FX risk and records the market's expectation of "an uptick in price premium and market impact for trades executed later in the NY trading day"; the market record is "less than 1% of trades in the $7 trillion OTC FX market" in that window, a half-hour pricing-engine outage at 5pm ET, and "bid/ask spreads widen and liquidity shrinks". **This is a baseline and a cost model, not an opportunity**: a concession that exists only in a sub-1%-of-volume window with widening spreads cannot survive a round trip. |
| EU ETS surrender-month premium (compliance-deadline concession) | **Published** (S-101): purchases concentrate in April, April returns average about 10%, the implied premium is of the order of €5 billion, and operator flow predicts future returns. The *state-conditioned* version is unclaimed but currently untestable (P-008): the aggregate balance is published **after** the pre-2023 deadline it would inform (S-121, G-13), so the decision-time rule has three observations and none in a deficit year. Any future candidate in this family must not present the surrender-month premium as a discovery. |
| Exchange-published maintenance-margin schedules as a liquidity and volatility determinant | **Published** (S-122): margin increases cause hedgers and speculators to reduce open positions and produce "funding liquidity spillovers that affect price impact for all contracts", but "I do not find a significant effect of margin changes on the futures price level", while "realized variance increases by 50% on average on the day of a margin increase". The margin schedule is published by the venue and its setting rule is published too ("2.5 times the daily standard deviation of returns"). **This is a baseline**: the family is studied, and its published effect is on liquidity and volatility, not on the price level. |
| ETF primary-market arbitrage and AP inventory as a driver of ETF mispricing | **Published, institutionally** (S-123): with a regulatory dataset covering the primary and secondary market transactions of 128 ETFs from 2018 to 2022, the FCA finds "a connection between mispricing … and AP's inventory", with "excess AP inventory … widen[ing] discounts". **This is a baseline**, and its dataset is proprietary — the same AP-level data constraint that blocks IDEA-010. |
| Spot-crypto-ETF flow as a channel into the underlying market | **Published, institutionally and as a preprint** (S-124, S-125): the Fed finds crypto ETPs "track NAV less-closely than other ETPs and ETFs" and that the premium reflects segmentation and arbitrage difficulty; the preprint finds that the ETF channels large, uninformed demand into the spot market, "making it busier but shallower", with volume rising "only within U.S. trading hours, when ETF and Authorized Participant activity is concentrated". **This is a baseline**: the creation-flow channel is published, with a *liquidity-deterioration* sign and a published intraday timing pattern. |
| **The disappearing index effect (added in Cycle 13)** | **Published, peer-reviewed** (S-146, *Journal of Finance* 2025). The S&P 500 inclusion effect has collapsed: additions below 1% over the past decade (7.4% in the 1990s), deletions 0.1% between 2010 and 2020, an average inclusion effect of −3 basis points in 2020, and "no apparent relationship between net purchases [by index trackers] and the index inclusion effect, for either additions or deletions". **Any future candidate in the index-reconstitution family must start from this paper and must explain what is left**; the magnitudes are version-dependent between the working-paper and journal versions, so the version must be cited. |
| **Anticipatory liquidity provision at reconstitution (added in Cycle 13)** | **Published as a model and as a documented structure** (S-147, working paper; S-171, preprint; S-151, peer-reviewed). Speculators pre-position, which shifts price pressure away from the effective date; short interest rises on implementation dates and falls afterwards; "apparent front-running reflects competitive liquidity provision and benefits index investors"; and the counterparty to passive demand is the issuing firm. **This is a baseline**: a candidate that trades the pre-announcement anticipation or the post-implementation unwind of reconstitution is presenting published work. |
| **Benchmark-specific passive-asset estimation from reconstitution-day volume (added in Cycle 13)** | **Published, peer-reviewed** (S-148, *Journal of Financial Economics* 2024): the passive ownership share was 33.3% in 2021, roughly double the index-fund-only figure, and the AUM tracking each of the Russell 1000, Russell 2000, S&P 500, S&P MidCap 400 and Nasdaq 100 is inferred from reconstitution-day closing volume for 2000–2021. **This is a method note, not a candidate**: it closes G-02/G-07 as construction questions. |
| **Single-name variance risk premia are frequently zero or positive (added in Cycle 13)** | **Published, peer-reviewed** (S-153, Driessen, Maenhout & Vilkov, *JF* 2009; S-154, Carr & Wu, *RFS* 2009): individual equity options do not embed a negative variance risk premium, single-stock variance swap returns are insignificant, and the negative premium is a large-index phenomenon with correlation risk doing the pricing work. **This forecloses the single-name extension of IDEA-003**; the aggregate version is a baseline (S-014, S-093, S-094) and IDEA-003 must always be described as a state-conditioned refinement of it. **Cycle-107 line-verification note (the row title stands, the wording is sharpened):** S-153's published article body was obtained directly and confirms every limb in substance — the individual variance risk premium is "not significantly negative for almost all index components" (the individual test table records "H0: RV − MFIV = 0 **not rejected 98**" of 127 stocks, with 22 rejecting a non-positive null and 7 rejecting a non-negative one), the cross-sectional individual variance risk premium is "**insignificant**" with a **positive** point estimate (0.077, t = 1.74) set against the negative index premium, and "almost all of the first-step betas are statistically insignificant". S-154's 2004 working-paper body was also obtained and **confirms** the large-index limb ("The largest t-statistics come from the S&P 500 and S&P 100 indexes … which are strongly significant"), while showing that the individual-stock premia are **negative in point estimate but insignificant for 32 of 35 names** — so "frequently zero" is a fair paraphrase of "frequently statistically indistinguishable from zero", while "**or positive**" is a property of one *point estimate on one factor* (S-153's individual variance risk factor), **not** of the individual-stock premia generally. S-154's separate claim that individual stocks "do not load on a variance risk premium factor" was **contradicted by that paper's own factor analysis and struck in the ledger**; it does not reopen this row, because the paper's own message is that what is priced is a **common market variance risk factor**, i.e. a market-level rather than a single-name object. **Cycle-108 note (the individual-stock magnitudes are the *working paper's*; the published version's front matter is now line-verified and its body is closed):** the published 2009 *RFS* body could not be obtained over **twenty-one named routes** (Oxford Academic on four path forms, the Silverchair CDN, JSTOR on two path forms, the author-posted SSRN copy of the published version — indexed as "Not Available for Download", the Internet Archive over five legacy OUP path forms and its full-text index, Unpaywall, OpenAlex, Semantic Scholar, Crossref, the two authors' pages and the NYU Faculty Digital Archive), so at that point **every individual-stock figure quoted in this row and in S-154 was the May 2004 working paper's** and had to be attributed as such — **this attribution is retired by the Cycle-109 rewrite below.** **Cycle-109 REWRITE (the published body is obtained; the row is re-based on the published version, and the working-paper figures are retired):** the **published 2009 *RFS* body was obtained** on the **course-host route form** — a Duke University course directory copy, `…/courses/201/spr12/DOWNLOADS/WorkingPapers_Now_Published/cw_rfs_09.pdf`, **HTTP 200, 213,058 bytes, application/pdf, 31 pages, 98,823 characters** — and it is the **publisher's typeset file** (the PDF's own `/Title` is `hhn038.dvi`, its `/ModDate` is 2009-02-10, and the text layer carries the running head "**The Review of Financial Studies / v 22 n 3 2009**" on 15 pages, ending at page **1341**), and it is the **published** version and not the working paper ("VarianceSwap15.tex" **0 hits**, "This version: May 27, 2004" **0 hits**, "Published by Oxford University Press on behalf of The Society for Financial Studies" and "Advance Access publication April 10, 2008" present, "(JEL G10, G12, G13)" present, and the published acknowledgements thanking "**Yacine A ¨ıt-Sahalia (the editor), an anonymous referee**" — the working paper thanks neither). The **published** results are now line-verified: the index premium is "**strongly negative for the S&P 500 and 100 indexes and for the Dow Jones Industrial Average**", and the published Table 3 records the S&P 500 mean premium at **−2.74 in the table's own units, (RV − SW) × 100** — about **−$2.74 per $100 of notional** on the register's reading of that unit — (log excess return **−66 %**; annualised Sharpe ratios on shorting 30-day variance swaps **0.98, 0.85 and 0.87** for SPX, OEX and DJX), while among the 35 individual stocks "**only seven** generate variance risk premiums that are **significantly negative** at the 95 % confidence level" and "**for 23 out of 35** individual stocks, the mean **log** variance risk premiums are significantly negative at the 95 % confidence level". The published Table 3 also shows **twelve of the 35 individual stocks with a positive mean premium** (INTC +2.49, UNPH +6.59, BRCD +9.36, CIEN +5.22, EMC +3.87, MOT +2.19, TXN +1.84, NOK +1.51, BRCM +0.66, TRV +0.35, HWP +0.25, LU +0.18) and **none of those twelve is significant** (largest absolute t-statistic = 1.34, INTC), so this row's "**or positive**" is supported for *point estimates* and is **not** supported for *significant* premia, while "frequently statistically indistinguishable from zero" and "the negative premium is a large-index phenomenon" are both **confirmed on the published evidence**. The published version's own factor analysis **also contradicts the entry's third limb** and now supplies the register's evidence for it: the variance beta βV_j = Cov(ln RV_j, ln RV_SPX)/Var(ln RV_SPX) and the cross-sectional regression "**LRP_j = 0.0061 − 0.3283 βV_j + e, R² = 18.4 %**", with "the slope estimate is **negative** and statistically **significant**" (t = −2.96) — so individual stocks **do** load on a common market variance risk factor, and the published version's own bottom line is that "**classic risk factors cannot fully account for the negative variance risk premiums on the stock indexes**". **The published version's magnitudes differ from the May 2004 working paper's** (working paper: "insignificant for all but three of the 35" and "significantly negative for 21 of the 35", with a **positive** variance-beta slope on β²_V; published: 7 of 35 and 23 of 35, with a **negative** slope on β_V; and the published option-data window is "**starts in January 1996 and ends in February 2003**"), so the **working-paper figures are retired from this row and from S-154** and the published version's figures are used from here on. **Under the Cycle-108 pre-registration, outcome (ii) applies — the row is rewritten, the single-name screen is re-examined, and IDEA-003's G-05 closure is re-opened for that limb only** — and the re-examination, recorded in the IDEA-003 dossier's Cycle-109 block, **re-closes** the closure on the published evidence, so the closure **stands and is strengthened**. |
| **Factor-profitability decay and the crowding/alpha relation (added in Cycle 13)** | **Published, peer-reviewed for the decay** (S-156, *JFQA* 2023: the momentum factor's returns fell from 0.92% to 0.16% per month after mid-2002, with an identified mechanism) and **preprint for the crowding result** (S-172: crowding-based factor selection fails out-of-sample; crowding predicts tail risk rather than average returns). **This is a baseline for any crowding-conditioned candidate**: the base effect must be shown to survive, and the preprint's title has already changed between versions, so the version must be cited. **Cycle-107 attribution correction (the magnitudes are the working paper's, not the published article's):** the 0.92% → 0.16% pair (and the companion 0.61% → 0.14% for factor momentum) is confirmed **verbatim in the earlier NBER Working Paper 28624 revisions (rev1/rev2, 2021)** and is **absent from the published *JFQA* article** — the string "0.92" returns zero hits there. The published article's own magnitudes are **53.4 basis points per month** (the profitability of momentum-type factors after the Morningstar reform), **23.9 basis points** (the most conservative estimate of the incremental decline against other factors), **9 and 54 basis points** (non-U.S. versus additional U.S. decline), **−36 to −40 basis points** (the US × MomType × Post2002 triple interaction), and **0.77% a month** in the international triple-difference. The **substantive baseline claim is unchanged and is now line-verified in the published version itself**: a large, U.S.-specific, mechanistically explained decline in momentum-related factor profitability following the June 2002 Morningstar rating reform. **Any future candidate citing the decay must cite the exact version it quotes.** |
| **Crowding inferred from return correlations — "comomentum" (added in Cycle 13)** | **Published, peer-reviewed** (S-157, Lou & Polk, *RFS* 2021): arbitrage-activity intensity is inferable from return correlations, so it is computable from public prices at decision time. **This is a baseline and the object of the new gap G-18**: it is a *different construct* from the holdings-based position-concentration measure (S-026), and the two have never been validated against each other. |
