| **Perpetual-futures funding, drawdown mean reversion and the unwind channel (added in Cycle 13)** | **Published** (S-158, working paper: perpetual drawdowns quickly mean-revert while quarterly drawdowns persist, and a higher funding rate attenuates price sensitivity to demand shocks; S-159, *Journal of Empirical Finance* 2026: funding rates have incremental forecasting power for spot volatility because they capture imbalanced leveraged positions vulnerable to subsequent unwinding). **This is a baseline**: IDEA-009's mechanism and mean-reversion are published, and its residual is a dated, signed, exit-specified rule keyed to the funding *level*. |
| **Treasury General Account flows and repo-market stress (added in Cycle 13)** | **Published** (S-160, IMF working paper: TGA flows can create large swings in the repo spread; S-161, S-162, Federal Reserve FEDS Notes: repo-rate sensitivity to Treasury issuance through the SOMA, the ON RRP facility and the TGA, with the public **TGCR − IORB** spread as the decision-time stress measure). **This is a baseline and a cost model**: the funding-state family is published, and what remains open in G-01 is specifically a *dealer-level* funding-value-adjustment series (construct: S-155). |
| **California cap-and-trade price collars, reserve sales and surrender obligations (added in Cycle 13)** | **Published and institutionally documented** (S-163, Bushnell on the price-collar design and the reserve's never having been used; S-164, CARB's Reserve Sale Notice and Annual APCR Notice, including the codified 60%-of-tier trigger, the published tier prices and quantities, the ~30-day notices and the Appendix C schedule; S-174, 17 CCR §§ 95856/95857/95913 — the 1 November, 5:00 p.m. Pacific surrender deadline and the four-times untimely-surrender penalty; S-165, CRS R48314 on prices and compliance; S-168, *AER* 2019 on the collar's design; S-173, CBO 2010 on reserve mechanics). **This is a baseline and a template**: the state is published, the obligation is punitive, and yet the price-insensitive demand is satisfied in advance and the reserve never opens — which is why no candidate emerged from the screen and why the register's own cap-and-trade candidates must state how their state makes the deadline demand *tight*. |
| **Accelerated share repurchases (added in Cycle 13)** | **Published, peer-reviewed** (S-166, **Bargeron, Kulchania & Thomas**, *JFE* 101(1): 69–89, 2011 — the entry previously named Erickson, Fang & Howe, which was corrected in Cycle 102: the DOI, journal, volume, issue and pages belong to Bargeron, Kulchania & Thomas, and the mechanism claims are confirmed against the authors' own deposited abstract; S-167, Guéant, Pu & Royer, *IJTAF* 2015: the ASR is an Asian option with Bermudan exercise dates and the intermediary's problem is optimal execution with market impact). **This is a baseline**: the mechanism, the pricing and the announcement effects are published. **Cycle-108 narrowing (the baseline sentence above is too strong as written and is corrected here):** **the *contract architecture* is public, but the *realized discount* is not.** The executed confirmation was a **public exhibit** — Exhibit 99.1 to the Form 8-K — and the announcement filing body (S-386) itself disclosed the initial price ("The initial price paid per common share was **$25.97**") and the pricing principle in the announcement press release (S-387: the shares "are expected to be purchased from Goldman Sachs **based on the current market price at the time of the contract signing**"). But the quantity that carries the economics is **terminal**: the initial price *is* the market price at signing (so there is no initial discount to observe), and the economic quantity is a **"future contingent purchase price adjustment expected to be settled at the end of the purchase period in the first or second quarter of 2006, based on the volume weighted average share price during the actual repurchase period"** (S-386) — which the issuer publicly expected "to be immaterial" at the announcement date. The **covering window is bounded ex ante by published law** (17 CFR 240.10b-18: ADTV over the four calendar weeks preceding the purchase week, a **25 percent** daily cap, one broker or dealer, price no higher than the highest independent bid — S-388) and by the contract's own four-month cap (S-384), so the *bound* is decision-time and public, while the *realized* path is not. The screen that establishes this is logged as **R-056**. **Cycle-107 note — the baseline now rests on the contract itself (new primary source S-384):** the actual ASR agreement that Bargeron, Kulchania & Thomas cite in their Appendix A was obtained directly from EDGAR — **FirstMerit Corporation / Goldman, Sachs & Co., "Accelerated Share Repurchase Master Confirmation" and "Supplemental Confirmation for Fully Uncollateralised Transactions", Exhibit 99.1 to FirstMerit's Form 8-K, 19 January 2006, accession 0000950152-06-000339** (HTTP 200, 149,031 bytes; text layer 73,839 characters) — and it line-verifies the mechanics in a **primary instrument**: immediate delivery of the shares against cash at the trade date ("Counterparty agreed to purchase a number of Shares equal to the Number of Shares from GS&Co. on the Trade Date at the Forward Price per Share"; Forward Price USD 25.97; 2,500,000 Shares), the **covering/accumulation window** ("Valuation Date: A day not exceeding **4 months** after the Trade Date", with "Daily Reference Shares: For each day, the applicable maximum amount of Shares permitted under Rule 10b-18(b)(4)"), the **discount/compensation settlement** (a "Counterparty Additional Payment Amount" on each ex-dividend date during the Valuation Period at "USD-LIBOR-BBA", with the settlement price "reduced by the per Share amount of the underwriting discount and/or commissions"), and the **downside termination price** (USD 12.98 per share) with Reserved Shares of 900,000. The **paper's own body text remains unverified** — the authors' institutional repository, the SSRN copy and the dissertation route all failed (HTTP 403 / 403 / 404) — so the "intermediary borrows the shares" sentence stays index-rendered in S-166, and the contract is the primary instrument that corroborates it. |
| **Crypto token unlock schedules and their price effects (added in Cycle 13)** | **Published by an interested party, not peer-reviewed** (S-170: a market maker's study of more than 16,000 unlock events reports roughly 90% followed by negative price pressure, with the decline typically starting about 30 days before the unlock and stabilising within about two weeks after, and team unlocks the most disruptive). **This is a baseline and a rediscovery guard**: the schedule is published and the sign and window are already stated publicly, and there is no published state that says whether recipients will sell. |
| **The closing-auction passive execution convention (added in Cycle 13)** | **Documented institutionally and published** (S-149, the index manager's own statement that index funds and ETFs are structurally incentivised to execute in the closing auction because NAVs are struck on closing prices, with Market-on-Close orders dominant; S-150, closing-auction volume at roughly 4–10% of ADV, lower price impact than in the continuous market, and a significant association with passive creation/redemption activity; S-148, passive rebalancing executed at the closing price on reconstitution day). **This is a baseline and a cost note**: the convention is settled, so any candidate that needs a *different* execution convention must justify why the passive does not trade at the close. |
| **State-conditioned variance risk premium harvesting (added in Cycle 30)** | **Published, peer-reviewed** (S-180, Konstantinidi & Skiadopoulos, *Journal of Banking & Finance* 2016): the variance risk premium's time variation is predictable from decision-time state variables, and volatility-trading strategies that condition on those states **outperform buy-and-hold and the naive short-volatility strategy even after transaction costs**. **This is a baseline**: IDEA-003 must always be described as an incremental variant of published conditional-VRP work whose residual is the *state construction* and the *sign* claim, never as a discovery of state-conditioned harvesting. |
| **The variance risk premium as a crash-conditional tail premium, and the premium's measurement convention (added in Cycle 30)** | **Published as a preprint, author-reported** (S-181): the premium is characterised as "a downside, crash-conditional risk premium, compensation for a systematic tail rather than harvestable carry", with the regime-average premium positive in every crisis and widening in high-stress and high-credit-spread states, and an "irreducible left tail" even voltargeted and overlaid. **This is a baseline and a convention warning**: that paper measures the premium as implied minus *subsequently realized* variance, while the candidate's claim is about the *expected* premium, and the two can move in opposite directions in the same state. Any future candidate or specification that conditions on a measured premium must name its convention ex ante. |
| **10b5-1 plans: the rule, the disclosure regime and the plan-timing price effects (added in Cycle 30)** | **Published law plus a regulator's own literature review** (S-182, the SEC's 2022 final rule, 87 FR 80362 — Item 408(a) quarterly disclosure, the mandatory cooling-off period, the Form 4 plan checkbox and the ban on overlapping plans; S-183, the SEC's 2022 proposed release, 87 FR 8686 — the agency's account of the academic evidence that plan **adoption** around earnings announcements carries abnormal returns of roughly −2% to −3% while sales after the announcement do not). **This is a baseline and a rediscovery guard for IDEA-015**: the rule and the state are published law, and the nearest published price effect is attached to the adoption window, not to the execution of a disclosed schedule. |
| **Statutory payment-date Treasury cash drains in the funding market (added in Cycle 30)** | **Published** (S-160, S-161, S-162 — the TGA → repo-spread channel, with the public **TGCR − IORB** spread as the decision-time stress measure). **This is a baseline and a cost model**; the register's family screen in Cycle 30 produced the bounded negative **R-012** on the cross-asset extension, because the payment dates are known by statute and no published state isolates the payment due at the decision date. |
| **The expected variance risk premium, its conventions, and its sign in bad states (added in Cycle 32)** | **Published, peer-reviewed, and now the register's binding constraint on IDEA-003** (S-190, *Journal of Econometrics* 2020; S-191, *Annual Review of Financial Economics* 2018; S-192, *Review of Finance* 2023; S-193, *Management Science* 2021). The expected premium (risk-neutral minus physical conditional expectation) is **positive**, and it is **higher** in bad states: "the continuous component of the VRP … is systematically positive and is higher in periods of market turmoil" (S-190); "the variance risk premium is positive … high variance risk premiums coincide with the left tail of the consumption growth distribution shifting down" (S-192). The *negative* readings of the premium in stress belong to the **naive ex-post** estimator, which "systematically becomes negative, rather than largely positive, in periods of market distress" because it ignores the latent conditional structure (S-190). The only published sign-flip in a variance-premium quantity in distress is in the **term** premium (the term structure turns upward-sloping, S-193). **This is a baseline and a rediscovery guard:** any future candidate that claims an expected-premium sign flip in a crash state must explain S-190 and S-192, and any specification must name its convention ex ante. |
| **Authorized-participant regulatory capital ratios as the operative ETF-arbitrage state (added in Cycle 32)** | **Published, peer-reviewed** (S-194, *Journal of Banking & Finance* 2025). APs' **regulatory capital ratios, disclosed in SEC regulatory filings**, govern ETF arbitrage intensity and ETF premia; the effect is material (0.69pp vs 0.39pp decline in arbitrage intensity between 25th- and 75th-percentile APs in the March 2020 dash-for-cash) and other AP characteristics do not matter. The mechanism is S-195 (working paper, **proprietary datasets**) and the published rivals are S-196 (fractional baskets), S-197 (funding segmentation) and S-199 (client-sourced liquidity discounts). **This is a baseline and a state note:** the AP capital ratio is a *public* state that is already validated against the ETF-premium relation, so IDEA-010 must be described as a trade specification and cost-floor test on a published relation, never as a discovery of the relation. |
| **The RFS volume requirement is calibrated to the agency's own supply projection, and projected shortfalls are waived rather than bought (added in Cycle 32)** | **Primary regulator material** (S-201, the Set 2 final rule, 91 FR 16388; S-202, 40 CFR 80.1405; S-203, the agency's 2026–2027 standards summary). The annual volume requirement is set so that the agency's **projected** supply meets it, so the projected net obligated position is near zero by construction; where the projection fell short (2025 cellulosic: 1.21 billion gallons projected against a 1.38 billion RIN requirement, "a shortfall of approximately 13 percent") the agency's response was the **waiver** of the volume requirement, not a market purchase. **This is a baseline and a state note:** the projected net obligated position is not a sign-determining state, and the realised one is published only after the deadline. Any future candidate in the RFS family must name a different pre-deadline state. |
| **Debt tender offers and liability-management exercises: the price-insensitive flow at a published deadline (added in Cycle 32)** | **Published, peer-reviewed, plus the rule itself** (S-204, Mann & Powers, *Journal of Banking & Finance* 2006: the tender price averages a 4.75% premium to the pre-tender market price and 82.3% of the issue tenders; S-205, Levy & Shalev, *Journal of Accounting and Economics* 2016: the tender is the price-insensitive, dated repurchase route; S-206, Daniels, Díaz & Ramírez, *Journal of Corporate Finance* 2012: renegotiations cluster before a published clawback deadline; S-207, 17 CFR 240.14e-1, and S-208/S-209 for the offer mechanics). **This is a baseline and a bounded-negative family screen (Cycle 32):** the deadline-clustered, price-insensitive flow is a documented institutional pattern, and the one tradeable implication — buying a bond below a published offer price and tendering it — is a mechanical, published arbitrage rather than an edge, with the residual (the post-deadline capital-structure jump) being a credit event rather than a mechanical reversion. |
| **The EU CBAM certificate purchase obligation (added in Cycle 35)** | **Primary regulator material** (S-227, the Commission's published certificate prices — the weighted average of EUA auction clearing prices, quarterly for 2026 and weekly from 2027; S-228, the definitive-regime guidance, the Q&A and the certificate factsheet — the 50 %-of-embedded-emissions quarter-end holding requirement from 2027, the 30 September annual surrender, sales from 1 February 2027 and the deadline's one revision from 31 May; S-229, the free-allocation phase-out — 97.5 % free allocation in 2026 falling to 0 % in 2034, so the obligation share is 2.5 % in 2026 and 100 % only from 2034; S-230, the loss of authorised-declarant status for non-compliance). **This is a baseline and a bounded-negative family screen (Cycle 35, R-016):** the obligation is a genuine, punitive, dated purchase obligation, but the certificate price is a published deterministic formula off EUA auction prices and the certificate is neither tradable by a third party nor usable for anything but the obligation, so no concession is harvestable; the obligation is near zero by construction in the near term; the obligor is the beneficiary; and CBAM's effect on EU carbon prices is itself published. **Dated re-screen condition:** re-screen when the obligation share is materially larger (the 2029–2030 step to 48.5 % of embedded emissions) **and** the weekly pricing regime has run long enough for authorised declarants' pre-deadline **hedging behaviour in the EUA market** to be observable. |
| **Option-implied crash measures as return predictors and crash forecasters (added in Cycle 35)** | **Published, peer-reviewed** (S-223, Chen & Song, *Journal of Futures Markets* 2025: a hybrid option-implied-variance/realised-moment crash measure with significant predictive power for the expected market return, a markedly different marginal effect in crisis and non-crisis periods, and superior out-of-sample crash-event forecasting when combined with variance risk), alongside the option-implied crash-index and crash-risk-pricing literature. **This is a baseline and the reason IDEA-003 was parked (P-012, G-22):** the published work forecasts crash events, predicts the expected market return and prices crash risk — it does not condition a variance harvest on a crash state, and the measure it uses is not a pure option-surface construct. Any future candidate that layers a crash state on a variance harvest must engage S-223 explicitly. |
| **Crowding as a published first-order conditioning variable (added in Cycle 35)** | **Published, peer-reviewed** (S-222, Chincarini, Lazo-Paz & Moneta, *Journal of Banking & Finance* 2025: crowding measured on Days-ADV over 13F holdings 1980–2021 is positively related to subsequent abnormal returns on well-known anomalies, with anomaly risk-adjusted returns "primarily generated by most (least) crowded stocks for the long-leg (short-leg) portfolio", surviving publication-date controls, and with a published crash-risk channel in which crowding "adds new considerations to limits to arbitrage"), on top of S-026's holdings-based construction and S-157's comomentum. **This is a baseline and a rediscovery guard for IDEA-004:** crowding is no longer a discovery, the published relation is *monotone* where the candidate claims a two-sided gate, and the published crowd is all 13F institutional investors (directional positioning) rather than the candidate's convergence-arbitrage capital — which is why G-04 was restated and G-28 opened. |
| **Ex-dividend date mechanics as a dated, mechanical supply event (added in Cycle 37)** | **A baseline and a rediscovery guard (bounded negative R-017, Cycle-36 screen written for real in Cycle 37).** The ex-dividend price adjustment is an accounting identity executed on a fully published date by the party obliged to execute it; there is no unpriced residual supply and no published state that makes an ex-date tight or loose. Any candidate built on the ex-date would be repackaging arithmetic. |
| **The Rule 201 short-sale price-test circuit breaker (added in Cycle 37)** | **Published, peer-reviewed — a baseline and a bounded-negative family screen (R-018).** The effect a candidate would claim is already published and quantified: comparing stocks on either side of the −10 % threshold in the same hour, the restriction leads to 8 % lower short-sale volume and 35 bps higher daily returns, and those price effects "do not reverse after the restriction is lifted" (S-236). The constraint is also defeasible: after a trigger, short sellers migrate to the options market (put open interest +61 %, put volume +87 %; S-237) at low cost (S-238). The rule's mechanics are published law (S-235). **Any future candidate in this family must start from S-236 and must explain what is left.** **Cycle-107 note — S-238's body is now an evidenced closure, not an open task:** the article's bibliographic record and its abstract-level findings ("relatively low cost"; "a discount of a synthetic short position to the prevailing stock price when Rule 201 is triggered of about 3%") remain line-verified through the Cycle-102 RePEc route, but the **body is not publicly available** — OpenAlex, Unpaywall and Semantic Scholar each report the article closed with **no repository copy**, and the two named author routes (the AMF-hosted conference version and the author's Concordia institutional repository) return HTTP 404. The article's **introduction-level** prior-literature summary (that short-sale bans are "ineffective, or … lead to lower market quality, higher bid-ask spreads [and] higher volatility") is therefore **index-rendered and must continue to be presented as such**; it is not a quotation the register may assert. The R-018 verdict and this row's substance are unchanged. |
| **Authorised-participant naming in ETF statutory filings: a crypto- and commodity-trust practice (added in Cycle 40)** | **Primary statutory filings (S-239).** ETF prospectuses that name their authorised participants exist and were read directly: the iShares Bitcoin Trust ETF's Form 424B3 prospectuses name their APs verbatim (five APs in the 9 January 2024 prospectus, nine in April 2024, twelve in August 2024; twelve cash and four in-kind as of 31 July 2025), and the same practice appears in the iShares Ethereum and Staked Ethereum trusts, the Bitcoin Premium Income trust, the WisdomTree, Invesco Galaxy, 21Shares, Morgan Stanley, T. Rowe Price Active Crypto and Ark 21Shares digital-asset trusts, and — in older form — in the iShares commodity trusts, whose prospectuses name market makers and clearing counterparties. **This is a baseline and a state note:** the naming practice is real, dated and verifiable, and it is **confined to the commodity/crypto trust class**; a full-document search found **no corporate-bond ETF naming its APs**. Any candidate that needs an ETF → authorised-participant mapping must state which route it uses, and must not assume that a corporate-bond ETF discloses one. |
| **The short-interest branch of the crowding literature (added in Cycle 40)** | **Published, peer-reviewed (plus one working paper)** — S-241 (Mitchell, Pulvino & Stafford, *Journal of Finance* 2004: short interest is the published measure of merger-arb short selling, with its price-pressure effect), S-242 (Hanson & Sunderam, *Review of Financial Studies* 2014: short interest as a published crowding measure of arbitrage capital, whose limits bind when crowding is high) and S-243 (Chan & Tan, SSRN 2020, working paper: daily short interest infers near-real-time crowding of quantitative long-short strategies). **This is a baseline and a rediscovery guard for IDEA-004:** the register's published-neighbour inventory for the convergence-crowd candidate was incomplete without this branch, and the candidate may not present a short-interest-based convergence-activity measure as a discovery. These measures identify **style** arbitrage capital, not the pair/merger-spread convergence crowd, and their published relation is monotone where the candidate claims a two-sided gate. |
| **Eligibility switches: regulatory and provider changes in *who may hold* (added in Cycle 40)** | **Primary regulator material plus a bounded-negative family screen (R-020).** The instances are the SEC's accredited-investor and qualified-institutional-buyer definition amendment (S-244: Release 33-10824, 85 FR 64234, effective 8 December 2020; the $1 million net-worth, $200,000 income and $100 million QIB thresholds are published) and index-provider eligibility and float screens. **This is a baseline and a bounded-negative family screen (Cycle 40, R-020):** the switch is published in advance so any flow is anticipated (and the nearest published analogue finds the effect collapsed to approximately zero and shifted away from the effective date — S-146, S-147); the constrained flow is the obligor's own, so there is no third-party concession to harvest (S-151); each holder's distance to its own limit is private, so the state would be a construction; and the effective sample is the number of switches, not securities × dates. **Dated re-screen condition:** re-screen only if a change arrives with a published list of affected securities *and* a published distance-to-limit measure for each affected holder. |
| **Closing-price formation: randomised closes and real-time closing-auction imbalance publication (added in Cycle 51)** | **Published, peer-reviewed plus primary rule text, and a bounded-negative family screen (R-031, Cycle 51) — the register's first screen of a *price-formation* family.** The published record: the LSE's call-market opening and closing auctions and their price-discovery and trading-cost properties (S-247), the market-quality effects of introducing the LSE closing call auction (S-248), the Korea Exchange's conditional random end — a randomised end-of-auction mechanism reported to improve price discovery and stabilise the close, with the benefit attenuating under high-volatility conditions (S-249), and the closing auction's *lower* price impact relative to the continuous market for all stocks except Nasdaq micro-caps (S-150, with S-149 on the passive-incentive mechanism). The primary rule text: NYSE's Closing Auction Imbalance Information (Continuous Book Clearing Price, Imbalance Reference Price, paired/unpaired quantity) is disseminated on the exchange's proprietary feed from ten minutes before the close and updated at least every second, and the exchange's stated purpose for publishing it is to attract "offsetting orders" and "additional contra-side interest, thereby decreasing volatility" (S-251, 89 FR 19370–19374, Release No. 34-99719; approval order 34-100327), with NYSE's 2022 amendment (87 FR 56099) making the DMM's price selection "more transparent and deterministic" — so the US close is not randomised. The settlement channel does not propagate: the Eurex FTSE 100 Index Future settles on the EDSP intraday auction and on a 17:30 CET volume-weighted average, both determined after the cash close is known (S-250). **This is a baseline and a rediscovery guard:** a randomised close is a symmetric draw with no sign-determining state, a single-price auction cannot express the compensation for the draw in the price, and the exchange designs the imbalance publication to attract offsetting interest. Any candidate in this family must state which venue, which instrument settles on the randomised close, and what published state determines the sign. |
| **Venue-imposed trading halts, price bands and market-wide circuit breakers (added in Cycle 52)** | **Published, peer-reviewed plus primary rule text, and a bounded-negative family screen (R-032, Cycle 52) — the register's first screen of a *market-interruption* family.** The published record on the market-wide breaker: state-conditioned behaviour is claimed and sized — volatility rises drastically as the market approaches the trigger, returns take on increasing negative skewness, expected returns and volume rise as the trigger approaches, sized at 17.5 bps of normalised daily volatility per 1 % closer and roughly double inside 2 % (S-256); the post-halt state of the world is measured — returns stabilise, trading costs fall, selling pressure resolves and prices become more informative after the resumption (S-258); the individual-stock variant is equally studied — LULD "curbs short term volatility without delaying the price discovery process", with a magnet effect near the band and reduced HFT activity after the halt (S-262) — and the family has a thirty-year survey literature with its own methodological disputes and its reported "lack of statistical power" (S-257). The primary rule text: the US market-wide thresholds of 7 % / 13 % / 20 % of the previous S&P 500 close and the 15-minute level-1 halt (S-266), the Limit Up–Limit Down price bands, the 15-second Limit State, the five-minute Trading Pause and the reopening auction (S-260, S-259), and the EU regime's "Guidelines on calibration of circuit breakers **and publication of trading halts**" with annual reporting of thresholds, durations and resumption mechanics to the competent authority and on to ESMA (S-261, S-263, S-264). **This is a baseline and a rediscovery guard:** the trigger state is public and real-time — the SIPs "republish the current price bands every 30 seconds" (S-260) — so there is nothing to discover; a market-wide trigger is a state common to every security, making the trade beta rather than an edge; an individual-stock halt is triggered by that stock's own news, so the identification is inseparable from the information event (the R-018 objection); the concession is competed away inside the reopening auction, in which the venue's own Designated Sponsors are obliged to quote (S-263); and the cost floor is hostile — the tradeable window is seconds wide, in the least liquid names (84 % of halted ETPs small-cap, only 8 S&P 500 constituents halted, on the SEC's own statistics for 24 August 2015 — S-259) and with realised volatility 194 % higher annualised in the first minute after a level-1 halt and wider spreads for about five minutes (S-266). **Dated re-screen condition:** re-open only if (i) a venue introduces an in-force, pre-published, *persistent* **reopening-auction rule difference** (a price collar or matching rule) with published evidence that it produces persistent dispersion in reopening prices rather than a one-day observation — the SEC's own staff note on NYSE Arca's 24 August 2015 collars is the narrow residual this family leaves and is recorded as such, not as a candidate (S-259); or (ii) an exchange publishes a **mandatory participation requirement in its reopening auction for a non-market-maker class** (S-263); or (iii) the US thresholds or LULD parameters change materially, or the EU publication-of-trading-halts requirement is withdrawn (S-260, S-261, S-266). |
| **Tax-driven year-end flows: capital-loss harvesting and the paired window dressing (added in Cycle 56)** | **Published, peer-reviewed plus primary tax-authority material, and a bounded-negative family screen (R-035, Cycle 56) — the register's first screen of a *tax-incentive flow* family.** The published record: the mechanism is canonical and its sign is stated (S-274: realising a capital loss before year-end "can accelerate the tax benefits of the loss by as much as twelve months", with the strength of the turn-of-the-year return pattern depending on whether the law encouraged early realisation; S-275, the canonical account of tax-motivated trading by individual investors); the **rule-dated natural experiments have already been run** — the US rule history (S-274), the Swedish five-year carry-forward change whose value is measured at 75 percent of expected lottery proceeds (S-276), and the Japanese 2003 capital-gains-tax reduction (S-279, a working paper); and the family also carries a published **null** (S-278: six Swedish tax regimes, "no support for either of the proposed hypotheses"). The primary rule text: the US limitation on using net capital losses against ordinary income (S-273: the lesser of $3,000 or the total net loss, with the remainder carried forward). **This is a baseline and a rediscovery guard:** no participant is *obliged* to act, so the flow is set by a tax rule rather than by a dated obligation; the sign-determining state (which investors realise which losses) is private tax-lot data, so any cross-sectional condition is a construction; the constrained flow is the seller's own, so there is no third-party concession (the S-151 analogue), and the paired institutional leg is published as well (S-277). **Dated re-screen condition:** re-screen only if a jurisdiction with thin tax-loss-selling literature publishes a loss carry-forward regime change with an effective date and a published expected-flow measure, or if tax-lot-level flow data become publicly disseminated. |
| **Cross-session price formation: the stale-price window between a closed session and the next open (added in Cycle 57; the row was written for real in Cycle 58)** | **Published, peer-reviewed, and a bounded-negative family screen (R-036) — the register's first screen of a *cross-session price-formation* family.** The published record: overnight news impounding into the next open is documented, peer-reviewed prior art — Lu, Malliaris & Qin (S-287) on night-minus-day return predictability and heterogeneous liquidity providers; Camilleri & Green (S-288) on stock market predictability and non-synchronous trading; Barclay & Hendershott (S-305) on after-hours price discovery and trading, which finds that after-hours price discovery is concentrated in a small subset of stocks; and Challet & Gourianov (S-289) on the opening-and-closing auction dynamics that absorb the imbalance. **This is a baseline and a rediscovery guard:** the close-to-open move is an information impoundment, so a candidate claiming a stale-price concession would be claiming a sign the literature already explains; the sign-determining state (overnight news) arrives *after* the decision time, so the sign cannot be conditioned on; the venue's own opening call auction supplies the intermediation at the restart; and the tradeable window is minutes wide, in the thinnest after-hours liquidity, with wider spreads outside regular hours. **Dated re-screen condition:** re-open only if a venue introduces a scheduled, pre-published cross-session trading mechanism that creates a decision-time state with a sign determinable before the next open, or if a published study identifies a residual close-to-open price effect that survives the information-impoundment explanation and is sign-determinable before the open. |
| **Contractual barrier hedging: the published knock-out level and the issuer's hedging discontinuity (added in Cycle 58)** | **Published, peer-reviewed, plus primary regulator and exchange-listing documentation, and a bounded-negative family screen (R-037) — the register's first screen of a *contractual-barrier* family.** The mechanism, its sign and its magnitude are published prior art: Auh & Cho (S-306, *The Review of Asset Pricing Studies*) find that "structured equity derivatives can cause significant price pressure of the underlying stock upon an event of dramatic payoff change", that "a single event accounts for a −6.4 % return on the event day", that one event "increases the probability of a subsequent event by 21.3 %", and that "traders try to liquidate ahead of each other"; Lim & Choi (S-307) document the delta discontinuity at the knock-in level in Korean retail ELS; Cui, Li & Zhang (S-299) document that an autocallable's delta "can become too large near the barriers", so that the authors "apply payoff modification and barrier shifting techniques". The discontinuity is **removed by design**: the barrier shift is the standard remedy (S-308, S-299) and dealers shift the knock-in basis "to reduce the amount of futures they have to buy" (S-304). The published record on the state and the market: the barrier level **is** published before the decision — in the PRIIPs KID (S-302) and in exchange listing documentation (S-303) — but the issuer's hedge is expressly discretionary and unpublished ("The Issuer may enter into hedging arrangements with market counterparties … in order to hedge its exposure"); the regulator publishes per-product barrier levels, offer dates and **volume issued**, not outstanding notional or barrier-level concentration, and records that auto-callable sales volumes have declined sharply in Germany after 2011 and collapsed in Italy (S-301); the dealer-hedging channel is itself a published central-bank argument and is publicly disputed (S-298, S-304); the most direct working-paper claim of the family states that three of its four required variables are not publicly measurable and that its event-window results are "consistency results, not causal identification" (S-300); and the family has already been **turned into a published strategy claim in the damping direction** — retail structured-product issuance creates a net supply of gamma, bank rehedging "compresses the realised volatility of those underlyings", and the phenomenon "gives birth to profitable trading strategies in dispersion format" (S-309, a non-peer-reviewed preprint). **This is a baseline and a rediscovery guard:** any candidate in this family must start from S-306 and must explain what remains; the register's own screen records that the effect is documented, anticipated, engineered away and unmeasurable in magnitude. |
| **Published price of a constraint: repo specialness, securities-lending fee curves, CDS financing spreads and standing-repo award rates (added in Cycle 59; the row was written for real at the Cycle-59 wrap-up)** | **Published as institutional rate series, and a bounded-negative family screen (R-039) — the register's first screen of a *published-price-of-a-constraint* family, and its first screen run in the explicit *state-first* order.** The published state exists and is continuous: the administered reference rates and the secured overnight financing rates are published daily by the administrator and the central bank (S-041), and the repo market's own operating and distribution statistics are published by the central bank and its primary-dealer survey (S-042). **This is a baseline and a rediscovery guard:** in the published work these series are used as *indicators* — states that condition an effect whose sign and existence are already claimed — rather than as an unclaimed edge with a clean sign-determining condition. The screen's own findings are the reason the family is closed: **(i)** the rates are published, widely observed and therefore anticipated or competed away; **(ii)** the decision-time state a candidate would need at the required granularity is *not* cleanly public in the relevant places — securities-lending fee data is fragmented and largely proprietary, and repo specialness is episodic and specialised rather than a continuous series; **(iii)** the family conditions the **magnitude** of an effect more often than it changes the **sign or the existence** of one, which is G-12's binding limb; and **(iv)** without a clean published decision-time state that is not a public real-time by-product of the constrained flow's own trading (the Cycle-52 third test), no candidate in the family could pass the strict bar. **Dated re-screen condition:** re-screen only if a continuously published, decision-time series of a funding, balance-sheet or capacity constraint becomes available at the granularity a candidate would need, with documented cadence and coverage, and the literature shows an unclaimed sign-determining effect that it conditions. |
| **Published fee or toll that prices a third party's dated flow (added in Cycle 60)** | **Published fee schedules exist, but bounded-negative family screen (R-040) — the register's first screen of a *fee/toll* state-kind family.** Fee schedules (clearing/processing, registration/tender, depositary/conversion charges) are published costs; they are typically fixed in schedule form or already embedded in arbitrage calculations. **This is a baseline and a rediscovery guard:** the screen failed on G-12 (sign/existence) — the fee level is a friction, not an unclaimed sign-determining state that flips existence of a published effect; decision-time variation that would meet the bar is not clearly published as a continuous state with an unclaimed sign, and any comparison is already treated as a cost in published baselines. **Dated re-screen condition:** re-screen only if a continuously published, decision-time fee/toll schedule shows a threshold that varies by published state with documented cadence/coverage, and the literature shows an unclaimed sign-determining effect it conditions. |
| **Published count or queue state at decision date (added in Cycle 61)** | **Published counts/queues exist in many markets, but bounded-negative family screen (R-041) — the register's first screen of template (D).** A published count at a decision date is a baseline *level* already embedded in published baselines; to change sign/existence (G-12) rather than just size, an unclaimed sign-determining instance would need a published threshold that flips whether the action is worthwhile, with the count published *before* the decision and not already claimed. The count alone does not constitute an unclaimed sign-determiner (fails G-12); decision-time variation that would flip existence is not clearly published as a continuous state at the needed granularity; any claimed threshold is anticipated or already treated as a friction/baseline; strict bar not satisfied. **Dated re-screen condition:** re-screen only if a continuously published, decision-time count/queue state shows a threshold that varies by published state with documented cadence/coverage, published *before* the decision, and the literature shows an unclaimed sign-determining effect it conditions. |
| **Published reserve/availability/headroom state at decision date (added in Cycle 62)** | **Published reserve/headroom levels exist as baseline indicators (margin cushion, available capacity, position-limit headroom, inventory availability), but bounded-negative family screen (R-042).** Reserve/headroom levels are widely published as baseline indicators already embedded in price formation and do not constitute unclaimed sign-determining states that flip sign/existence of known published effects; conditioning changes magnitude in published work more often than sign/existence in an unclaimed way; many measures are fixed schedule-level or published with a lag; where public and widely observed, effects are anticipated or already embedded in prices; no feasible clean test with an unclaimed mechanism. **Dated re-screen condition:** re-screen only if a continuously published, decision-time reserve/availability/headroom state shows a threshold that varies by published state with documented cadence/coverage, published *before* the decision, and the literature shows an unclaimed sign-determining effect it conditions. |
| **Published constraint breach / non-compliance events at decision date (added in Cycle 63)** | **Published constraint breach / non-compliance events exist as institutional event series (margin violations, position-limit breaches, enforcement actions, covenant breaches), but bounded-negative family screen (R-043) — the register's first screen of template (F).** Event publication is a third-party obligation to disclose rather than a constrained flow from which the screen extracts a concession; published breach events are lagged, security-specific and already embedded in price formation (an enforcement action or breach disclosure is itself price-moving information); to change sign/existence (G-12) rather than just size, an unclaimed sign-determining instance would need a published threshold that flips whether an action is worthwhile, published *before* the decision and not already claimed — no such general-family instance was identified; the effective sample is the number of breaches, not securities × dates; and anticipation/competition through CDS/borrow pricing washes out any residual. **Dated re-screen condition:** re-screen only if a continuously published, decision-time breach/non-compliance event series shows a threshold that varies by published state with documented cadence/coverage, published *before* the decision, and the literature shows an unclaimed sign-determining effect it conditions. |
| **Published constraint waiver/amendment events at decision date (added in Cycle 64)** | **Published constraint waiver/amendment events exist as institutional event series (covenant waivers, position-limit amendments, margin-requirement amendments, regulatory forbearance notices, contractually published renegotiation outcomes), but bounded-negative family screen (R-044) — the register's first screen of template (G).** Event publication is a third-party disclosure obligation rather than a constrained flow conceding an edge; published waiver/amendment events are lagged, security- or issuer-specific and already embedded in price formation; conditioning changes terms or sizing (magnitude) rather than sign/existence in any unclaimed way; the effective sample is the number of waivers/amendments, not securities × dates; and anticipation/competition through CDS/borrow pricing washes out any residual. **Dated re-screen condition:** re-screen only if a continuously published, decision-time waiver/amendment event series shows a threshold that varies by published state with documented cadence/coverage, published *before* the decision, and the literature shows an unclaimed sign-determining effect it conditions. |
| **Published regulatory change-of-law state at decision date (added in Cycle 65)** | **Changes of law or regulation with published effective dates exist as institutional series, but bounded-negative family screen (R-045) — the register's first screen of template (H).** A change of law is published in advance and anticipated; it changes terms and magnitude far more often than sign or existence in an unclaimed way; the constrained party adjusts for its own account, so there is no third-party concession flow to harvest (the S-151 analogue); the effective sample is the number of changes of law, not securities × dates; and anticipation/competition through CDS/borrow pricing washes out any residual. No unclaimed sign-determining instance meeting G-12 was identified for the general family. **Dated re-screen condition:** re-screen only if a change of law creates a dated, price-insensitive, non-discretionary flow executed by a participant other than the obligor, with a **published list of affected securities** and an **unclaimed sign**, published *before* the decision, and the literature shows an unclaimed sign-determining effect it conditions. |
| **Published clamp/cap band of a continuously published state at decision date (added in Cycle 66)** | **Published clamps, caps, floors and bands exist and are visible in real time, but bounded-negative family screen (R-046) — the register's first screen of template (I).** The band is a **published function parameter of the state it bounds**, so its crossing is a deterministic function of an already-published price: conditioning on the band adds no information beyond that price, and it re-describes the state's level rather than supplying a new one. It **fails G-12 as an edge** — a bound changes the magnitude and tail distribution of a state, not the sign of an unclaimed effect (the published work treats the clamp as an *inaction region* in which the state carries no information, S-317, S-318). The band's real role is as a **filter**, not an edge: that is how the register now uses it in IDEA-009's amended specification (V-008, Cycle 66 amendment) and how the published literature uses it, so a published bound used to clean another candidate's state is a baseline, not an unclaimed edge. The parameter is **governance-adjustable and venue-specific** (dYdX publishes that governance may change the clamp factor, the premium-vote clamp factor, the sampling interval and the impact notional, S-321), so the state is not stable across regimes and does not transfer across venues without restatement. The effective sample is the number of band exits, and those episodes are already the sample used by the published work (S-310, S-311, S-319). **Dated re-screen condition:** re-screen only if a continuously published state has a published bound that changes the **sign or existence** of an effect (not merely its magnitude), where the bound is **not** a deterministic function of an already-published price, where the parameter is stable or its changes are published in advance with a dated effect, and where the literature shows an unclaimed sign-determining effect conditioned on the *bound* rather than on the state it bounds. |
| **Crypto perpetual funding-extreme reversal as a published, direction-conditional effect (added in Cycle 66)** | **Published, as direct tests of the hypothesis (S-310, S-320, S-319) and as a systematic review of the family (S-312).** The reversal after an extreme funding rate is published **as conditional on the market's direction**: across 274 extreme events on BTC, ETH and SOL from 2021 to 2026, events on up days reverted by +1.88 % over the next 24 hours while events on down days **continued** by −1.74 % (S-310; author-reported, not peer-reviewed); the unconditional form is a near-null (slope −0.668, R² 0.00109, F 0.388 on 357 df, with reversals reported only at the extremes, S-320; author-reported industry research); the nearest published out-of-sample test of the premise collapsed (validation Sharpe +0.80 → holdout −1.17, "viable only with explicit regime detection", S-319; book case study, not peer-reviewed); and the correction speed is published as **realised-volatility-conditioned** (median 27.0 h against 3.0 h; S-311). **This is a baseline and a rediscovery guard: IDEA-009 must always be described as an incremental variant of published conditional funding-reversal work, never as a discovery of the funding-level reversal, and its unconditional form may not be presented as unclaimed.** |
| **Crowding-conditioned arbitrage profits: the two-sided crowding gate on an arbitrage strategy (added in Cycle 72)** | **Published, peer-reviewed (S-325).** The *shape* of the crowding conditioning that IDEA-004 claims as its differentiator — that the direction of the conditioning on an arbitrage strategy's profit is **two-sided rather than monotone** — is published: "In times of low arbitrage activity, the beta-arbitrage strategy exhibits delayed correction, taking up to three years for abnormal returns to be realized. In contrast, when arbitrage activity is high, prices overshoot and then revert in the long run", with a positive-feedback channel through firm leverage (Huang, Liu, Lou & Polk 2024, *Management Science* 70(8): 5367–5385). The crowding state is a return-comovement measure of the strategy's legs, built on the construct in S-157. **This is a baseline and a rediscovery guard for IDEA-004:** the candidate may no longer present a two-sided crowding gate as unclaimed, and the Cycle-30 note in that dossier asserting the opposite is corrected. The published trade family, universe and crowd are *not* the candidate's, and the published measure is **not** a position-concentration measure of a paired convergence crowd, so **G-28's structural null is untouched** and the published crowding measure **may not be substituted** for the candidate's own state. |
| **Slow-moving arbitrage capital measured from fund flows (added in Cycle 72)** | **Published, peer-reviewed (S-326), with a published design and identification literature alongside it (S-327).** Persistent aggregate capital flows to hedge and mutual funds predict monthly factor returns with an out-of-sample R² reaching 6.6 %, while transient flows "display no such power despite being more predictable", and the mechanism is active fund managers' capital constraints (Dong, Kang & Peress 2025, *Review of Financial Studies* 38(10): 2936–2987). The newest published crowding work (Rodríguez Domínguez & Noguer i Alonso 2026, arXiv:2608.08405) states the full published inventory by which deployed arbitrage capital has been inferred — short interest, return comovement, institutional holdings, and persistent fund flows — records that those measures "rest on different primitives … and they have rarely been benchmarked against one another", publishes a **borrow-rate-derived crowding state** and a crowding-decay calibration (q̂ = 0.280 for four non-overlapping momentum strategies, 0.269 for all thirteen), and establishes that "aggregate crowding is not identified from within-date variation by any estimator" (Proposition 3.18). **This is a baseline and a rediscovery guard for IDEA-004:** the slow-capital mechanism and the fund-flow state of the strategy complex may no longer be presented as unexploited, and any future crowding-conditioned rule must pre-register how the aggregate-crowding effect is identified. The flow state is aggregate and strategy-level, so it is **not** the candidate's pair-level, netted convergence crowd and **may not be substituted** for it. |
| **Quarter-end bank window dressing shifts money-market-fund demand into Treasury bills (added in Cycle 75)** | **Published, as a bounded-negative family screen (R-047) — the register's first mechanism-first screen, a family defined by an institutional mechanism rather than by a published state.** Quarter-end bank window dressing moves institutional demand from private to public safe assets: "Around regulatory reporting dates, Non-U.S. banks reduce borrowing from U.S. money market funds; constrained to hold short-term, high-quality assets, the funds redeploy the displaced cash into Treasury bills. As a result, short-maturity U.S. Treasury yields fall by 3–9 basis points at quarter-ends", with a network shift-share estimate that inflows equal to 1 percent of Treasury-bill market value lower yield spreads by 4.9 basis points (Sun & Tian 2026, SSRN working paper 6913784, *When Banks Retreat: The Demand for Public Safe Assets*; abstract **index-rendered**, not line-verified). **This is a baseline and a rediscovery guard:** the sign, the window and the size of the effect are published, so the family's headline is claimed in the literature; and the *state* that would determine the size is not published at decision time, because it depends on non-US banks' private reporting-date balance-sheet positions and on money-market-fund holdings published only with a lag. |
| **Intraday price pressure around U.S. Treasury auctions and the dealer risk-bearing conditioner (added in Cycle 75)** | **Published (S-330), as the second instance that closes R-047's family.** "We examine intraday price pressure in the U.S. Treasury market around auctions. We find yields rise in the hours before an auction and reverse afterward. This pressure strengthens when dealers face tighter risk-bearing constraints, consistent with a model in which dealers' balance sheets absorb inventory risk" (Fleming, Liu & Nguyen 2026, Federal Reserve Bank of New York Staff Report 1188, March 2026, revised July 2026, DOI 10.59576/sr.1188; **line-verified** from the staff-report page). **This is a baseline and a rediscovery guard:** a dated, non-discretionary, price-insensitive flow obligation in the same market already has a published price effect, and its size-determining conditioner — dealers' risk-bearing capacity — is itself quarterly and lagged, so it is not observable at decision time either. |
| **The price of residual supply: a capacity-scarcity state and a state-scaled premium (added in Cycle 75)** | **Published, as a preprint (S-328), and the trigger for IDEA-004's Cycle-75 re-read.** A market-clearing model of residual-supply pricing in which "forced-sale pressure predicts actual fund selling, contemporaneous price declines, and positive returns over the following one to six months", with the premium "roughly doubles when market-wide absorption capacity is tight, and concentrates in stocks with thin investor bases and limited trading capacity — precisely the cross section in which clearing the imbalance should be most costly, and a pattern that mechanical return reversal does not generate" (Wang 2026, arXiv:2605.30672; **line-verified**). The state is **published and constructible** — 13F ownership for the thickness of the natural investor base, CRSP liquidity and aggregate mutual-fund stress for absorption capacity — and the paper's own keyword list includes **"slow-moving capital"**. **This is a baseline and a rediscovery guard for IDEA-004:** a premium that scales with the capacity of the capital that arbitrages or absorbs may no longer be presented as unclaimed; the published state is **security-level** and measures *risk absorbers*, so it is **not** the candidate's pair-level netted convergence crowd and **may not be substituted** for it; and the published flow-pressure risk-absorption premium is a **rival explanation** that any future test of the candidate's convergence return must separate from. |
| **Flow price impact depends on the execution channel, not on the dollar (added in Cycle 75; **line-verified in Cycle 76**)** | **Published, as a working paper (S-331 — **line-verified in Cycle 76**: the author-hosted PDF was fetched directly, HTTP 200, 978,520 bytes, 63 pages, and its text layer was extracted with `pypdf`; the Cycle-75 extraction failure was a tooling gap, not a document defect, and the entry has moved from *index-rendered* to *line-verified*).** The same dollar committed to the same stock through different vehicles moves prices very differently: institutional capital allocations executed by specialised intermediaries (transition managers) reduce trading costs by 80–90 % with negligible price impact, while mutual-fund flows move prices (Huang, Hu, Song & Xiang 2026, "Same Dollar, Different Impact", HKU working paper, 23 February 2026). **This is a baseline and a rediscovery guard for the register's flow-impact family, and a trigger for IDEA-010:** a candidate that claims an institutional flow exerts price pressure must state the **execution channel** through which the flow reaches the market, and must engage the finding that a flow routed through a specialised intermediary may move prices little or not at all. |
| **The ETF-arbitrage sensitivity is conditioned by volatility, bond-market illiquidity and trading costs (added in Cycle 76)** | **Published, as a working paper (S-195 — Pan & Zeng, *ETF Arbitrage under Liquidity Mismatch*, ESRB Working Paper No. 59, December 2017; **line-verified in Cycle 76** from the ESRB PDF, fetched directly and text-extracted).** The same paper that supplies the candidate's mechanism also states a **competitor conditioner** of the same relation: "the sensitivity of ETF arbitrage by APs is declining in market volatility, bond market illiquidity and costs of trade", with the empirical form "a one standard deviation increase in market volatility generates a 10% decline in AP arbitrage" and the asymmetry "the decrease in arbitrage sensitivity is asymmetrically larger when the ETF premium is negative". **This is a baseline and a rediscovery guard for IDEA-010:** a *published* conditioner of the same ETF-arbitrage relation is **available at decision time** in the candidate's universe (market volatility, bond-market illiquidity, trading costs), whereas the candidate's own state — the AP's regulatory capital ratio — is **not attachable** to a corporate-bond ETF from public filings (G-29, G-30); the candidate may therefore no longer be presented as uniquely state-conditioned. The same paper also publishes the **sign-reversal mechanism** — "ETF arbitrage may go in the opposite direction than what would be implied by the initial relative mispricing" — which is a published instance of the risk the candidate's rule faces. **Caution on the ledger's own quotations:** three strings the ledger quoted for S-195 (the dual-role sentence, the illiquidity sentence and the flow-shock sentence) are the wording of the **SSRN 2019 revision**, which is still index-rendered, not the December 2017 ESRB version's wording; the *substance* of all three is line-verified in the 2017 version in different words. |
| **Fractional baskets and persistent ETF–NAV discrepancies in corporate-bond ETFs, and the AP as the buffer between the ETF and the bond market (added in Cycle 78)** | **Published, as an institutional working paper (S-196, BIS Working Paper No. 975, **November 2021**; SSRN 3886881) and line-verified in Cycle 78** (the BIS PDF fetched directly, HTTP 200, 1.7 MB, 54 pages, text extracted with `pypdf`). The paper's own words: "the portfolio of bonds that are exchanged for new or existing ETF shares (called creation or redemption baskets) often represents a small fraction of ETF holdings – a fact that we call '**fractional baskets**'"; "**ETFs with fractional baskets exhibit persistent premiums and discounts**, which is related to the slow adjustment of NAV returns to ETF returns"; and "We develop a simple model to show that an ETF's authorized participants (APs) can act as a **buffer** between the ETF market and the underlying illiquid assets, and help mitigate fire sales." A companion line-verified source, **S-198** (OFR Working Paper 22-01), publishes the AP-balance-sheet account in the same universe from a policy episode — SMCCF ETF purchases generated "intraday spillover price effects on a relatively short horizon (within 15 minutes)" on other corporate-bond ETFs with overlapping holdings, bonds with more exposure to those APs showed "lower yield spreads (higher prices)", and ETFs with greater overlap "experience higher net inflows the day after closing at a price premium to NAV" — while also stating that "we do not have information on AP-level inventories, we can indirectly infer their corporate bond inventories from public FR 2004 disclosures on aggregate primary dealer activities". **This is a baseline and the rediscovery guard for IDEA-010 (parked, P-014):** a **mechanical** account of the corporate-bond ETF–NAV discount is published in the candidate's own universe, and **no published work separates it from a transient reading (G-34)**. Any future candidate claiming that the ETF–NAV discount of a corporate-bond ETF is a transient, harvestable dislocation must engage S-196 and S-198, must state which reading it claims, and must show that the reading is separable at decision time. |
| **Fixed income cash: Treasury auction price pressure, the cash-futures basis trade, quarter-end window dressing and repo specialness (added in Cycle 80)** | **Published, as a bounded-negative mechanism-first family screen (R-048) — institutional mechanisms in U.S. Treasury cash, repo, futures and the auction cycle rather than a published state.** S-330 documents that yields rise in the hours before a Treasury auction and reverse afterward, sized at 0.7–1.2 bps intraday and 2–5 bps over ten days, strengthening when dealer risk-bearing capacity is tight (line-verified). The Treasury cash-futures basis trade is a published leveraged convergence arbitrage (Barth & Kahn, OFR Working Paper 21-01; Glicoes, Iorio, Monin & Petrasek, Fed Notes 2024-03-08; CFTC MRAC report), quarter-end window dressing's 3–9 bps quarter-end yield move and 4.9 bps per 1 percent-of-market-value inflow elasticity are published (R-047; S-329 index-rendered SSRN abstract), and repo specialness's on-the-run premium and its auction-cycle seasonality are published (D'Amico & Pancost 2018, FEDS WP 2018-21). **This is a baseline and a rediscovery guard:** the sign, window and magnitude of each instance are published, so each headline is claimed in the literature; and the state that determines the size — dealer risk-bearing capacity (quarterly and lagged), money-market-fund holdings (two-month lag), episodic repo specialness — is not published at decision time, so each instance fails G-12's sign-and-existence limb and the decision-time-state bar. No unclaimed sign-determining state was identified for the fixed income cash venue. |
| **Weather-derivative contract pricing: the HDD/CDD temperature-premium family (added in Cycle 87)** | **Published, as a bounded-negative mechanism-first family screen (R-049) — exchange-traded contracts written on a city's heating- and cooling-degree-day indices, the options on them, and the seasonal strips.** The **sign** of the premium is published: contracts are "priced near their actuarially fair value" in normal times, and "contract prices one-month before maturity fall by 2.2%" in the financial crisis, "equivalent to an annualized weather risk premium of 26%", with the declines "greatest for high margin and high total risk contracts" and larger when the TED spread and the VIX rise (S-332, Weagley 2019, *The Review of Financial Studies* 32(6): 2456–2497, DOI 10.1093/rfs/hhy098 — **line-verified from the working-paper version**, the published body being paywalled; S-334, the dissertation lineage, **line-verified**, which also reports that the **normal-time** average premium is "less than 2% per year and is statistically insignificant" and that the crisis premium reaches 30 % per year). The **option leg** is published as well: implied volatilities of weather derivatives are "substantially greater than the realized volatilities for all cities in our sample", a weather variance risk premium of **0.29 on average** (S-333, Bae et al., SSRN 4572123, with the conference version of 29 August 2024 **line-verified**). The **market structure** is published: energy and utility companies buy and short the local temperature futures as hedgers, financial institutions supply the liquidity, and the venue is "**relatively illiquid**" with "**Bid-ask spreads are large**" and many deals "conducted off exchange and submitted as block trades"; markets typically open 1–3 weeks before the contract month (the median market is opened 39 days before maturity) and "open interest does not change on 85% of trading days"; the market's notional value fell "from $32 billion to $15 billion" and monthly-futures open interest fell 40 % between the third and fourth quarter of 2008 (S-332, S-334). The venue is **not dead** — average open interest for January–September 2023 was four times 2022's and twelve times 2019's (S-336, FORRS 2024 factsheet, **line-verified**). **This is a baseline and a rediscovery guard:** the sign, the window and the conditioner are published, so the headline is claimed; what survives is a **magnitude** whose conditioner is not published at decision time; the field's own published sample has to **discard every quote with zero or missing open interest** (S-333); and the published record **contests the premise** (S-335, Chincarini 2011, *Journal of Futures Markets* 31(1): 1–33 — **line-verified in Cycle 88**: the published abstract states that "generally the market seems very efficient despite its lack of liquidity", and the published premia are "0.47% and insignificant" for HDD in 1999–2004 against a CDD premium of "5.76%" that **changes sign** between the two sub-periods). Any future candidate in this family must name the published premium it is differentiating from, must state which of the two published magnitudes (the futures premium or the option-leg WVRP) it claims, and must show that its own conditioner is observable at decision time. **The venue's current listing status, contract specifications and settlement basis were verified in Cycle 88** (S-344 … S-348), which closes R-049's condition (d) without re-opening the family. |
| **Closed-end-fund / BDC traded price-to-NAV discount: a structural, no-creation/no-redemption discount family (added in Cycle 88)** | **Published, as a bounded-negative mechanism-first family screen (R-050) — listed closed-end funds and listed business development companies, i.e. vehicles that issue a fixed share count, offer no continuous creation/redemption, and therefore trade at a persistent discount or premium to a published net asset value.** The **headline and its magnitude are published**: a closed-end fund "usually trade[s] at a discount to NAV", and the discount is a model-derived quantity that "results from a trade-off between the liquidity benefits of investing in the CEF and the fees charged by the fund's managers" (S-337, Cherkes–Sagi–Stanton, **line-verified**); BDCs "tend to trade at a mid single-digit discount in the long run (x̄_MCW = −4.6%)" with a high cross-sectional standard deviation of 11.9 % (S-341, a **non-peer-reviewed** undergraduate thesis, used only as a secondary magnitude source), and the listed-BDC index returns "8.63% per annum based on market values and 9.41% based on NAVs" (S-340, Suhonen, *Financial Analysts Journal* 80(1), **line-verified from the publisher's version of record**). The **sign-changing state is published** — the discount widens "except during times of market stress when the discount can widen substantially" (2020) (S-340) — and so are the **dated episodes**: before the May 2014 index exclusion "BDCs' shares were traded at 5%–10% above their NAVs on average", and the exclusion "pushing BDCs' shares to be traded at a discount for nearly 3 years thereafter" (S-339, Davydiuk–Marchuk–Rosen, *RFS* 37(4), **line-verified**). The **trade itself is published, with a published sign**: "discounts are negatively correlated with future abnormal returns. Those who buy high discount CEFs thinking that they are bargains will have the numbers against them; the apparent undervaluation of the assets is only a delusion" (S-338, **line-verified**, a student working paper), and BDCs with a greater price-to-NAV premium outperform those with a larger discount "by all return metrics" (S-340). The **arbitrage is structurally blocked and the blocking is published law**: CEFs "are not subject to large-scale creation or redemption of shares" (S-337); BDCs may sell below NAV only with shareholder approval (S-339; 15 U.S.C. § 80a-62) and a BDC with that approval must repurchase "25 percent of those securities … in each of the 4 calendar quarters" at NAV (S-342, **line-verified**), while a closed-end company's own repurchase is bounded by the 90 %-distribution condition and by a price rule (S-343, 17 CFR 270.23c-1, **line-verified**). The **decision-time state is stale**: the NAVs "are unknown on such dates and will only be published in the financial reporting of the BDC several weeks after the NAV date" (S-340). **This is a baseline and a rediscovery guard:** the sign, the magnitude, the episodes, the trade and the structural blockers are all published, so any candidate must name the published effect it differentiates from, must answer the published negative result rather than assume it away, and must show a discount-closing mechanism that the statutory repurchase route and the below-NAV issuance constraint have not already provided. |
| **Catastrophe bonds and insurance-linked securities (ILS): a modelled risk premium with a delayed, illiquid secondary market (added in Cycle 90; re-issued as R-051 in Cycle 91)** | **Published, as a bounded-negative mechanism-first family screen (R-051) — privately placed bonds whose coupons pay a spread above a modelled expected loss, with trigger-based structures (indemnity, industry loss, parametric, modelled loss) and settlement often delayed until the triggering event is verified.** The **premium is published as a baseline, with a magnitude**: "Empirically, the cedents pay more than they receive in expectation … the investors' average return to catastrophe bond in coupon is around 2 to 4 times the expected loss", and the published comparison is benchmark-relative — "Whether the positive spread is expensive depends on the benchmark to compare" (S-354, IMF Staff Climate Note 2022/004, **line-verified**). The **spread's own behaviour is published and retrospective**: "risk spreads have widened materially following major catastrophes in the past, such as 2006 following a US hurricane and 2012 following earthquakes in Japan and New Zealand" (S-354). The **sign-determining condition is not determinable ex ante**: the loss is a third-party *estimate* and the resolution architecture is documented in a primary legal record — "a Development Period applied, namely a further time period in which to monitor whether the estimated insured loss developed sufficiently in order to trigger a payment … the Development Period End Date was 18 months after the Risk Period End Date" (S-355, Supreme Court of Bermuda, [2022] SC (Bda) 30 Com) — while the market's most-used trigger type is the slowest, "Indemnity triggers typically involve a relatively lengthy payout process, since loss reporting and verification must be accomplished before the release of funds" (S-353, Wharton Impact Cat Bond Primer, **line-verified**). The **premium's benchmark is a model output and the data do not exist**: "The modeling firm also provides an estimate of the expected loss for investors … historical data on the most extreme natural disasters are typically too scarce for a reliable estimation of the outer tail of the loss distribution" (S-353). The **market structure binds**: "their markets remain fairly shallow", and the barriers are published — "high transaction costs, the requirement of complicated underlying catastrophe models, and a narrow investor base" (S-354). **This is a baseline and a rediscovery guard:** the premium is documented and quantified, the state that moves it is a retrospective event, the sign condition is not knowable before the decision, the benchmark is a private model output, and the market is shallow and costly. Any future candidate in this family must name the published premium it differentiates from and must show a published, decision-time, tradeable state — with verifiable history and low-latency availability — that changes the sign or existence of CAT bond returns after costs in a sample with sufficient liquidity. |
| **Freight-derivative contract pricing: the dry-bulk FFA forward curve and the container-freight futures family (added in Cycle 91)** | **Published, as a bounded-negative mechanism-first family screen (R-052) — the Baltic Exchange's dry-bulk Forward Freight Agreement forward curve (Capesize, Panamax, Supramax, Handysize routes), the container-freight futures listed on the Shanghai International Energy Exchange (SCFIS (Europe service): multiplier CNY 50 per index point; six listed months — February, April, June, August, October, December; daily price limit within ±10% of the preceding settlement; minimum trading margin 12% of contract value, rising to 20% and then 30% near expiry; cash settlement on the arithmetic mean of three weekly SCFIS prints) (S-352, the venue's own 2023 handbook, **line-verified**), and the published container spot indices against which those contracts settle.** The **premium's formation and decomposition are published**: "the bulk of basis volatility can be attributed to expectations about future physical market conditions rather than expectations about future risk premia", with "significant predictability of risk premia by both price-based signals and economic variables" and a published account of "near-rational" but slightly contrarian beliefs, on monthly Baltic data for January 2007 – September 2016 (S-350, Moutzouris & Nomikos 2019, *Journal of Futures Markets* 39(8), accepted version, **line-verified**). The **trade itself is published, with published results and published caveats**: "trading rules do outperform the buy-and-hold benchmark in general" and certain technical rules "consistently outperform the benchmark in the FFA markets even after accounting for transaction costs and order delays induced by thin trading", with the authors' own caveats of "illiquidity and a small sample size" (S-351, Nomikos & Doctor 2013, *Transportation Research Part E* 52, accepted version, **line-verified**). The **cost floor and the market's structure bind**: FFA are "cash-settled forward contracts … the underlying asset being one of the freight indices published by the Baltic Exchange", and "Trades are primarily carried out through brokers in the OTC market and are therefore subject to 'friction' in terms of the speed of execution, liquidity and transaction costs, which may significantly affect profitability" (S-351). The **sign-or-existence limb is not met**: the forward curve is published at decision time and already carries the information, and the published conditioners are literature-standard and lagged (S-350). The **sample and the regime are adverse**: monthly data ending in 2016 (S-350) and a published robustness check that removes 1 April – 24 December 2008, with the published framing of the edge as a "younger market" result expected to decay as the market matures (S-351). **This is a baseline and a rediscovery guard:** the premium, its decomposition, the trade and its caveats are all published, so a candidate here would re-present published work and must answer it explicitly — including the published result's own stated weaknesses of illiquidity, thin trading, order delays and sample size. |
| **Electricity-derivative contract pricing (wholesale power futures/forwards and swaps, added in Cycle 92)** | **Published, as a bounded-negative mechanism-first family screen (R-053).** Wholesale electricity derivatives exist to hedge volatility/spikes; futures do not provide unbiased forecasts of spot prices and premiums are regime-dependent (S-356). Accurate modeling requires mean reversion, changing long-run expectations, seasonality, extreme volatility/spikes, time-varying jump intensity to fit spot and futures (S-357). Long-tenor contracts can be scarce ("missing markets") reflecting incompleteness/costs (S-358). The literature splits between price-formation archetypes and empirical work with few reconciliations (S-358). **This is a baseline and a rediscovery guard:** hedging/tail-risk is well-documented; no published decision-time state was identified that changes the sign or existence of a tradeable edge after costs not already captured by the baseline literature, so the family fails G-12. |
| **Interval-fund / semi-liquid private-credit repurchase: a periodic tender at net asset value with no secondary market (added in Cycle 93)** | **Published, as a bounded-negative mechanism-first family screen (R-054) — registered closed-end funds and BDCs that offer a periodic repurchase of their own shares at net asset value under Rule 23c-3, with no secondary market for those shares.** The **mechanism is published law fixed in advance**: the company "shall repurchase the stock for cash at the net asset value determined on the repurchase pricing date"; the offer amount "shall not be less than five percent nor more than twenty-five percent"; the pricing date "shall occur no later than the fourteenth day after a repurchase request deadline"; payment follows "seven days after the repurchase pricing date"; and the schedule is fixed in a fundamental policy "changeable only by a majority vote of the outstanding voting securities", notified "no less than twenty-one and no more than forty-two days before each repurchase request deadline" (S-359, the rule itself, **line-verified**). The SEC's own account is that interval funds "offer the predictability of a mandatory, limited, rules-based liquidity framework" (S-360, 91 FR 63389). The **tradeable object does not exist**: a fund's own notice states "the purpose of this offer is to provide a level of liquidity to shareholders **since no secondary market exists for these Shares**" and "The Shares are not traded on any organized market or securities exchange" (S-361, Form N-23c-3, **line-verified**); the regulator locates the discount object in the **listed** class — "Where listed shares trade at a discount to the underlying portfolio's NAV, there may also be opportunities for the fund to take actions that benefit all shareholders" (S-360, 91 FR 63448) — and the published account distinguishes the two legs: "Publicly traded BDCs face little direct redemption pressure because investors can exit by selling shares in secondary markets" (S-363, **line-verified**). The **sign-determining state is unobservable at decision time**: "The NAV at which the Fund will repurchase Shares will not be calculated until the Repurchase Pricing Date … may be higher or lower", and the sector's supervisor states that "data gaps, lack of a secondary market to publicise valuations, and limited disclosure" make the vulnerabilities hard to monitor (S-362).
