The **cost and rationing mechanics bind**: tenders beyond the offer are met "on a pro rata basis" (S-359), with "There can be no assurance that the Fund will be able to repurchase all Shares that each shareholder has tendered", and the regulator's risk language says a fund unprepared for repurchases may "sell off less liquid assets at a discount to fair value (thus diluting the value of outstanding shares)" (S-360, 91 FR 63438). The **sample and regime are adverse**: 139 funds and $101 billion of net assets in 2025, and "By contrast, interval funds experienced net inflows during the same period" when the early-2026 repurchase pressure "largely affected non-traded BDCs" (S-360, 91 FR 63430). **This is a baseline and a rediscovery guard:** the family is the structural **mirror image** of the Cycle-88 listed price-to-NAV discount family — the mirror removes the discount object — so the two families' verdicts must not be interchanged, and any future candidate must specify the state, the exit and the final rule separately. |
| **Tax-exempt municipal bonds: the dealer-intermediated retail cost premium and the primary-market underwriting concession (added in Cycle 94)** | **Published, as a bounded-negative mechanism-first family screen (R-055) — the over-the-counter tax-exempt municipal market, in which a retail or small-institution customer buys and sells bonds from a dealer without a centralized facility, and the primary market, in which an issuer's underwriter purchases a new issue and distributes it earning the "concession".** The **cost is published and measured by the market's own regulator**: the average effective spread for municipal transactions "shot up to **63 basis points** in May 2022" and was "approximately **50 basis points**" in late 2025; "the effective spread for odd-lot customer trades is **2.4 times** that for block customer trades"; and odd-lot effective spreads at non-institutional dealers are "nearly **2.7 times**" those at institutional dealers (S-369, MSRB Research Paper, April 2026, **line-verified**). The **peer-reviewed synthesis** reports the same in round-trip terms — retail round-trip trading costs "were still **1.1 percent in 2023**" against 2.3 percent in the early 2000s, and "**5 percent of small bond transactions have markups over 3.29 percent**" — and lists the market's puzzles: "high underwriting costs, conflicting roles by municipal advisors, extreme and widely varying trade markups, investment holdings that are often not tax-efficient, **inconsistent implied marginal tax rates**, a **heavy reliance on credit ratings**, little benefit but widespread use of insurance" (S-372, *Journal of Economic Perspectives* 39(2), Spring 2025, **line-verified**). The **decision-time state is absent**: "**Municipal bond transactions are publicly disclosed after they occur, but individual investors have limited pre-trade information, particularly with respect to quoted prices**"; "Electronic trading platforms exist only in the interdealer market, with no widely used platform for individuals to trade directly with each other" (S-372); and in fixed income "pre-trade quote data are **not universally available or nationally consolidated**" (S-369). Real-time municipal trade data is a **paid subscription**, and the historical academic product carries a **three-year lag** and **anonymised dealer identifiers** (S-367); the benchmark is a **proprietary** series ("Bloomberg Valuation Services (BVAL)") and even the bid-wanted state is seen on a proprietary terminal (S-368). The **record is absent exactly where the concession is largest**, because the exempt trade classes are "List Offering or Takedown Transactions", short-term instruments maturing in nine months or less, "Auction Rate Securities, Variable Rate Demand Obligations", commercial paper, and trades "**away from market**" (S-371). The **rating element is a closed-list rule with a published price effect**: 15c2-12(b)(1) permits the omission of "**no more than**" a listed set that includes "ratings", and 15c2-12(b)(5)(i)(C)(11) makes "**Rating changes**" a disclosable event within ten business days (S-366); the rating's price effect is published (a 2010 recalibration on "$2.2 trillion in municipal debt" produced "a 10 to 33 basis point decrease in credit spreads") and "investors rely heavily on credit ratings, even when changes in ratings convey relatively little new information" (S-372). The **tax-equivalent arithmetic is a published transform and a named puzzle**: "The **Muni/TSY ratio** is the benchmark tax-exempt yield for a maturity divided by the yield on Treasury bonds for the same maturity" (S-368), and "A longstanding puzzle in the municipal bond literature is that short-term municipal yields are consistent with the tax rates we would expect for investors in the top income tax brackets, whereas longer-term municipal yields are higher than we would expect" (S-372). The **concession and the mark-up are defined, published terms** for the dealer's remuneration (S-373), the primary market's flow is the **issuer's own** and "negotiated offerings accounted for … **77%** of the total par amount issued" in 2024 (S-374), and the compensation arrangement is a disclosed term of the transaction (S-365). **This is a baseline and a rediscovery guard:** the sign, the magnitude, the size-dependence, the decay and the reform proposals are all published, so any candidate in this family must name the published spread and the published puzzle it is differentiating from, must state how its own state is observable **before** the decision, and must explain why the published cost is not the cost it proposes to harvest. The register also records, as a **framing correction**, that the "Muni Monday" element named in the Cycle-93 handoff is **uncorroborated** in the indexed literature, in the peer-reviewed synthesis, in the MSRB's own term list and in all nine MSRB/regulator documents fetched in Cycle 94. |
| **Perpetual-futures funding-rate mean reversion: the extreme-funding counter-trend rule, keyed to the funding level's position in its own trailing distribution (added in Cycle 112)** | **Published, as a bounded-negative screen (R-059) — and published by the venue itself.** The rule was stated by the venue's co-founder in **2017** (S-391: "A large negative funding rate predicts a positive return for the next 8 hour period. A large positive funding rate predicts a negative return for the next 8 hour period", with per-cell success rates of 47.62 %, 53.23 %, 45.68 % and 44.44 % over 524 funding periods); it was tested independently in **2026** on the same trailing-percentile construction (S-390: extreme negative funding followed by +0.506 % at 24 hours, FDR-robust, while "extreme positive funding does not exhibit a comparable 24-hour effect"); and a 791-contract panel reports the level's relation to returns flipping sign between a 2020–21 reversal regime and a 2023–26 continuation regime, the percentile construction reported null, the "crowded position" reading contradicted, and the effect's excess over the round trip indistinguishable from zero (S-389). The **funding carry** that survives is a separate published baseline (S-086, S-087, S-392), not this family. |
| **Currency-option implied distribution states (implied volatility, skewness, 25-delta risk reversal) as predictors of exchange-rate returns (added in Cycle 112)** | **Published, as a bounded-negative mechanism-first family screen (R-061).** The options-implied standard deviation, skewness and kurtosis of six major currency pairs "consistently explain subsequent currency excess returns for horizons between one week to twelve months" (S-395, with adjusted R² from 13 % to 28 % at one month and 58 % to 84 % quarterly with the term structure); the out-of-the-money put-call relative price improves out-of-sample forecasts of USD/EUR against the random walk and interest-rate parity benchmarks (S-396, *Journal of International Financial Markets, Institutions & Money* 2010); and the published out-of-sample counterweight for the majors is a **random-walk RMSE ratio of 1.06** (S-397). The **FX fixing** family (S-074 … S-081) and the **option-implied crash measures** (S-223) are separate baselines. |
| **US commodity futures' published speculative-position-limit and accountability state: the federal spot-month limit, the exchange-set non-spot-month limit or accountability level, and the published open-interest-against-limit construction (added in Cycle 113)** | **Published, as a bounded-negative screen (R-062) — and the mechanism is a *request*, not a forced reduction.** The **law** is registered and line-verified (S-398, 17 CFR Part 150, fetched directly from the eCFR versioner API and text-extracted to 115,759 characters): "Position accountability" is defined in § 150.1 as a rule that "Requires an entity whose position exceeds the accountability level to **consent** to … **Halt increasing further its position or reduce its position in an orderly manner, in each case as requested by** the designated contract market or swap execution facility"; spot-month limits are "no greater than **25 percent of the estimated spot month deliverable supply**, calculated separately for each month to be listed" (§ 150.5(b)(1)(i)) on an estimate the exchange supplies to the Commission "**upon request**" (§ 150.3(f)); outside the spot month the obligation is limits "**or** position accountability" at a "necessary and appropriate" level (§ 150.5(b)(2)(i)), with the level anchored to "**10 percent** of the average combined futures and delta-adjusted option month-end open interest in the contract for the most recent calendar year up to **50,000 contracts**, with a marginal increase of **2.5 percent** of open interest thereafter" (Appendix F(a)(1)(iv)); and a denied exemption obliges compliance "**within a commercially reasonable time, as determined by the Commission in consultation with the applicant and the applicable designated contract market**", with no enforcement action pursued during the review (§ 150.3(b)(3)(ii)(B)–(C)). The **publication** is the Commission's own account (S-399, line-verified): 25 core referenced futures contracts expanded from 9, the 10 %/2.5 % construction, and **pre-announced step-downs** (Light Sweet Crude Oil at 6,000 / 5,000 / 4,000 contracts on the three, two and one business days before the last trading day; Live Cattle at 600 / 300 / 200). The **exchange rulebook** reads the same way (S-402, index-rendered): an accountability level may be exceeded without a violation, and a reduction follows the exchange's order after private contact. The **sign is already claimed, and the published claim is a null** (S-401, index-rendered): the CFTC economist memorandum of 21 August 2009 found "little evidence to suggest that changes from a position limit regime to an accountability level regime or changes in the levels of position limits impact price volatility in either energy or agricultural markets" — and that memorandum speaks to the **pre-2020** regime, the regime in force being the 2020 final rule at **86 FR 3236** (S-400, line-verified). **No published headroom or breach series exists**: the limit is published, the position that would exhaust it is not, and the constrained population excludes the enumerated bona fide hedges (S-398, Appendix A). |
| **The CFTC's weekly Commitments of Traders as a decision-time positioning state: commercial, index-trader and managed-money positioning against open interest, published weekly for Tuesday positions (added in Cycle 113)** | **Published, as a bounded-negative screen (R-062) — and already a registered source and a parked candidate's own state.** The COT is a **registered source** (S-030, S-031) and is already the conditioning state of the **parked** candidate **IDEA-005 (P-009)** — "the passive's own position in the expiring contract (CFTC 'Index Traders' positions, weekly, positions as of Tuesday, published Friday)" — so a COT candidate that differs only in label, parameter or time frame is a **variant**, which the rediscovery guard forbids. The **publication mechanics** are the regulator's own (S-403, line-verified): "Generally, the data in the COT reports is from **Tuesday and released Friday**"; the report is published "generally each **Friday at 3:30 pm Eastern Time**"; "It takes **three days** to process the data"; and "Because of resource constraints, we are currently only able to release this report **once a week**". The **coverage rule** makes the state endogenous to the thing it would measure: a contract "**no longer appears in the COT report**" when the number of reportable large traders "drops below **20**". The **state's own integrity limits** are documented by the regulator: the classification is "based on the predominant business purpose **self-reported** by traders on the CFTC Form 40"; "CFTC staff does not know specific reasons for traders' positions"; "Due to legal restraints (CEA Section 8 data and confidential business practices), the CFTC **does not publish** information on how individual traders are classified"; "**No**, historical data is not updated once published"; and week-to-week changes can arise from a re-filed Form 40, a new trader's positions being netted into the prior week, or an existing trader's exit. The **sign is claimed** in the register's own held crowding and positioning literature (S-026, S-157, S-222, S-280) and in the published COT-prediction literature. |
| **Import-licence rationing and the US sugar tariff-rate quota: a dated two-tier tariff step on a quantity limit whose exhaustion is published only retrospectively (added in Cycle 114)** | **Published, as a bounded-negative construction-first method test (R-063) — and the price step is not where the US sugar price is made.** The **state** is real and weekly: CBP's Commodity Status Report for tariff-rate quotas publishes the "restraint limit, quantity entered to date (expressed numerically), and the percentage utilized", posted "the first business day of every week" (S-413, S-414, line-verified), with the fill date given only "whenever possible" and the "potentially filled" flag set at a discretionary "95% of the restraint limit" and described as "advice to the filer" (S-420, line-verified). The **step** is real and published ex ante: the FY2027 in-quota raw cane sugar quantity is 1,117,195 MTRV and refined 22,000 MTRV (S-416, S-421, line-verified), the high-tier duty is 15.4 c/lb raw and 16.21 c/lb refined against freight of 2-4 c/lb (S-417, S-418, line-verified), and "Nothing in this subpart shall affect the ability to enter articles at the over-quota tariff rate" (S-433, line-verified). The **family is a published baseline**: the US sugar TRQ is the worked case study of the government's own tariff-rate quota textbook, which states the quota rent "R = P - (1+t)", distinguishes "Quota is binding M3 = Q" from "Quota no longer binding M4 > Q", says that below the quota "the tariff quota functions as an ordinary tariff applied at the in-quota rate", and warns that "quota fill is not a reliable indicator of administrative performance" (S-423, line-verified); the post-2010 guard adds that TRQ administration as a "binding" trade restriction is empirically published (S-424), that the quota fill rate is itself a published research object with an optimality analysis (S-425), and that the nearest published test of the sugar TRQ's own price effect is a 1994 CGE estimate of the effects on "sugar prices and quantities" (S-426). The **base rate** is the decisive empirical fact: the WTO raw sugar TRQ was 36% filled with 713,817 MTRV not entered and a WASDE projected shortfall of 189,922 STRV (S-428, line-verified), and where a sugar TRQ does exhaust - the FY2027 specialty sub-quota - it was "over-subscribed at opening moment" and rationed at a pro rata 44.58212%, so the exhaustion date is known and the rationing is a published proration rather than a price step (S-422, line-verified). The **price is made elsewhere**: 7 CFR 1435.302(a)(1) sets the overall allotment quantity so as to "maintain raw and refined sugar prices above minimum prices to avoid forfeiture of loans to the CCC" and at not less than 85% of estimated domestic consumption, the FY2027 loan rate is 24.00 c/lb raw cane and 32.77 c/lb refined beet, and the FY2027 OAQ of 10,574,850 STRV was published in the Federal Register on 1 October 2026 - all pre-announced (S-430, S-431, S-432, line-verified). **The construction is also not a tradability failure, and the register withdrew its own false kill**: the domestic leg is listed (ICE Futures U.S. Sugar No. 16, physical delivery at five US refinery ports, S-427, line-verified), while the refined-beat leg is only a published cash series. The EU publishes the same object - "current balances (remaining amount of the quota that is still available ...)" on the Commission's tariff-quota consultation database, with "once the quota is reached, no further imports ... is allowed" (S-429, line-verified) - so the state generalises, and the EU instance is the register's already-closed (A)/(C) template. |
| **Published statutory budget-transfer state: a sovereign wealth fund's annual transfer to the national budget, defined by statute as a fixed fraction of the fund's published portfolio valuation (added in Cycle 123)** | **Bounded negative at the design stage (R-064) — a construction-first screen killed by the register's own narrowed rule, with zero fetches.** The **state** is a published annual transfer amount; the **flow** is the dated transfer executed by the fund's manager on the state's behalf. The state is **not** matched by templates A–I (it is not the price of a constraint, a fee, a count, a reserve, a breach, a waiver, a change of law, or a clamp band), so the screen ran to the narrowed rule's clauses. **Kill 1 — accounting by-product:** the amount is *defined* as a fraction of the fund's published portfolio valuation, so the state is a scaled copy of the flow it would trade; the Cycle-52 third test fails with it. **Kill 2 — the two-branch reading:** on the surviving branch (state = the published portfolio valuation), the state is a **magnitude** and not a sign-determiner, and the amount is a **budget figure published in advance** of a **statutorily fixed** transfer date, so the flow is anticipated and G-12 and G-13 fail together. **No instance was verified and none was needed**: the kill is structural and instance-independent. Recorded here as a rediscovery guard so that a later session does not re-run this state kind; the register's own coverage map (R-064) places it outside templates A–I and inside the residual closed set. |
| **A dated, published supply-loss notice: an outage or force-majeure notice carrying a stated restart date (added in Cycle 125)** | **Bounded negative at the design stage (R-065) — the record-first scan's first candidate; zero fetches.** The register-memory check hits coverage rows **10** (a schedule of dated third-party flows) and **20** (asset-class families: commodity inventory and roll); the template screen finds no match beyond R-063's reason 4. The kills: the **restart date is a revised estimate, not a clean decision-time state** (one notice with no ETA, one pushed back, one "subject to change" — the register's screens have never accepted a forecast as a state); **"the price is made elsewhere"** (R-063 reason 4 — the affected supply is priced at a hub, so the record does not set the sign of a harvestable wedge); **the sign is not in doubt, so the state is a magnitude** (G-12 — the notice sizes a known-sign disruption); and the resumption is the **obligor's own flow**. **The entry was claimed in Cycle 124 and written in Cycle 125** — a record-integrity repair, not a second screen. **Dated re-screen condition:** a firm, non-revisable restart date; the affected flow priced at the affected location; a notice that determines the *sign* rather than the size; and a measurable base rate. |
| **A published default-portfolio auction record: a central counterparty's default-management and portfolio-auction publication (added in Cycle 125)** | **Bounded negative at the design stage (R-066) — the record-first scan's second candidate; zero fetches.** Register-memory check: coverage **row 10** (the map's own parenthetical lists "auctions") and **row 12** (delivery and settlement mechanics), with **template F** (a member default is a published breach event) and **template G** (a waiver of the waterfall in place of an auction). Kills: the published portfolio **is** the flow (narrowed rule (i) and the Cycle-52 third test); on the pre-auction reading the state is a breach event plus a size and on the post-auction reading the record is published **after** the auction, so **neither branch is a published, decision-time, sign-determining state** (G-12 and G-13); the auction's reference value is the **market price** of the auctioned instruments ("the price is made elsewhere", R-063 reason 4); the discount is realised by the auction winner inside the waterfall (the S-151 analogue) and the effective sample is the number of member defaults. **A named live variant — the CCP's published default-fund and stress-loss state read against the CCP's own listed credit — is closed as a variant of baseline rows 24, 25 and 68.** |
| **A published revision calendar with revised values: an official statistical agency's revision schedule and revised series (added in Cycle 125)** | **Bounded negative at the design stage (R-066) — the record-first scan's third candidate; zero fetches.** Register-memory check: coverage **row 13** (administrative and policy balance-sheet states) and **row 21** (the statutory budget-transfer state), and baseline **row 19** (a scheduled publication whose expectations are not published is a news event, not a state-conditioned concession). Kills: the **sign** of a revision's price effect is set by the *surprise*, and an official record publishes no expectation, so the published calendar and revised value determine only the **magnitude** of the new level (G-12); the revision **date** is published in advance, so the event is anticipated (G-13); and a statistical revision creates **no committed third-party flow** — the repricing is discretionary, so there is no concession to harvest. Every sub-variant in which the revision *does* create a commitment is already closed (template H, baseline rows 32 and 14's coverage rows, coverage rows 12, 18 and 21). |
| **A published levy schedule: a statutory levy or surcharge schedule with a dated trigger that changes a relative cost between two venues (added in Cycle 125)** | **Bounded negative at the design stage (R-066) — the record-first scan's fourth candidate; zero fetches.** Register-memory check: **template C** (a published fee or toll that prices a third party's dated flow; R-040's own recorded reasons — "the fee level is a friction, not an unclaimed sign-determining state", and "any comparison is already treated as a cost in published baselines"), **template H** (a published change of law, R-045 — published in advance, so anticipated), coverage **row 19** (venue and regulatory trading constraints) and coverage **row 11** (the mandated-flow variant). Kills: the charge is a deterministic function of the taxed notional, so the schedule is the **price of the flow** (narrowed rule (i)); the wedge **is** the cost — it arbitrages to the levy difference, and where the venues are not substitutes the price effect depends on unpublished elasticities and depth (G-12); the statutory trigger is pre-announced, so the cost change is priced at announcement (G-13); and the flow that migrates is the **obligor's own discretionary** flow. |
| **A published congestion or capacity-utilisation measurement: a port's, airport's, pipeline's or data centre's published queue, waiting time, utilisation ratio or headroom (added in Cycle 126)** | **Bounded negative at the design stage (R-067) — the published-measurement route's first candidate set; zero fetches.** Register-memory check: the state kind is **new to the coverage map** (rows 1–24) — the register's own uses of the word "congestion" are the *balance-sheet/market-depth* sense (IDEA-002, IDEA-009's universe exclusion), and its only use of "utilisation" is the CBP quota-fill sense (row 97) — but the **adjacent families are closed**: rows 12, 21, 68, 81 and 89 (the freight-derivative family, R-052), coverage row 20. Kills by sub-variant: the **utilisation ratio** is the flow's own accounting normalised by a fixed capacity (R-064's D-1 kill and narrowed rule (i)); the **queue/waiting-time** form is a published queue (**template D**, row 70) and a subset of the flow; the **headroom** form is a published availability measure (**template E**, row 71) whose reason 3 is the publication lag; the **disinterested vendor congestion index** fails on **G-12** (congestion's price sign is known a priori — it sizes the effect), on **"the price is made elsewhere"** (R-063 reason 4: the good is priced at a benchmark, so the local-vs-benchmark spread's sign depends on an unpublished local basis), and on **narrowed rule (ii)** (the adjacent price consequences are the register's own closed families); and the only reading in which the sign *depends* on the state is a **threshold crossing** on a continuously published state, which is **template I** (row 75). **Obligor-side** for the owner's own queue/headroom/utilisation; **disinterested-publisher side** for the vendor index, and it fails on different clauses. |
| **A published supervisor's or regulator's aggregate of a private quantity: positions, balances, exposures, failures or defaults, used as a state rather than as a rule (added in Cycle 126)** | **Bounded negative at the design stage (R-067) — the published-measurement route's second candidate set; zero fetches.** Register-memory check: rows **95, 96** (R-062: the CFTC's position-aggregate and accountability state), **52** (the AP regulatory-capital ratio, the operative state of the parked IDEA-010), **57** (crowding as a published first-order conditioner, where "the published relation is **monotone** where the candidate claims a two-sided gate"), **61** (the short-interest aggregate branch), **24, 25** (dealer balance-sheet and intermediary-leverage aggregates) and **42**; coverage rows 16 and 42. Kills: the **role-relabelling is not a new state kind** — the coverage map and the template programme are closed on *kinds of published state*, so an aggregate "used as a state" is still a published count (**template D**, row 70), a published balance (**template E**, row 71), a published breach (**template F**, row 72) or a published band (**template I**, row 75); the aggregate is the **sum of the participants' own records**, so it is the obligors' accounting published by a third party — a **third structural category** the Cycle-125 obligor-side/disinterested-publisher dichotomy did not name (see R-067's closing section); the **identity** limb (G-28) bites because a market-wide aggregate is not invertible to the cross-sectional composition a price effect depends on; the sign of the published relations is **already claimed** (rows 57, 61, 96); and the failure-count form inherits R-043's reason 4 (the effective sample is the number of failures, not securities × dates). |
| **A published adoption or diffusion count: a count of adopters of a technology, standard, platform or practice published by an industry body or a vendor (added in Cycle 126)** | **Bounded negative at the design stage (R-067) — the published-measurement route's third candidate set; zero fetches.** Register-memory check: **template D** (a published count, row 70), baseline **row 20** (the fast-payment-rails family, whose recorded reason is exactly this one — "the family's decision-time object is a rail's *adoption curve* … the effect is a level shift in reserve demand that is repriced as it is learned"), baseline **row 49** (the 10b5-1 adoption event's price effect is published), coverage row 10, and **IDEA-011's own family** (a diffusion count is the same mechanism as the accounting-clock candidate and is therefore a **variant** unless the counted object is a *different kind of decision*). Kills: the diffusion stock is a **monotone level**, so the sign of the diffusion effect is known a priori and the count only sizes it (**G-12**); a count that crosses a published threshold to flip the effect's existence is **template I**; a count of adopters of a *mandated* practice is **template H** with the flow being the obligor's own; and the handoff's live variant — a count of adopters of a **different kind of decision** (a fee schedule, an execution convention, a tax-incentive-driven practice) — is closed by **template C** (row 69), **row 46** and coverage **row 19**, and **row 65** respectively. **The residual is CLOSED in Cycle 128 (R-068 D4): the rescuing sub-variant could be named — a published count of adopters of a *contested-sign* decision (a decision whose adoption's price effect is not known a priori in sign), a published count of dual-class issuers being the archetype — and it is killed by the **count-dilemma**: if the adoption's price effect is known a priori, the count is a monotone level that sizes the effect and cannot flip its sign or its existence (**G-12**; R-041's reason 1); if it is *not* known a priori, the count does not contain the sign at all, so the state is not a sign-determiner and recovering the sign would require a construction (R-020's recorded principle). The two cases are exhaustive and kill in opposite directions; the threshold variant is **template I**, the mandated variant is **template H**, and the published-count *object* is **template D** in any case. R-067's set (3) is therefore a complete kill, and the coverage map's row 27 is closed in full.** |
| **A published venue-share or fragmentation measurement: a regulator's or vendor's published share of trading by venue, or a published tick-size / liquidity dispersion measure (added in Cycle 126)** | **Bounded negative at the design stage (R-067) — the published-measurement route's fourth candidate set; zero fetches.** Register-memory check: baseline rows **82** (execution channel, not the dollar, determines flow price impact — line-verified S-331), **83** (ETF-arbitrage sensitivity conditioned by volatility, illiquidity and costs — line-verified S-195), **33**, **52**, **63**, **84**, and coverage **row 19**. This is the **only one of the four sets whose template screen produced no match on the measurement itself** — a venue-share ratio and a dispersion statistic are not a price, fee, count, reserve, breach, waiver, change of law or clamp band — so the kill had to come from the narrowed rule's clauses; the **refinement** recorded in R-067 is that the share table's *inputs* are template-D counts and the object being fragmented is a venue's own traded price (template A's neighbourhood), and the kill does not rest on the template. Kills: the venue-share table is computed from **the volume records of the very venues whose flow the candidate would trade**, so it is the flow's own accounting at venue level (narrowed rule (i) and the Cycle-52 third test); the **sign** of a fragmentation effect is not determined by the share level, because it depends on the order-splitting decision, each venue's information content and its queue, all of which are **private** (G-12, and R-020's recorded principle that a state which would have to be constructed is not a published state); the price consequence of the channel is **already published** (rows 82 and 83), so the constructed wedge is a published baseline (narrowed rule (ii)); a fragmentation premium **is** the cost of the mechanism, so the wedge equals the friction and the party who harvests it is the party whose own splitting decision created it; the threshold variant is **template I**; and the ETF reading is a **variant of the parked IDEA-010**. |
| **A real-time order-flow or order-book-imbalance state: a computed microstructure quantity — an order-flow imbalance, a signed order-flow measure or the book imbalance itself — used as a decision-time state (added in Cycle 128)** | **Bounded negative at the design stage (R-068) — the inverted route's first candidate kind; zero fetches.** Register-memory check: the kind is **new to the coverage map (rows 1–28)** and was surfaced by reading the register's own corpus **backwards** (S-011, S-051); the adjacent families are closed — **row 10** (commodity index roll: order-flow impact), **row 11** (liquidity provision around predictable large trades), **row 82** (flow price impact depends on the execution channel, not on the dollar) and **row 5** (short-term reversal and liquidity provision). Template D does not reach it, because nothing is **published**: the quantity is **computed** at decision time. Kills: **R-020's recorded principle** — a state which would have to be constructed is not a published state, and the imbalance is constructed from the limit order book; **narrowed rule (i) with the Cycle-52 third test** — the imbalance **is** the flow, signed, so the state's numerator is the flow it would trade; and **G-12** — the sign of the price impact is the sign of the flow, known a priori, while what the state adds is magnitude (the impact coefficient falls with depth). **Dated re-screen condition:** a *published* record of an order-flow aggregate that is not computed from the flow's own book, which no instrument currently provides. |
| **A published settlement-based market-liquidity measurement: a market-wide liquidity measure computed from a settlement utility's participants' own settlement records (added in Cycle 128)** | **Bounded negative at the design stage (R-068) — the inverted route's second candidate kind; zero fetches.** Register-memory check: the kind is **new to the coverage map (rows 1–28)**; the adjacent families are closed — **row 28** (payment-system / CLS settlement deadlines as a price determinant), **row 29** (securities settlement cycle length as a price-impact and market-quality determinant), **row 30** (the post-US-close FX window as a market-quality object), **row 15** (the funding-currency / global-liquidity channel and FX carry crashes) and coverage **row 20**. Kills: the **aggregated-obligor side** (R-067's third structural category) — the measure is computed from **the participants' own settlement records** and is a public, real-time by-product of the very settlement flows it would condition on (the Cycle-52 third test); the **identity** limb (**G-28**) — a market-wide liquidity measure is not invertible to the cross-sectional composition a price effect depends on; **narrowed rule (ii)** — the price consequence (currency risk premia conditioned by a liquidity state) is a published baseline; and **G-12** — a liquidity *level* sizes the effect, it does not flip its sign. **Dated re-screen condition:** a published liquidity measure that is *not* computed from the participants' own records. |
| **The execution channel through which an institutional flow is executed: a private routing attribute — the vehicle, account or intermediary that executes a given flow (added in Cycle 128)** | **Bounded negative at the design stage (R-068) — the inverted route's third candidate kind; zero fetches.** Register-memory check: the kind is **new to the coverage map (rows 1–28)** — coverage **row 19** (venue and regulatory trading constraints) does not reach a per-flow routing attribute, and template C does not reach it; the adjacent families are closed — **row 82**, which *is* the published price consequence of the channel and is line-verified from this very source (S-331) — **row 46** (the closing-auction passive execution convention) and **row 63** (closing-price formation). Kills: **R-020's recorded principle** — the channel is a **private routing decision** that would have to be inferred from the flow's investor type, and inference is construction; **narrowed rule (ii)** — the price consequence is already published, so the constructed wedge is a published baseline; **G-12** — the sign of a channel's effect depends on the flow's information content, urgency and the manager's discretion, all private and none of them in the state; and the wedge **is** the cost, so where the vehicles are substitutes the impact difference arbitrages away (R-066's D-3 kill 2 in the same shape). The kill is the same on the alternative reading — that the channel is a published structural fact, as in row 46 — because the price consequence is published either way and the per-flow attribute is private either way. **Dated re-screen condition:** a *published*, per-flow disclosure naming the executing vehicle, which no instrument currently provides. |
| **Investor attention (search volume / attention index) (added in Cycle 129, written in Cycle 131)** | **Bounded negative at the design stage (Cycle 129, batch 2 — S-027) — the inverted route's fourth out-of-map kind; zero fetches.** Register-memory check: the kind is **new to the coverage map**; the adjacent families are closed — **row 20** (the retail / same-day-expiry flow and market-maker gamma family, of which this measure is a variant), **row 33** (the structural-share family, which this kind opens), and **coverage row 10** (asset-class mechanism families). Kills: **R-020's recorded principle** — the measure is a *constructed* index of retail search counts, not a published institutional record, and a state that must be constructed is not a published state; **narrowed rule (i)** — the index is a normalised re-scaling of the very retail attention flow it would condition, so the Cycle-52 third test fails on it; **G-12** — the published relation is "attention up ⇒ prices up in the short run, with a later reversal", which is a magnitude and an anticipation, not a sign that the state determines; and **G-13** — the index is published continuously, so the effect is anticipated. **Dated re-screen condition:** a *published institutional record* of attention that is not computed from search counts or retail-flow aggregates, and that can flip the sign of an effect rather than size it. |
| **0DTE and options volume share (a structural share measure) (added in Cycle 129, written in Cycle 131)** | **Bounded negative at the design stage (Cycle 129, batch 2 — S-043) — the inverted route's fifth out-of-map kind; zero fetches.** Register-memory check: the kind is **new to the coverage map**; the adjacent families are closed — **row 20** (the retail / same-day-expiry flow and market-maker gamma baseline, which states that the size of the index-level channel is estimated by the exchange to be de minimis and that the single-name question is open under G-05), **row 33** (the structural-share family, which this kind opens), **row 28** (the venue and regulatory trading-constraint family) and **coverage row 19**. Kills: **G-12** — a volume share is a monotone level that sizes an effect, and the published relation is the *composition* of the flow rather than a sign the state determines; **narrowed rule (ii)** — the price consequence (the index-level effect of same-day-expiry flow) is already a published baseline in this register, so the constructed wedge would be a re-presentation of published work; and the **descriptive** kill — the object is a description of a structural change in market composition, not a decision-time state that can condition a flow. **Dated re-screen condition:** a published state that makes the *sign* of the same-day-expiry channel contingent, rather than its size. |

---

## Rediscovery guard

Before adding any candidate, check it against (a) the fifteen dossiers in `idea_register.md`,
(b) the baseline table above, and (c) the validation hand-offs in `validation_handoffs.md` (**eight of the eleven are not executable** — V-001,
V-004, V-005, V-006 and V-007 withdrawn because their candidates were rejected or parked, V-003
marked never-to-execute because IDEA-003 was parked in Cycle 35, **V-010 marked never-to-execute because
IDEA-010 was parked in Cycle 78 (P-014)**, and V-002 frozen pending G-28). If the candidate differs from
a baseline only in parameterisation, in label, or in the time frame, it is a variant and belongs in
this log with the baseline named, not in the register. A candidate that differs from a baseline only
in the *state variable* it is conditioned on must still show that the state variable is observable at
decision time — and must show that the conditioning changes the *sign* or the *existence* of the
effect, not merely its size.

**One standing condition added in Cycle 35 (state integrity), and it is a guard on the register's
own bookkeeping, not on candidates.** *No artifact may be described as written, executed, verified
or complete anywhere in this register until the agent has checked that it is present in the artifact
set, and every count and claim in the handoff must be re-derived from the files rather than carried
forward.* In Cycle 35 an audit found that a prior cycle's source-ledger header and handoff had
asserted a set of artifacts — the sources S-210 … S-218, the "Cycle 33 sources" and "Cycle 33 gap
verdicts" blocks, the candidate IDEA-016, the specification V-011, the bounded negative R-014, the
parkings P-012 and P-013 and a formal G-25 verdict — that had **never existed in any file and appear
in no commit in the repository's history**; a note in that same header asserted that the missing
blocks were "the executed record". The register's candidates were not damaged, but its *claims* were,
and a claim that cannot be checked is worse than no claim. The identifiers **IDEA-016, S-210 … S-219,
V-011, G-26 and P-013 are therefore reserved and unused**: they are left unused on purpose so that a
later reconstruction of that missing work cannot collide with new work. The standing practice that
follows: (i) re-derive the state of the register from the files at the **start** of every cycle,
before generating anything; (ii) where a prior claim turns out to be unwritten, either **execute** it
if it is a research question, or **record it as unwritten** — never reconstruct a document by
guessing; and (iii) a *bounded negative* is a legitimate outcome, and an *unwritten artifact* is not
a result.

**Cycle-37 record under the standing condition.** The Cycle-36 handoff asserted "one new family
screen logged as a bounded negative"; the screen existed in the cycle log but had **no entry, no
identifier and no verdict in this file**. Cycle 37 wrote it as **R-017** — a screen that had genuinely
been executed, so it was written for real rather than reconstructed — and corrected this log's own
stale header counts (which still read "Cycle 120 complete … 10 rejections, 10 parkings, 34-row
known-baseline table" while the file actually held 16 rejections, 12 parkings and 57 baseline rows;
it now reads 18, 12 and 59). A second, larger instance of the same failure was found and repaired in
the same cycle, in the *source ledger* and the *candidate dossier*: Cycle 36's central negative claim
about IDEA-010's state availability ("broker-dealer firm-level regulatory capital ratios are not
publicly available") was recorded without a check against EDGAR, and Cycle 37's check showed it to be
**false** — the state is public (S-231). The lesson generalises: **a negative recorded without an
executed check is a claim, not a result**, and a negative about *data availability* must be tested at
the level at which the data would actually be used — the filing, not the dataset.

**One standing condition added in Cycle 13 (after R-011), and it is the most important entry in
this guard.** *Before a candidate is written, its nearest published test must be re-read in the
post-2010 sample.* The register's rank-1 candidate survived six cycles of screening because its
literature had been read only up to 2023; the 2025 *Journal of Finance* paper (S-146) then showed
that the effect it trades had collapsed to approximately zero and that its stated differentiator
had a published null. The failure was not in the screening logic — G-13 and G-12 were applied
correctly — but in the *age of the evidence base*. Therefore: (i) every candidate must carry a
**dated evidence-currency check**, and (ii) a candidate whose base effect is documented as decayed
(S-146, S-156, S-033) must show that the *conditioned* effect survives where the base has not, or
be parked. The register also now keeps two version-drift warnings on file (S-152, whose published
and working-paper versions carry opposite signs, and S-172, whose title changed between versions):
**a number quoted from a working paper must be cited with its version.**

**Three standing screening conditions added in Cycle 5** (they are now part of the guard, not
advice):

1. **G-12 — the conditioning must change the sign or the existence of the effect.** A published
   effect conditioned on a published state is a baseline re-description unless the conditioning
   flips the sign or creates/destroys the effect. IDEA-007 and IDEA-013 both failed this test in
   different ways, and both are recorded so the failure is not repeated.
2. **G-13 — the state must be published *before* the decision.** An official state published on a
   calendar that runs after the decision it informs (the EU ETS aggregate balance before 2024,
   S-121) supports an explanatory decomposition but never a decision-time rule. Every candidate must
   record, per observation, the state's publication date relative to the decision date.
3. **The literature-closure rule, applied symmetrically.** If a published paper already shows the
   candidate's effect, the candidate is **rejected, not narrowed** — this is the rule IDEA-006 and
   IDEA-012 set for themselves and the rule that closed both of them (R-009, R-010). A residual
   survives only if it is (a) genuinely untested, (b) testable with the data that exist, and
   (c) large enough to survive the cost structure of the venue in which it would be traded.

**Cycle 6 — the two screens are now applied to every candidate, and a fourth condition was added.**

4. **G-13 and G-12 are run as a screen on every active candidate before it is ranked**, and their
   results are recorded per candidate in `idea_register.md` and summarised in the source ledger's
   "Cycle-6 verdicts" block. In Cycle 6 the screen **parked two candidates** (IDEA-005 on G-12,
   IDEA-008 on G-13) and re-scored one (IDEA-010, 68.0 → 65.0). A candidate that fails either
   screen is re-scored or parked; it is not left in the active ranking with the failure noted.
5. **New standing condition — a failed G-13 with a *dated* future publication calendar is a
   parking with an unblocking date, not a rejection** (IDEA-008: the lending-rate state is not
   publicly disseminated until 29 March 2029 — S-126 — so the candidate is parked as P-010 with the
   unblocking condition recorded as gap G-14). The complementary case is recorded too: a G-12
   failure where the *closest published test of the candidate's own state variable is null* is a
   parking, not a rejection, because nothing published shows the candidate's effect (IDEA-005:
   S-099). **A rejection still requires a published result that shows the effect** — that remains
   the rule that produced R-007, R-009 and R-010.
6. **New standing condition — a candidate that differs from a published baseline only by a state
   variable, and whose differentiating state is *commercial*, is retained only with an explicit
   validation precondition** (IDEA-010: the published substitutes for AP balance-sheet stress are
   different constructs and have not been validated against a dealer-balance-sheet measure, gap
   G-16). Re-specifying around an unvalidated proxy is not permitted, and no validation
   specification may be written for such a candidate until the validation is done.


### R-015 — New mechanism family screen (Cycle 34)
- **Screen conducted:** one new mechanism family screening per Cycle 34 priorities (Priority 5).
- **Outcome:** bounded negative. No new candidate met the register's bar (published decision-time state, dated price-insensitive flow, unclaimed sign, no published result showing the effect).
- **Rationale:** examined multiple potential families (e.g., treasury refunding announcements, CDS index roll dates, corporate bond maturity walls, SPAC combination deadlines, FTSE/index rebalancing effects beyond those already covered). None possessed a clear signed pre-decision state that differs meaningfully from existing known baselines. Most were either already covered by existing literature/baselines or failed the "price-insensitive flow with unclaimed sign" criterion.
- **Decision:** log bounded negative (R-015). No new dossier created. No score change.

### R-014 — Crypto token-unlock schedule as a dated, price-insensitive supply flow (Cycle 35 family screen — bounded negative; written for real)
- **Why this entry exists.** The Cycle-33 source-ledger header and the Cycle-34 handoff both asserted
  that a crypto-unlock family screen had been executed and logged as **R-014**. It had not: no entry,
  no verdict and no source existed in any artifact. Cycle 35 executed the screen and wrote the result.
- **Screen conducted.** Token unlock schedules — project, team, foundation and investor allocations
  whose vesting cliffs expire on published dates — as a **dated, pre-announced, price-insensitive supply
  flow** with an unclaimed sign and a state that is published before the event.
- **Outcome: bounded negative. No new candidate; the family is a baseline.**
- **Rationale.**
  1. **The channel is already documented, not unclaimed.** The register's own corpus holds the
     Cycle-13 unlock event study (S-170): across more than 16,000 unlock events, roughly 90 % were
     followed by negative price pressure, prices typically begin declining about 30 days *before* the
     unlock, team unlocks were the most disruptive, and the move stabilises within roughly two weeks
     after. A dated supply whose price effect is already published, in a signed direction, with a
     published pre-event window, is a baseline by the register's rule ("a candidate that merely
     repackages a known idea is not a candidate"). Cycle 35's screen reproduced that verdict and added
     nothing that could un-claim it.
  2. **The state is commercial, not regulated.** Unlock schedules are compiled and distributed by
     third-party trackers, not by the issuer, an exchange or a regulator. The register's data rule
     requires a published, reproducible state from a primary source; a vendor's calendar is a
     commercial dataset whose coverage, revision policy and definitions are not auditable, and the
     register's own standing condition (added in Cycle 30 for IDEA-010) is that a candidate whose
     differentiating state is *commercial* is retained only with an explicit validation precondition
     and may not be re-specified around an unvalidated proxy.
  3. **The obligor population is anonymous and the flow cannot be sized in advance.** The recipients
     of an unlock are wallet addresses, and their intent to sell is not observable; no pre-event
     measure of "scheduled supply about to be absorbed" can be constructed that is not a restatement of
     the schedule itself.
  4. **Venue risk is inherited and unresolved.** The register's surviving crypto candidate
     (IDEA-009 PERP-FUND, 60.0) already carries an unresolved crypto-venue risk condition; a second
     crypto family would inherit that operational risk without adding a signed, published state.
- **Decision:** bounded negative (R-014). The token-unlock channel is recorded in the known-baseline
  set; no dossier was created; no IDEA identifier was consumed; no score changed.

### R-016 — The EU CBAM certificate purchase obligation as a dated, mandatory compliance flow (Cycle 35 family screen — bounded negative)
- **Screen conducted.** The EU Carbon Border Adjustment Mechanism's **mandatory certificate purchase
  obligation** — importers of CBAM goods (iron and steel, aluminium, cement, fertilisers, electricity
  and hydrogen) must hold certificates for a share of embedded emissions at each quarter end from 2027
  and must surrender them by 30 September each year, the annual deadline having been moved once, from
  31 May (Regulation (EU) 2025/2083, in force 20 October 2025) — as a dated, mandatory, price-insensitive
  flow with a published pre-deadline signed state. The family was screened because it is the closest
  thing the register has not yet covered to the register's own **compliance-deadline concession**
  template, and because IDEA-013 (EU ETS) and IDEA-014 (RIN) are both parked or retired — so the
  template needed a *third* market with a *different regulator* (the European Commission) and a
  *different obligor population* (importers rather than fuel producers) before the template could be
  declared exhausted.
- **Outcome: bounded negative. No new candidate; the family is a baseline with a dated re-screen
  condition.**
- **Rationale — five independent reasons, any one of which is sufficient.**
  1. **The flow is not into a tradable market and the concession is not harvestable.** The certificate
     price is a *published deterministic formula* — the weighted average of EUA auction clearing
     prices (S-227) — and the certificate is sold only by the Commission to authorised declarants on
     the common central platform and can be used only to discharge the CBAM obligation. A third party
     cannot buy the certificate, so no participant can capture a concession; and the declarant
     receives a certificate, not an allowance, so certificate sales do not consume EUAs and create no
     EUA demand.
  2. **The would-be sign-determining "state" is a definition, not a state.** Because the certificate
     price is a *lagged average* of EUA auction prices, any gap between the certificate price and the
     spot EUA price is an artefact of the formula. Declarants can time purchases within the window
     (weekly prices from 2027, sales from 1 February 2027), so the gap is arbitraged by the obligor
     rather than being a price. There is no state that decides the sign of a harvestable effect.
  3. **The obligation is near zero by construction in the near term.** The share of embedded emissions
     that generates a certificate obligation is the complement of the free-allocation factor: **2.5 % in
     2026**, 5 % in 2027, 10 % in 2028, 22.5 % in 2029, 48.5 % in 2030 and 100 % only from 2034
     (S-229; the Directive's "CBAM factor" is the *retention* factor — 97.5 % in 2026 — and the
     obligation share is its complement; the two conventions must not be confused). This is the same
     "near zero by construction" structure the register already closed against IDEA-014's RIN state
     (G-20), so the near-term flow is immaterial against EU allowance turnover.
  4. **The constraint is real but captive.** An authorised declarant that fails to hold the required
     certificates risks loss of its authorised-declarant status for serious or repeated infringement
     (S-230), so the family does pass the register's "participant and constraint" test. The party that
     would capture any concession is that obligor, not a participant the register could act as.
  5. **The macro channel is an already-published baseline.** CBAM's effect on EU carbon prices, on
     trade and on competitiveness is a large and growing published and policy literature, including the
     Commission's own materials and the European Parliament's briefing at S-229. A candidate claiming
     "CBAM moves EUA prices" would be presenting a published baseline.
- **Dated re-screen condition (so the negative is not permanent).** Re-screen the family when **both**
  hold: (i) the obligation share has grown materially — the 2029–2030 step to 48.5 % of embedded
  emissions (S-229); and (ii) the weekly pricing regime has been running long enough for the
  **pre-deadline hedging behaviour of authorised declarants in the EUA market** to be observable, i.e.
  published evidence of *how* declarants hedge a certificate obligation priced off EUAs and with what
  timing. That residue — obligor behaviour, not a regulatory-calendar fact — is the only part of the
  family that could carry a sign.
- **Decision:** bounded negative (R-016). The CBAM certificate family is recorded in the
  known-baseline set with the dated re-screen condition above; no dossier was created; no IDEA
  identifier was consumed; no score changed.

### R-017 — Ex-dividend date mechanics as a dated, mechanical supply event (Cycle 36 family screen — bounded negative; written for real in Cycle 37)
- **Why this entry exists.** Cycle 36 ran a family screen on **ex-dividend date mechanics** and recorded
  a bounded negative in the cycle log and in the Cycle-36 handoff ("one new family screen logged as a
  bounded negative"). No entry, no identifier and no verdict were ever written into this file, so the
  screen existed only as prose. That is the state-integrity failure this log's standing condition was
  written to stop: a negative that cannot be found cannot prevent rediscovery. Cycle 37 assigns the
  chronologically next free identifier, **R-017**, and writes the entry for real.
- **Screen conducted.** The **ex-dividend date** as a dated, pre-announced, price-insensitive supply
  event: on a published ex-date the entitlement to the dividend separates from the share, the
  reference price adjusts, and any index member or exchange-traded fund obliged to track that index
  must trade the adjustment through on the date.
- **Outcome: bounded negative. No new candidate; the family is a baseline.**
- **Rationale.**
  1. **The event is an accounting identity, not a concession.** The ex-dividend price adjustment is
     mechanical: the entitlement transfers from the seller to the holder on a date whose every element
     is published in advance. A trade that buys the adjustment is buying arithmetic; any measured
     effect would be the reversion of a known, published adjustment rather than the compensation of
     an unpriced risk.
  2. **The flow is executed by the party that is obliged to execute it.** The adjusting trade is
     required of the index member or the fund, not offered by a participant the register could act
     as, so there is no residual supply to harvest — the same structural reason that closed the CBAM
     certificate family (R-016) and the EU ETS instance.
  3. **The state is fully published and fully anticipated.** The dividend amount and the ex-date are
     public before the decision, and the rule's published state is not a *signed* state: it says when
     the adjustment happens, not whether the adjusting buyer is unusually eager or unusually
     constrained on that date. No published state distinguishes a tight from a loose ex-date, so
     there is no state-conditioned claim to make.
  4. **The family is already inside the register's baseline set in substance.** The register's
     closing-auction and index-mechanics rows (S-149, S-150, S-148) and the index-inclusion row
     (S-001 … S-004) already record that passive execution and index mechanics are documented
     institutional facts rather than discoveries.
- **Sources.** None were added. This entry asserts no external claim beyond the mechanical character
  of the ex-dividend price adjustment and the structural fact that the adjusting trade is obligatory;
  it is filed as a baseline and a rediscovery guard, and it does not rest on a literature finding.
- **Decision:** bounded negative (R-017). The ex-dividend family is recorded in the known-baseline
  set; no dossier was created; no `IDEA-` identifier was consumed; no score changed.

### R-018 — The Rule 201 short-sale price-test circuit breaker as a regulatory seller-disabling switch (Cycle 37 family screen — bounded negative)
- **Screen conducted.** **SEC Rule 201 of Regulation SHO** — the "alternative uptick rule". The rule is
  a genuinely different template from the compliance-deadline family the register has been
  exhausting: it is **not** an obligation converging on a calendar date. It is a **regulatory switch
  that disables a seller**, triggered in real time by a published and observable threshold (a decline
  of 10 % or more from the prior day's closing price), and it stays in force for a published and
  bounded duration (the remainder of the trigger day and the following trading day), with the
  short-sale price test requiring short orders to be placed above the national best bid (S-235).
- **Outcome: bounded negative. No new candidate; the family is a baseline.**
- **Rationale — four independent reasons, any one of which is sufficient.**
  1. **The effect is published, peer-reviewed and positive — it is the claim a candidate would make.**
     Barardehi, Bird, Karolyi and Ruchti (2025, *Management Science*) test precisely the proposition
     that short-sale restrictions raise prices, using quasi-experimental variation from the −10 %
     threshold: the restriction "leads to 8% lower short-sale volume and 35 bps higher daily
     returns", and "these price effects do not reverse after the restriction is lifted" (S-236).
     A candidate trading the trigger would be trading a published, quantified result. The register's
     G-12 rule requires an **unclaimed** sign; here the sign is claimed, sized and peer-reviewed.
  2. **The constraint is defeasible by the seller it constrains.** After a trigger, short sellers
     migrate to the options market: put option open interest rises 61 % and put trading volume 87 %,
     with call open interest up 40 % and call volume up 80 % (S-237), and the circumvention is
     documented as low-cost (S-238). The mechanism a candidate would need — a binding constraint on
     the marginal seller's ability to press the price — is therefore only partly binding.
  3. **The trigger is an information event, and the identification has to be separated from it.** A
     10 % single-day decline is overwhelmingly news-driven. The published design compares stocks on
     either side of the threshold *in the same hour* (S-236); a tradeable form of the claim — buy
     after the trigger — is exposed to the news that produced the trigger, which dominates the risk
     in exactly those names. The 35 bp figure is a threshold-contrast estimate, not a captureable
     post-trigger return, and the working paper itself observes that the restricted short-selling
     opportunities "are transient in nature".
  4. **The cost floor is severe in the affected universe.** The affected names are those that moved
     10 % in a session: the widest spreads, the largest gaps, the highest borrow costs and the most
     borrow recalls. A 35 bp daily difference does not survive a round trip in that universe. (The
     published result finds *narrower* spreads under the restriction itself, so this objection is
     about entering at the trigger, not about the restriction.)
- **What is *not* the reason.** The state is public, real-time and published in the rule, so G-13
  (decision-time availability) **passes**; and the register's own discipline was applied: the screen
  was run against the literature before any conclusion, and the literature was found to be
  peer-reviewed, positive and directly on point.
- **Dated re-screen condition.** Re-screen only if (i) the SEC materially changes the trigger
  threshold or the scope of the restriction; or (ii) a published study identifies a residual that
  survives the threshold-contrast design — for example an asymmetry around the **expiry** of the
  restriction at the end of the following trading day that is not already covered by S-236's finding
  that the price effects do not reverse after the restriction is lifted.
- **Decision:** bounded negative (R-018). The Rule 201 family is recorded in the known-baseline set;
  no dossier was created; no `IDEA-` identifier was consumed; no score changed.

### R-019 — The Rule 10b-18 buyback repurchase cap (rate-limited price-insensitive demand) (Cycle 38 family screen — bounded negative)

> **Merge note (Cycle 40).** This file carried **two** entries under the identifier R-019 — a short one and a
> longer one, both describing the same screen. They are merged here into a single entry; the longer text is
> retained and the short text's distinctive points are folded into it. One identifier, one entry.

- **The family screened.** Rule 10b-18's safe harbour for issuer repurchases limits the volume of shares an issuer may repurchase in a single day to the greater of 25% of the average daily trading volume for the four calendar weeks preceding the week of the repurchase, or the issuer's average daily volume for the same four-week period — a **daily** capacity cap (not monthly) that makes an issuer's own buyback demand *rate-limited* and deferred across days.
- **Why no candidate.**
  1. **The constrained flow is the issuer's own flow, not a third-party's.** The template the register seeks is a deadline-driven constraint on *another participant* (arbitrageur/liquidity provider) that creates a harvestable concession. Rule 10b-18 constrains the issuer's repurchase volume; in the standard mechanism this is a limit on the issuer supplying liquidity to the market, not a deadline forcing a third party to trade at a concession.
  2. **Remaining capacity is not published with a clean decision-time granularity.** Issuers are not required to publish their remaining 10b-18 capacity intraday or even daily in a central public feed. The 10b-18 repurchase tables appear in periodic reports (10-Q/10-K) and typically disclose total repurchases over periods, not a continuous remaining capacity series. No 10b-18 source existed in the ledger and the state is not a decision-time published state suitable for the register's rule.
  3. **The mechanism is price-sensitive and literature-dense.** Buyback literature studies price impact and signaling; the safe-harbour cap is a legal/volume constraint, not a published deadline switch that disables a participant at a specific time with an unclaimed sign. Existing literature addresses whether buybacks push prices and the signaling channel rather than a rate-limited third-party concession template.
  4. **No unclaimed sign or clean separation.** Establishing that the cap binds on a given day and produces a concession to a third party requires identifying binding episodes from sparse public data and separating from price-sensitive execution. The bar (published decision-time state, dated price-insensitive flow, unclaimed sign) is not met.

- **Verdict: bounded negative — no candidate.** The family is a baseline of rate-limited supply, not the deadline-driven third-party constraint the register screens for. No 10b-18 source added in the main ledger in this cycle; the finding is recorded as a bounded negative. Two further points from the shorter duplicate entry, retained on merge: **no unclaimed sign or clean separation** — establishing that the cap binds on a given day and produces a concession to a third party requires identifying binding episodes from sparse public data and separating them from price-sensitive execution; and the **state is periodic, not decision-time** — issuers are not required to publish remaining 10b-18 capacity intraday or even daily in a central public feed, and the 10b-18 repurchase tables in periodic reports disclose total repurchases over periods rather than a continuous remaining-capacity series.

---

### R-036 — Cross-session price formation: the stale-price window between a closed session and the next open (Cycle 57 family screen — bounded negative)

- **The family screened.** A different family from R-014–R-035: **cross-session price formation** — non-overlapping trading hours, scheduled market holidays, and the stale-price window between a closed session and the next open one. This is the register's first screen of a **cross-session price-formation** family. It is genuinely new in kind: every screen so far (R-012 … R-035) covered an obligation, a switch, a supply schedule, an expiry-driven flow, a price rule, a market interruption, a settlement mechanism or an incentive flow, and the known-baseline table holds no row for this family.
- **Instances screened.** The close-to-open stale-price window across equity sessions (overnight information impounded at the next open); non-overlapping trading hours between venue sessions; and scheduled market holidays that extend the stale window. Literature instances run: Lu, Malliaris & Qin (2023, S-287) on night-minus-day price formation; Camilleri & Green (2014, S-288) on non-synchronous trading; Challet & Gourianov (2018, S-289) on opening and closing auctions absorbing overnight imbalance; and Barclay & Hendershott (2003, S-305) on after-hours price discovery and trading, which finds that after-hours price discovery is concentrated in a small subset of stocks. **Corrected in Cycle 58:** the entry as first written also cited "Hendershott on after-hours price discovery (S-290)"; a verification pass found that S-290 carries no DOI, no stable URL and no index record, and a Crossref bibliographic search returned no matching work, so **S-290 is unverifiable and has been struck from this screen's evidentiary basis**, with its two functions taken over by S-287, S-288 and S-305.
- **Why no candidate.**
  1. **The mechanism is published prior art, and the sign is already claimed.** Cross-session price formation — overnight news impounding into prices at the next open — is a documented, peer-reviewed result (S-287, S-288, S-305). The sign of the close-to-open move is information-driven (it follows overnight news), so a candidate claiming a stale-price concession would be claiming a sign that the literature already explains as an information impoundment, not an unclaimed price concession to a third-party liquidity provider.
  2. **The sign-determining state is not known at decision time.** The overnight news that determines the sign of the next open's price move arrives *between* the close and the open; at the decision time (the close), it is unknown. A trade placed into the stale window is exposed to that unknown news, so the sign cannot be conditioned on at decision time. G-13 fails at the firm/session level.
  3. **The opening auction absorbs the imbalance.** The opening call auction (S-289) is designed to aggregate overnight order imbalances and set the opening price; the venue itself supplies the intermediation at the restart, which is exactly the structural reason there is no residual concession for an outside participant to harvest.
  4. **No unclaimed sign meeting the strict bar.** Published before decision, sign-determining, not a public real-time by-product — not met: the sign is determined by information that arrives after the decision time, and the effect is a published price-formation channel rather than an unclaimed concession.
  5. **The cost floor is hostile.** The stale window is narrow (minutes between close and open, or longer across holidays), spreads widen outside regular hours, and after-hours liquidity is thin (S-305, which finds after-hours price discovery concentrated in a small subset of stocks). The round-trip cost of entering the stale window exceeds any residual stale-price move that is not itself an information impoundment.
- **What is *not* the reason.** The state is arguably public (session times, holiday calendars are published), so G-13's publication test is technically met — but it is simultaneously useless: the sign-determining state (overnight news) is not public at decision time. The screen was run against the literature before any conclusion was reached.
- **Dated re-screen condition.** Re-open this family only if (i) a venue introduces a **scheduled, pre-published cross-session trading mechanism** that creates a decision-time state with a sign determinable before the next open; or (ii) a published study identifies a residual close-to-open price effect that survives the information-impoundment explanation and is sign-determinable before the open. Absent either, this family stays closed.
- **Decision:** bounded negative (R-036). The cross-session price-formation family is recorded in the known-baseline set (row 66); no dossier was created; no `IDEA-` identifier was consumed; no score changed; no validation specification was added.

---

### R-037 — Contractual barrier hedging: the published knock-out level and the issuer's hedging discontinuity (Cycle 58 family screen — bounded negative)

- **The family screened.** The **contractual barrier** in retail structured products: an autocallable, a
  barrier reverse auto, a knock-in / knock-out structured note or an ELS whose **knock-out or knock-in
  level is published** in the term sheet, the Key Information Document or the exchange listing
  documentation, and whose passage through the underlying's price **changes the issuer's or the dealer's
  hedging discontinuously** — the note's payoff switches off or on, the hedge is unwound or re-delta'd,
  and the underlying is bought or sold in a burst. The candidate the screen asks about is the one the
  register's own template wants: a **published, decision-time state** (the barrier level) whose passage
  forces a **discontinuous, price-insensitive hedge adjustment** by a constrained participant, producing a
  harvestable concession in the underlying. This is the register's first screen of a **contractual-barrier
  family**; the known-baseline table held no row for it, and every family closed in R-014–R-036 covered an
  obligation, a switch, a supply schedule, an expiry-driven flow, a price rule, a market interruption, a
  settlement mechanism, a cross-session window or an incentive flow rather than a **contractual payoff
  discontinuity**.
- **Instances screened.** (i) **Down-and-in knock-out** structures (barrier reverse autos, ELS) — the
  issuer is short a payoff that is dormant until the barrier is breached, so the breach forces a large
  *sale* of the underlying; (ii) **up-and-out / auto-call** structures — the issuer's short call-like
  position collapses at the barrier, forcing a *purchase* of the underlying; (iii) the **European** retail
  structured-product market, where the regulator publishes market structure (S-301) and the barrier level
  is published pre-trade in the KID (S-302) and in listing documentation (S-303); (iv) the **Korean**
  retail ELS market, where the same mechanism has been studied directly; (v) the **Chinese** retail
  autocallable ("snowball") market, where the most recent working paper claims the family explicitly
  (S-300); and (vi) the **barrier-option hedging literature** itself, which is where the discontinuity
  was first described and where the industry's remedy was published (S-299, S-307, S-308).
- **Why no candidate.**
  1. **The mechanism, its sign and its magnitude are published prior art — in a peer-reviewed journal, with
     numbers.** S-306 (Auh & Cho, *The Review of Asset Pricing Studies*) documents, from the
     publisher-deposited abstract: "structured equity derivatives can cause significant price pressure of
     the underlying stock upon an event of dramatic payoff change"; "one event causes another: the event
     cascade amplifies the magnitude of the impact"; "a single event accounts for a −6.4 % return on the
     event day, and it increases the probability of a subsequent event by 21.3 %"; and "traders try to
     liquidate ahead of each other". S-307 documents the delta discontinuity itself at the knock-in level
     in retail ELS, and S-299 documents that the product's delta "can become too large near the
     barriers", so that the authors "apply payoff modification and barrier shifting techniques". The
     candidate's causal chain — published contractual barrier → discontinuous issuer hedge adjustment →
     price effect in the underlying — is therefore **not an unclaimed mechanism**; it is a documented one
     with a quantified event-day effect. A candidate built on it would be a *trade specification* on
     published work, which the register's ranking rubric scores as novelty 1–2, not as a discovery.
  2. **The published evidence says the flow is anticipated, which forecloses the register's concession
     template.** S-306's central behavioural finding is *anticipatory liquidation* — participants liquidate
     ahead of each other because the effect is expected — and the cascade finding means the event is
     partly self-fulfilling. S-304 records the dealer-side equivalent: dealers "shift the knock-in level
     model basis lower to reduce the amount of futures they have to buy", i.e. the hedging response is
     arranged *before* the level is reached. S-298 records that the dealer-hedging-of-structured-products
     channel is itself a **public, central-bank-published argument**, and S-304 records that its magnitude
     is publicly **disputed** by the exchange. A level that is published in advance, whose consequence is
     documented, and around which participants demonstrably pre-position is precisely the case the
     register's screens reject (the R-018 pattern: the flow is anticipated before the decision, so there
     is no concession left to harvest *at* the event).
  3. **The discontinuity is a modelling convention that the industry removes by design.** The delta jump
     at a barrier is documented (S-307, S-299, S-308) — but the *standard remedy* is the **barrier shift**
     and payoff modification, applied precisely to smooth that jump: S-308 publishes the correction
     exp(±βσ√Δt) with β ≈ 0.5826 and shows that the discontinuity is worst when the underlying is close to
     the barrier, which is the reason the shift is applied; S-304 records practitioners using the shift
     "to reduce the amount of futures they have to buy"; S-299 records the same remedy for autocallables.
     So the premise "barrier passage changes the issuer's hedging discontinuously" describes a state that
     a risk manager has already engineered away. What survives of it is a *modelled* jump, which is a
     modelling artefact and a hedging-cost problem rather than an observable flow.
  4. **The state that sets the magnitude is not publicly measurable, and where it has been estimated the
     estimate is small, conditional and explicitly non-causal.** S-300 — the paper that claims the family
     most directly — specifies that the monitoring framework needs **outstanding notional × barrier
     concentration × maturity concentration × local dealer gamma**, and states that in the market it
     proposes to extend the framework to, "only one of the four variables is currently measurable from
     public disclosure". Its flow magnitudes are **hypothetical** ("hypothetical one-way flows of RMB 5–10bn
     per 100 index points", "small relative to stress-window turnover"), and only a concentrated near-dated
     cohort would be "microstructurally meaningful if executed in a short window"; its event-window
     results are stated by the author to be "consistency results, not causal identification". S-301 shows
     what the regulator *does* publish — per-product barrier levels, offer dates, strike dates, expiry
     dates and **volume issued**, aggregated by country and payoff type — and **not** outstanding notional
     or barrier-level concentration; and it records that auto-callable sales volumes have **declined
     sharply** in Germany after 2011 and that the Italian market saw "a collapse in sales volumes overall".
     S-302 shows that the KID publishes the barrier and the performance scenarios pre-trade, but says
     nothing about the issuer's hedge; S-303 shows the listing document publishes the barrier level and
