models, and **a narrow investor base**" (S-354); the investor base itself is narrow and specialised (S-353).
The published information asymmetry compounds the cost floor: "For investors, indemnity triggers can be
**relatively opaque**, since they do not have direct access to information on the sponsor's insurance
portfolio. This raises a moral hazard concern" (S-353). A premium of the published size, in a market of
this depth, with this opacity, does not clear a cost-and-information floor.

**Dated re-screen condition.** Re-screen only if (a) a **published, decision-time** state is shown to
determine the **sign or the existence** (not merely the magnitude) of a CAT-bond return after costs, and that
state is neither a lagged publication nor a private model output; (b) the **published premium account** is
answered rather than assumed away (the "2 to 4 times the expected loss" figure and the benchmark-relative
question, S-354); (c) a **settlement architecture** with a materially shorter, published and observable
determination of the trigger is documented, so that the sign-determining condition becomes knowable before
the decision (against the 18-month development period of S-355 and the indemnity process of S-353); and (d) a
**liquidity and cost record** is obtained from a document that can be fetched and text-extracted — the
Cycle-91 route attempts for the venue-level and market-volume documents failed and are recorded as honest
negatives, so the market's depth and cost remain **documented but not measured** here.

**Decision:** **bounded negative (R-051).** The catastrophe-bond / ILS family is recorded in the
known-baseline table (**row 88**); no dossier was created; no `IDEA-` identifier was consumed; **three `S-`
identifiers were consumed (S-353 … S-355)** in this re-issue, none in Cycle 90; no score changed; no
validation specification was added; and **no new gap is opened**, because the reasons are independent of
data availability — the family failed on the *already-published-premium* limb (reason 1), the
*retrospective-state* limb (reason 2), the *unresolvable-sign-condition* limb (reason 3), the
*model-dependence* limb (reason 4) and the *cost-and-shallow-market* limb (reason 5). The screen's product
is a **rediscovery guard** (row 88) and a registered precedent that the **premium-and-structure** family
shape can be screened to the same standard as the others.

## R-052 — Freight-derivative contract pricing: the dry-bulk FFA forward curve and the container-freight futures family (Cycle 91 family screen — bounded negative)

**What was screened.** The register's **sixth mechanism-first family screen** (after R-047 in Cycle 75,
R-048 in Cycle 80, R-049 in Cycle 87, R-050 in Cycle 88 and R-051 in Cycle 90): the
**freight-derivative contract-pricing family** — the published forward curve of the Baltic Exchange's **dry-bulk Forward Freight Agreements**
(Capesize, Panamax, Supramax and Handysize time-charter and voyage routes), the **container-freight
futures listed on the Shanghai International Energy Exchange** (the SCFIS (Europe service) contract), and
the **published container spot indices** (the SSE's SCFI/SCFIS family) against which those contracts settle.
The family's shape is a **risk premium embedded in a forward price**: a charterer or shipper who is long
physical freight exposure pays a forward rate above the expected future spot to the counterparty who takes
the risk, and the forward curve is also the market's information-bearing price for future physical
conditions.

**Why the family was worth screening now.** The family is **absent from the known-baseline table** and from
every earlier screen (the state-first templates A–I, R-039 … R-046, and the mechanism-first family screens
R-047 … R-051): no row
covers freight, shipping, FFA, Baltic assessments or container-freight futures, and the whole file
contains no prior mention of the family outside the register's own new material. It was named in the
Cycle-89 and Cycle-90 handoffs among the absent families, and it is a **neighbour rather than a
re-presentation** of what the register already holds: the commodity rows concern exchange-traded futures
and their inventory states, while this family's distinguishing mechanism is that the **forward contract
itself is the price of a non-storable service**, is **brokered over the counter**, and settles against a
**published assessment** rather than a deliverable. The screen ran against a **pre-registered decision rule**
with five independent grounds, and was **stopped** as soon as five grounds were established rather than
padded out.

**Sources.** **S-350** (Moutzouris & Nomikos, 2019, *Journal of Futures Markets* 39(8): 1008–1031, DOI
10.1002/fut.21980 — **line-verified from the authors' accepted version** in the City Research Online
repository; the publisher's version is paywalled and was **not** obtained); **S-351** (Nomikos & Doctor,
2013, *Transportation Research Part E* 52: 77–93, DOI 10.1016/j.tre.2012.11.009 — **line-verified from the
authors' accepted version** in the same repository; the publisher's version is paywalled and was **not**
obtained); **S-352** (Shanghai International Energy Exchange, "Containerized Freight Index (Europe service)
Futures Trading Handbook, 2023 Edition" — **line-verified from the venue's own handbook**). **Three
neighbours were sought and not obtained**, and are **not** used as a verification basis for any reason
below: **Ko & Chang (2024)**, *Maritime Policy & Management* 51(5): 717–744 (the "Physical-Paper
Hypothesis" paper; every route returned **HTTP 403**), **Kavussanos, Visvikis & Menachof (2004)**, *Review of
Derivatives Research* 7(3): 241–266 (the unbiasedness paper; the host returned a JavaScript challenge), and
**Alizadeh, Kappou, Tsouknidis & Visvikis (2015)**, *Transportation Research Part E* 76: 58–75 (the
liquidity-premium paper; the publisher's body is paywalled and the working-paper route returned **HTTP
404**). Their bibliographic records were verified against Crossref, and each failure is recorded as an
honest negative in the source ledger.

> **Cycle-93 provenance annotation (record unchanged; verification upgrade and one bibliographic correction).**
> The Cycle-92 handoff named a bounded retrieval of the three sought neighbours above. It was executed in
> Cycle 93 and its result is recorded here **without changing this record's verdict, baseline row or
> re-screen condition**:
> (a) **Alizadeh, Kappou, Tsouknidis & Visvikis (2015) is no longer a neighbour — it is now line-verified and
> registered as S-364.** The authors' accepted version was obtained from the University of Reading's
> institutional repository (CentAUR) and text-extracted, and the paper's published liquidity-premium result
> is now quotable: "Results indicate that liquidity risk is priced and both liquidity measures have a
> significant role in determining freight derivatives returns", with "the Amihud trading volume-related
> liquidity measure and the bid-ask spread measure are both found to be positive and statistically
> significant in explaining returns on FFA contracts". **Re-screen condition (c) is therefore partially
> satisfied**: a cost and liquidity record now exists as a line-verified document for the dry-bulk FFA leg,
> with a documented 2008–2014 sample, proprietary Clarksons bid-ask data and a Baltic-Exchange Amihud
> measure. **This does not re-open the family** — reason 3 remains a negative for the reasons given below,
> because the record now *confirms* rather than removes the cost floor.
> (b) **Ko & Chang (2024) remains an honest negative** (T&F **HTTP 403**; the SSRN accepted version recorded
> by Unpaywall also **HTTP 403**), and its **bibliographic label is corrected**: Crossref verifies the paper
> at 51(5): 717–744 as Byoung-Wook Ko and Young-Tae Chang, "Analysis and forecasting of the dry bulk shipping
> market: structural VAR models using FFA-spot-time charter rates" (DOI 10.1080/03088839.2024.2348692), and
> the SSRN accepted version carries the same title. The Cycle-91 description of this paper as the
> "Physical-Paper Hypothesis" paper is **not corroborated by the publisher's bibliographic record** and is
> **no longer relied on** by the register.
> (c) **Kavussanos, Visvikis & Menachof (2004) remains an honest negative** (the Springer host returns its
> client-challenge interstitial; `cris.city.ac.uk` does not resolve from this environment; SSRN **HTTP 403**;
> CiteSeerX **HTTP 404**; Unpaywall reports the article as closed with no open-access location).

**Reason 1 — the family's headline is published, and so is its decomposition.** The formation of the FFA
rate is a published research question with a published answer: "We illustrate that **the bulk of basis
volatility can be attributed to expectations about future physical market conditions rather than
expectations about future risk premia**", and the residual is equally published — "**there exists
significant predictability of risk premia by both price-based signals and economic variables**", explained
by a framework in which "the average investor should hold “near-rational” but slightly contrarian beliefs"
(S-350). The premium's structure, its published stylised facts ("the 1- and 2-month risk premia are
positively autocorrelated and their autocorrelation increases with contract maturity which indicates the
existence of a momentum effect"; "risk premia and spot growth rates are negatively correlated") and its
published candidate explanations (roll yield, risk premium, and a "liquidity premium" demanded by short
physical hedgers) are all in the literature (S-350). A candidate in this family would re-present a
published effect with a published decomposition — a baseline, not a discovery.

**Reason 2 — the trade itself is published, with published results *and* published caveats.** The first
rigorous study of trading rules in this market is published: "**We find that in spite of robustness checks,
trading rules do outperform the buy-and-hold benchmark in general**", and the conclusion states that
"certain technical trading rules **consistently outperform the benchmark in the FFA markets even after
accounting for transaction costs and order delays induced by thin trading**" (S-351). A candidate here must
therefore answer a *published result*, not an absence of one. Worse for novelty, the published authors name
the caveats themselves: "We also explore the possibility that **illiquidity and a small sample size may
impact the results** of the tests and therefore offer an intuitive approach to mitigate their effects"
(S-351) — a candidate must beat a published result whose own stated weaknesses are illiquidity, thin
trading, order delays and sample size.

**Reason 3 — the cost floor and the market's own structure bind, and the exchange-traded leg carries a
documented margin ladder.** The instrument is a **brokered, over-the-counter** contract: "**Trades are
primarily carried out through brokers in the OTC market and are therefore subject to 'friction' in terms of
the speed of execution, liquidity and transaction costs, which may significantly affect profitability**"
(S-351), and it is a "**cash-settled** forward contract" of the kind that "trade[s] the value of expected
freight rates, the underlying asset being one of the freight indices published by the Baltic Exchange"
(S-351) — so the
"price" a rule must beat is a **brokered quote against a published assessment**, not an exchange order book.
The exchange-traded container leg is documented by the venue itself and is a *different* instrument with its
own published costs: the contract multiplier is "**50 Yuan per index point**", the "**Daily Price Limit**" is
"**Within ±10% of the settlement price of the preceding trading day**", the "**Minimum Trading Margin**" is
"**12% of contract value**" as of listing, rising to "**20%**" from the seventh trading day before the
last trading day and "**30%**" from the second trading day before it, settlement is by **cash settlement**,
and only **six contract months are listed per year** (S-352).

**Reason 4 — the decision-time state is a published price that already carries the information, and the
published evidence says the premium's conditioner is public.** The forward curve is published, and the
published decomposition says it is driven by expectations about future *physical* market conditions and that
the residual risk premia are predictable from **price-based signals and economic variables** (S-350) — i.e.
the conditioner of what survives is already in the literature, and the *published* spot, settlement and FFA
series are monthly observations from January 2007 to September 2016 (S-350). What a candidate could still
claim is a **magnitude** inside a published effect, not a sign; and the state that would have to be observed
at decision time to claim more is either already inside the published forward price or is a **lagged**
publication — the published volume state comes from clearing-house data (S-350), not from a real-time
feed. The register's standing test (G-12, the *sign or the existence* limb) is not met.

**Reason 5 — the effective sample and the regime dependence are adverse, and the published result is
explicitly a "younger market" result.** The published evidence base is **monthly** and ends in 2016
(January 2007 – September 2016, S-350), and the 2013 study's own robustness check removes "**the market
crash period … from the 1 April, 2008 to 24 December, 2008**" from the short-only scenario, after which
"the p-values are larger than before and as such **not sensitive to this turbulent period**" (S-351). The
published authors also characterise the market's profitability as a stage-specific property: the
inefficiencies "**may indeed yield economically significant results in the interim period as markets mature
and trading opportunities [are arbitraged away]**" (S-351). A candidate in this family would be claiming a
magnitude in a regime whose trigger is a violent freight cycle, inside a market whose published edge the
published authors themselves expect to decay as it matures.

**Dated re-screen condition.** Re-screen only if (a) a **published, decision-time** state is shown to
determine the **sign or the existence** (not merely the magnitude) of a freight-derivative return after
costs, and that state is neither already embedded in the published forward curve nor a lagged publication;
(b) the **published trading-rule result** (S-351) and the **published premium decomposition** (S-350) are
**answered rather than assumed away**, with the answer not resting on the same published sample; (c) a
**cost and liquidity record** is obtained from documents that can be fetched and text-extracted — the
Cycle-91 attempts on the venue's own FFA pages, on the exchange's live market data and on the
liquidity-premium paper all failed and are recorded as honest negatives, so the family's cost structure
remains **documented but not measured** here; and (d) the **current contract specifications, settlement
basis and listing status** of the exchange-traded leg are re-verified from a document that can be fetched
and text-extracted — S-352 is the **2023 edition**, so a later edition governs in the event of any
difference.

**Decision:** **bounded negative (R-052).** The freight-derivative contract-pricing family is recorded in
the known-baseline table (**row 89**); no dossier was created; no `IDEA-` identifier was consumed; **three
`S-` identifiers were consumed (S-350 … S-352)**; no score changed; no validation specification was added;
and **no new gap is opened**, because the reasons are independent of data availability — the family failed on
the *already-published-headline* limb (reason 1), the *published-trade* limb (reason 2), the *cost-floor-and-
structure* limb (reason 3), the *sign-and-existence* limb (reason 4) and the *sample-and-regime* limb
(reason 5). The screen's product is a **rediscovery guard** (row 89) and a fifth registered precedent that
the *mechanism-first* operation can be run on a genuinely new family; it is also the register's first
screen of a family in which the **forward contract is the price of a non-storable service** and the
"quote" is brokered rather than exchanged.


### R-058 — IASB list of entities permitted to apply IFRS early in 2005 (Cycle 110, Priority 1) — bounded negative

- **Question:** Was the IASB's published list of entities permitted to apply IFRS early in 2005 available prospectively (fixing the firm-level "repeat adopter" flag)?
- **Routes tried:** web search (queries "IASB published list entities permitted apply IFRS early 2005", "IASB early adopters 2005 list", "IFRS 1 early adoption list 2005"); inspection of IFRS Foundation historical publications (2003 Annual Report, 2005 Annual Report), IOSCO 2005 statements, IFRS 1 standard page/history; Wayback/archive checks for IASB pages from 2004-2005.
- **Finding:** No published firm-level early-adopter registry surfaced. The framework-level first-adoption facts are published (as established in Cycle 109 via jurisdiction profile PDFs), but the prospective firm-level list for 2005 could not be located.
- **Conclusion:** The firm-level "repeat adopter" flag for the 2005 event remains derivable from each firm's published prior-year basis-of-preparation note, not published prospectively. This closes the P-3 screen's named residual; V-009's comparator does not gain a published firm-level list, so classification remains derivation-based for that element.

---

## R-062 — US commodity futures' published regulatory and positioning states: (a) the speculative-position-limit and accountability state, and (b) the weekly Commitments of Traders (Cycle 113 pre-registered Priority-1 screen, two sub-families, one identifier — bounded negative on both)

**Framing correction made before the screen ran, and recorded here rather than silently adopted.** The Cycle-112 handoff stated that the two Priority-1 families "neither of which appears in the known-baseline table and neither of which has ever been screened". **That is false for family (a) and only half-true for family (b).** Family (a) is a **licensed re-screen** of the register's own closed templates — **R-042** (template E, "published reserve/availability/headroom state at decision date", whose family list names "position-limit headroom"; baseline row 71) and **R-043** (template F, "published constraint breach / non-compliance events at decision date", whose family list names "position-limit breaches"; baseline row 72), with the waiver and change-of-law variants closed as **R-044** (row 73) and **R-045** (row 74). Family (b) is **already a registered source** (S-030, S-031) and **already the conditioning state of a parked candidate**, **IDEA-005 ROLLCOND (P-009)**, whose state is "the passive's own position in the expiring contract (CFTC 'Index Traders' positions, weekly, positions as of Tuesday, published Friday)". The screen's first question was therefore not "is this family new?" but **"does this instance satisfy the dated re-screen condition, and if so does it pass the strict bar?"** — a *weaker* question than the handoff's, and the one actually run.

### Sub-family (a) — the published speculative-position-limit and accountability state

**Instance screened.** 17 CFR Part 150 (federal speculative position limits, exchange-set limits, position accountability), the exchanges' published limit grids and market-regulation notices, and the CFTC's own published statements of the regime — the object being a *forced* reduction of the marginal holder when a published cap binds, and the concession that reduction concedes.

**Reasons (independent):**

1. **There is no published headroom state and no published breach event — the template has no instance in this family.** Part 150 constrains "no person" and publishes only the **limit**; the position that would exhaust the limit is the participant's own and is not published (S-398, § 150.2). What the regulator does publish is the limit levels, the 10 %/2.5 % open-interest construction, the pre-announced step-downs and a § 150.5 monthly exchange report that is a **cash-market** report for hedging purposes (S-399). An exceedance of an accountability level produces a **request** for information and consent, not a published breach (S-398, § 150.1), so R-043's "published breach event" is absent by construction, and R-042's "published headroom" is absent with it.
2. **The threshold is stale by construction, so the state fails G-13 as a decision-time threshold.** Spot-month limits are "no greater than 25 percent of the estimated spot month deliverable supply" on an estimate the exchange supplies to the Commission "**upon request**" and is not required to publish (S-398, § 150.5(b)(1)(i) and § 150.3(f)); non-spot-month levels are anchored to "the average combined futures and delta-adjusted option month-end open interest in the contract for the **most recent calendar year**" (S-398, Appendix F(a)(1)(i) and (iv)).
3. **The reduction is not forced, and its timing is administrative and negotiated.** "Position accountability" requires the holder to "**consent** to … Halt increasing further its position or reduce its position in an orderly manner, **in each case as requested by** the designated contract market" (S-398, § 150.1); a denied exemption obliges compliance "**within a commercially reasonable time, as determined by the Commission in consultation with the applicant and the applicable designated contract market**", and no enforcement action is pursued during the review (S-398, § 150.3(b)(3)(ii)(B)–(C)); the exchange rulebook reads the same way — an accountability level may be exceeded without a violation and a reduction follows the exchange's order after private contact (S-402). The mechanism the family needs — a *forced* reduction at a *published* time — does not exist in the law.
4. **The binding is anticipated.** The step-down schedules are published in advance (S-399: Light Sweet Crude Oil at 6,000 / 5,000 / 4,000 contracts on the three, two and one business days before the last trading day; Live Cattle at 600 / 300 / 200), the general compliance date was published years ahead (1 January 2022), and the anti-evasion provision (§ 150.3(i)) confirms the regulator's own expectation that the limit will be worked around rather than that it will be a surprise. A dated, pre-announced reduction is the archetype of an anticipated event.
5. **The sign is already claimed, and the published claim is a null.** S-401 quotes the CFTC economist memorandum of 21 August 2009: "In our analysis of the impact of position limits, we find **little evidence to suggest that changes from a position limit regime to an accountability level regime or changes in the levels of position limits impact price volatility** in either energy or agricultural markets. Our results are consistent with those found in the existing literature on position limits." The surrounding published record is contested rather than open (the same paper records Jacks's volatility evidence and Irwin and Sanders's finding that "index positions have led to lower volatility in a statistical sense"), so no unclaimed sign survives.
6. **The closest published test is of a superseded regime, which weakens rather than strengthens the family.** The memorandum is from 2009; the federal limits it analyses were vacated in September 2012 and replaced by the 2020 final rule at **86 FR 3236** (S-400, line-verified), codified at S-398. The register could not locate a published test of the *current* regime that leaves a sign-determining effect unclaimed, and the absence of one is not an opening — it is the same already-claimed limb.
7. **Fails G-12 (sign/existence).** Nothing located shows a published limit threshold that flips the *sign* or the *existence* of an effect. Conditioning on a published limit changes sizing, risk parameters and the *magnitude* of a position's footprint, not whether an effect exists, and the published results the register holds are magnitude results.
8. **The effective sample is the number of constrained positions, and they are not observable.** Federal limits reach 25 core referenced futures contracts (S-399), the constrained set excludes the enumerated bona fide hedges and the spread transactions (S-398, Appendix A), and the marginal holder's headroom is private. The register treats a small effective sample as binding under its state-first discipline.
9. **The concession is a published baseline, and the cost floor is undocumented above it.** A forced unwind's concession is a **liquidation concession**, whose sign is claimed in the margin/funding-liquidity literature the register already holds (**S-009**, Brunnermeier & Pedersen), and the register's cost references (an 8 bps and a 13 bps round trip, S-389 and S-392) show that frictions of that order dominate an effect whose magnitude no source measures.

### Sub-family (b) — the CFTC's weekly Commitments of Traders as a decision-time positioning state

**Reasons (independent):**

1. **The variant test fails first, and it is decisive.** The COT is a **registered source** (S-030, S-031) and is already the conditioning state of the **parked** candidate **IDEA-005 (P-009)**. The register's rediscovery guard: "If the candidate differs from a baseline only in parameterisation, in label, or in the time frame, it is a variant and belongs in this log with the baseline named, not in the register." A COT-based candidate that differs from IDEA-005 only in the trader category read, the look-back or the horizon is exactly that.
2. **G-13 fails on cadence and lag.** The data are Tuesday's and are "released Friday" after "**three days** to process the data", generally at "**Friday at 3:30 pm Eastern Time**", and "Because of resource constraints, we are currently only able to release this report **once a week**" (S-403, line-verified). A state that is three calendar days stale — four over a weekend — cannot support a daily or intraday claim, and every family in this screen is a daily-horizon family.
3. **The state's coverage is endogenous to the very thing it would measure.** A contract "**no longer appears in the COT report**" when "the number of reportable Large Traders drops below **20**" (S-403). The state therefore exists only where participation is large, so the series' own existence is a function of the hypothesised state, and a cross-contract comparison is a comparison across different participation regimes. This is a G-13 and G-12 failure in one: the state is not a stable measurement of the thing it claims to measure.
4. **The state is a coarse, self-reported, non-revisable aggregate, so a change in it need not be a change in positioning.** Classifications are "based on the predominant business purpose **self-reported** by traders on the CFTC Form 40"; "CFTC staff does not know specific reasons for traders' positions"; "Due to legal restraints (CEA Section 8 data and confidential business practices), the CFTC **does not publish** information on how individual traders are classified"; "**No**, historical data is not updated once published"; and the regulator itself lists the three mechanisms by which a week-to-week change can arise — a re-filed Form 40 changing a trader's classification, a new trader's positions altering the prior week's numbers, and an existing trader's exit (S-403).
5. **Fails G-12, and the sign is claimed.** The register already holds the crowding and positioning literature (S-026, S-157, S-222, S-280), and the published COT-prediction literature claims the sign of the managed-money / commercial / index-trader relation in both directions. The family is a published baseline, not a discovery.
6. **The cost floor and the horizon mismatch bind last.** A weekly-cadence state read for a daily-horizon claim, with a weekly rebalance, leaves no room above the register's cost references for an effect of the magnitude any source claims.

**Routes tried.** (1) **17 CFR Part 150** through the eCFR versioner XML API for 2025-01-01, 2026-01-01, 2026-03-01, 2026-06-01, 2026-09-01 and 2026-10-01 and through the plain eCFR page (S-398; the plain page returned navigation chrome only and was not used). (2) The CFTC's own **"Position Limits for Derivatives"** page (S-399). (3) The **Federal Register API** for the CFTC's 2020 final rule and its metadata (S-400). (4) The CFTC's **Commitments of Traders** programme page and the Division of Market Oversight's FAQs (S-403). (5) The exchange's own **accountability rulebook and market-regulation advisory notice** (S-402). (6) The **EDHEC/SSRN** record and the author's PDF for the closest published position-limit test (S-401). (7) Literature routes, located through the search index on 21 October 2026 and **not registered, not fetched, and no claim in this entry rests on any of them**: the CFTC's 2018 study of position limits and speculative position limits and the 2020 economic analyses of position limits and of speculative position limits; the CFTC Office of the Chief Economist's review of the speculative-position-limit research and its "inconclusive" characterisation; Chen, Hu and Wang (SSRN 4291752) on position limits as a coordination device; Irwin and Sanders (2023) on position limits and firm-level trading; Brunnetti on speculation and price stability; Janardanan, Qiao and Rouwenhorst (2017) on price limits; the CFTC's interim staff report on the 20 April 2020 WTI price collapse; and, for sub-family (b), the published COT-prediction literature (Sanders, Irwin and Merrin 2009; Sanders, Boris and Manfredo 2004; Mayer 2025; Clark and Ng; Sapkota, Mohanty and Ordu-Akkaya 2023; Adhikari and Putnam 2025; Ganepola and Ordu-Akkaya 2025; Tebache and Saida 2025; the Federal Reserve's FEDS 2025-082). **The route list is recorded so that a later session does not repeat it, and so that it is honest about what was verified and what was not.**

**Dated re-screen condition.** **Family (a):** re-screen only if (a) the CFTC or an exchange begins **publishing** a decision-time series — participant-level positions against the applicable limit, or an exchange's own timestamped exceedance notice — at a documented cadence and coverage, published before the decision; (b) the reduction becomes **mandatory and dated** rather than consensual and "as requested", i.e. a published ex-ante reduction deadline with a published consequence; (c) a published test exists in the **current** regime (the 2020 final rule, 86 FR 3236) rather than the vacated pre-2020 regime, and leaves a sign-determining effect unclaimed; and (d) the concession's magnitude is documented against a cost floor. **Family (b):** re-screen only if (a) the COT's publication lag is shortened, or a sub-weekly positioning state is published by an exchange or the CFTC; (b) a **coverage-stable** series is published, so that the state does not vanish when participation falls below the reporting threshold; and (c) a published test exists in the **within-contract** form that the register's held literature does not already supply, with a sign that no held source already claims.

**Decision:** **bounded negative (R-062).** The two families are recorded in the known-baseline table (**rows 95 and 96**); no dossier was created; no `IDEA-` identifier was consumed; **eleven `S-` identifiers were consumed (S-398 … S-408)**; no score changed; no validation specification was added; and **no new gap is opened**, because the reasons are independent of data availability — family (a) failed on the *law itself* (reason 3), the *published-state* limb (reason 1), the *already-claimed* limb (reason 5) and the *sign-and-existence* limb (reason 7), and family (b) failed on the *variant* limb (reason 1), the *cadence-and-lag* limb (reason 2) and the *sign-and-existence* limb (reason 5). The screen's product is a **rediscovery guard** (rows 95 and 96) and a registered precedent that a **licensed re-screen** of a closed template must be reported as *weaker* than a first-time screen, and that the strict bar must be applied to the *law* before it is applied to the literature.

## R-063 — Import-licence rationing and the US sugar tariff-rate quota: a dated price step attached to an accounting-observable exhaustion moment (Cycle 114 construction-first method test, five pre-registered routes — bounded negative, and the register's answer to its own open question 5)

**Why this entry is different from every other entry in this log.** Every other rejection in this file is a **family screen**: the register starts from a published state *kind* and asks whether some instance of it can be turned into a candidate. This entry is the register's **first construction-first method test** — the Cycle-113 handoff's open question 5 asked whether *constructing a mechanism first, then screening it* is a better unit of work than screening state families, and this cycle ran that method against a pre-registered construction. The construction was written **before any fetch**, from the register's own memory, by a different route than the register normally uses; the screen then killed it. **The rejection is therefore a two-part product: a bounded negative on the constructed mechanism, and a measurement of the method itself.**

**The construction screened, as pre-registered in `cycle_notes.md` (Cycle 114, Phase 0) before the first fetch.** A family: **import-licence rationing**, in which a published and ex-ante-known **price step** (a codified two-tier tariff — a low in-quota rate and a high over-quota rate) attaches to a **quantity limit** whose **exhaustion date is not published ex ante** but whose fill is published as a **running accounting residual** in a weekly government ledger. The claimed trade: the **domestic–world spread** in the rationed commodity widens abruptly at the published exhaustion moment, because the over-quota price step begins to bind. The **state** is the quota's *entries to date*; the **flow** is imports; the **sign claim** is a one-sided widening at the exhaustion week; the **three predictions** were P1 (a step of a large fraction of the codified tariff differential at the exhaustion moment), P2 (the widening is conditional on exhaustion — it does not occur in years when the quota does not exhaust) and P3 (a cost-floor argument). The **named instance** was the US sugar tariff-rate quota: the **WTO raw cane sugar TRQ**, the in-quota aggregate quantity published by USDA's Foreign Agricultural Service, the fill published weekly by US Customs and Border Protection, and the high-tier duty published in the US Harmonized Tariff Schedule.

**Why this instance, and what the register's own memory did and did not contain.** A register-memory check of the five artifacts returned **zero** hits for "TRQ", "import licen", "tariff", "quota rent" and "sugar" in `rejected_and_parked.md` and `idea_register.md`, and zero in the baseline table; the family was **not** on the known-baseline list, so this was a genuine first-time screen, not a licensed re-screen. The construction was also written to be *complete without any external lookup* — all five steps (structural feature, participants and constrained flow, state and sign claim, three predictions, cost-floor reasoning) were completed from the register's own memory in Phase 0, which is what made the method test possible.

### The five routes and what each returned

**Route 1 — the publication: the state exists, and it is published *after* the flow has entered.** The ledger is real and it is what the construction named. CBP publishes a weekly **Commodity Status Report** for tariff-rate quotas, described by the agency as "designed to allow you to track quota as it is in the process of filling as well as giving the date and time of quotas already filled" (**S-413**), posted "**the first business day of every week**" (**S-414**, with the 19 October 2026 attachment). The regulator's own FAQ (**S-420**) is decisive on the register's two decisive limbs:
- **"The threshold is a quantity limit set in the automated system by HQ Quota that, when reached, triggers the weekly commodity status report that is posted on Mondays to display that quota as potentially filled. The percentage varies on a case-by-case basis at the discretion of HQ Quota, but is generally set at 95% of the restraint limit. This POTF status is advice to the filer."** The "potentially filled" flag is a **discretionary 95 % heuristic**, not the event.
- **"The Commodity Status Report for Tariff-Rate Quotas reflects the restraint limit, quantity entered to date (expressed numerically), and the percentage utilized … When a quota fills, the date and time of fill are indicated whenever possible … An updated report is posted on a weekly basis."** The **fill date is ex-post**, given only "whenever possible".
- The same page carries the FAQ "**Why do fill rates occasionally decrease from the amount I saw reported when I last checked utilization?**" — the published residual is **revisable**.
- The report's own weekly entries-to-date series is visible in USDA's monthly report, sourced to the "Customs and Border Protection Weekly Quota Status Report" (**S-428**): 11/4/2024, 12/2/2024, 12/31/2024, 2/3/2025, 3/3/2025, 3/31/2025, 4/28/2025, 6/2/2025, 6/30/2025, 8/4/2025, 9/1/2025, 9/30/2025.
- **Route 1 answer: the ledger exists, is weekly, is about one week behind the flow, and identifies the exhaustion only retrospectively.**

**Route 2 — the codified step: real, large, and published ex ante — and it is a *standing category*, not an event.** The in-quota aggregate quantity is announced in advance (**S-416**: FY2026 raw cane sugar **1,117,195 MTRV**, refined **22,000 MTRV**; **S-421**: the FY2027 notice, 91 FR 43080, 14 July 2026, repeats **1,117,195 MTRV** and **22,000 MTRV**, of which "**1,656 MTRV is reserved for specialty sugar**", with "**The FY 2027 specialty sugar TRQ will be opened on a first-come, first-served basis on October 1, 2026**"). The step's size is published: "for raw sugar, the tariff is set at **33.87 cents per kilogram or 15.4 cents per pound**; for refined sugar, it is set at **35.74 cents per kilogram or 16.21 cents per pound**", with freight "as low as **2-4 cents per pound for raw sugar**" (**S-418**), and the May 2026 Outlook repeats the **high-tier duty of 15.36 cents per pound** (**S-417**). The regulation makes the step a duty rather than a ban: "**Nothing in this subpart shall affect the ability to enter articles at the over-quota tariff rate**" (**S-433**), and the quota bulletin provides the two HTS rate classes and the certificate-of-quota-eligibility mechanic (**S-415**). **Route 2 answer: the step is real, quantified and published ex ante; the *exhaustion date* is not published ex ante, which is the construction's premise; and the ERS's own import decomposition shows "**High-tier duty**" as a **permanent standing category** of the programme, not an event (S-417).**

**Route 3 — the published literature: the family is a published baseline and the constructed state's price consequence is published economics.** The US sugar TRQ is the **worked case study of the US government's own textbook on tariff-rate quota economics** (**S-423**, Skully, *Economics of Tariff-Rate Quota Administration*, ERS TB-1893B), which states the two-tier model, the quota rent "**R = P − (1+t)**", the case distinction "**Quota is binding M3 = Q**" against "**Quota no longer binding M4 > Q**", the crucial sentence "**As long as imports satisfy domestic excess demand at a volume less than Q, the tariff quota functions as an ordinary tariff applied at the in-quota rate**", the sentence "**Tariff quotas are not considered quantitative restrictions because they do not limit import quantities. One may always import by paying the over-quota tariff**", and the sentence "**quota fill is not a reliable indicator of administrative performance**". The **post-2010** half of the guard: TRQ administration as an empirically studied trade barrier is published (**S-424**, Schaefer & Wolf, *Food Policy* 131, 2025 — Canadian dairy TRQ administration "**represented a 'binding' trade restriction beyond the negotiated quota**"); the **quota fill rate as a research object** is published, with an optimality analysis (**S-425**, Abbassi, *J. Industry, Competition and Trade* 26(1), 28 September 2026); and the **nearest published test of the sugar TRQ's own price effect** is published (**S-426**, Uri & Boyd 1994, *J. Economic Studies* 21(1) 16–40, a 14-sector CGE model of the effects of abolishing the TRQ "**on sugar prices and quantities**", with the companion 1995 paper at DOI 10.1300/j038v02n03_04). **Route 3 answer: the price consequence of a binding-versus-non-binding TRQ is published economics, the family is published, and the constructed state is a published research object. What is *not* published — and this is the honest residual — is the exhaustion-conditioned *spread-widening* test itself. The rediscovery guard therefore does not kill the construction outright; it removes the possibility that the mechanism is a discovery, and it is one of several independent reasons the construction fails.**

**Route 4 — tradability: the domestic leg *is* listed, and a false kill was caught and withdrawn.** The world leg is the benchmark raw-sugar contract, "**the world benchmark contract for raw sugar trading**", priced FOB country of origin (**S-419**). The domestic leg is **ICE Futures U.S. Sugar No. 16**, which "**prices the physical delivery of US-grown (or foreign origin with duty paid by deliverer) raw cane sugar at one of five U.S. refinery ports as selected by the receiver**", contract symbol **SF**, unit 112,000 pounds (**S-427**). **The interrupted Cycle-114 session had recorded, at S-418, that "both listed futures references (No. 11, No. 16) are world raw-sugar contracts" and had drafted a "the domestic leg is not tradable" caveat at S-419; both were wrong, and both were corrected in place this cycle.** The construction is therefore **not** killed on tradability, and this correction is recorded rather than quietly adopted — a "not tradable" kill would have been a *false* negative, and the register's own rule is that a wrong kill is as much a defect as a missed candidate. What *does* remain true is narrower: the **refined beet** leg, which is the price the ERS report and the construction's own measured US–world spread both use, is **not** a listed contract but a published cash series ("Refined beet sugar price, 3q Midwest"), and the No. 16 contract is also the price series the Sugar Program itself values against (**S-430**), which makes it *observable* as well as listed; its **liquidity is unestablished**.

**Route 5 — a second instance: the state generalises, and the generalisation produces no live candidate.** The EU publishes the same object. The Commission's Tariff Quota database page states that "**Most tariff quotas are managed by the European Commission on a 'first-come first-served' basis … Once the quota is reached, no further imports or exports under that quota is allowed**" and that "**In any given period, information about the current balances (remaining amount of the quota that is still available for use or allocation) of those tariff quotas which are managed on a first-come first-served basis is available on the Tariff quota consultation database**" (**S-429**). So the constructed state's *existence* is not a US artefact: an exhaustion moment published as a falling residual in a regulator's own database is a **general institutional form**. **But** the EU's out-of-quota duty is a published suspension rate and its steel-safeguard quota is the register's **already-closed template (A)/(C) family** (the published price of a constraint, **R-039**, **R-040**), so Route 5 confirms the state's generality and yields no candidate.

### Reasons (independent)

1. **G-13 fails, decisively and on the regulator's own words.** The decision the construction requires — to be positioned for the exhaustion moment — must be taken *before* the exhaustion moment, and the state that identifies the moment is published *after* the volume has entered: "**quantity entered to date**", weekly, "**the date and time of fill are indicated whenever possible**" (**S-420**), on a ledger posted "the first business day of every week" and dated to the preceding week (**S-414**). The "potentially filled" flag is a **discretionary 95 % threshold** and is "**advice to the filer**" (**S-420**), i.e. the regulator itself does not treat it as the event. This is not a marginal timing issue; it is the construction's own premise inverted. **The construction predicted this failure on itself** — the Phase-0 write-up recorded, at Phase 1 step 5, that "the ledger prints exhaustion only after the volume has entered, because the accounting quantity IS the flow" — and the regulator's own publication mechanics confirm it.
2. **The Cycle-52 third test fails for the same reason, and by the same words.** The register's third test is that a candidate's state must not be a **public real-time by-product of the flow the candidate would trade**. The construction's state *is* the flow's running accounting total. The register's own discipline on this test is unusually blunt, and it applies here without modification.
3. **The event's base rate for the named instance is near zero, and where the quota does exhaust the construction's premise is absent.** USDA's own monthly report for FY2025 records the WTO raw sugar TRQ at **403,378 of 1,117,195 MTRV entered — a 36 % fill rate, with 713,817 MTRV not entered** — and carries the agency's own **WASDE projected shortfall of 189,922 STRV** (**S-428**). The quota the construction names therefore does not exhaust, and the agency's own forecast says it will not. Where a sugar TRQ *does* exhaust, the mechanism is different in kind: the FY2027 **specialty** sugar sub-quota "**opened on Thursday, October 1, 2026, and over-subscribed at opening moment. The pro rata percentage of 44.58212%**" (**S-422**) — the exhaustion is **at the opening moment**, on a **known date**, and the rationing is a **published proration percentage**, not a price step. **Both the "unknown exhaustion date" and the "price step" are absent in the very instance where the exhaustion does occur.**
4. **The price-determining mechanism is elsewhere, and it is published in advance — which is the deepest kill.** The US sugar price is not set by the tariff step. 7 CFR § 1435.302(a)(1) requires the **overall allotment quantity** to be established "at a level: (1) **That is sufficient to maintain raw and refined sugar prices above minimum prices to avoid forfeiture of loans to the CCC**, but (2) Not less than **85 percent** of estimated quantity of sugar for domestic human consumption" (**S-430**), and the **loan rate is the floor**: "the national average loan rate to **24.00 cents per pound for raw cane sugar** and **32.77 cents per pound for refined beet sugar**" (FY2027, **S-432**). The FY2027 OAQ is set at **10,574,850 STRV = 85 % of 12,441,000 STRV** of estimated domestic consumption and is published in the Federal Register on 1 October 2026 (**S-431**). The agency's own model says the same thing: "**In the model, U.S. tariff-rate quota (TRQ) imports are not responsive to price movements … the level of high-tier imports is responsive whenever the margin between the U.S. and world prices exceeds the high duty and freight costs**" (**S-418**). So the binding constraint on US sugar supply is a **quantity announced in advance** and the price floor is a **statutory rate announced in advance**; the construction's tariff step is a **second-order duty wedge on a portion of imports that is usually not binding**. This is the register's "**the sign is already claimed and it is a magnitude**" problem in its sharpest form: the *price* of US sugar is a published, pre-announced administrative number, and the candidate's state conditions a wedge that does not set it.
5. **The family is a published baseline, and the constructed state's price consequence is published economics.** See Route 3. The register's rediscovery guard: a candidate whose *mechanism* is published economics is not a discovery, however carefully the state is specified. The published ERS model already states the construction's own conditional structure — a binding TRQ sets a different price regime from a non-binding one — and the fill rate is already a published object with a published optimality analysis (**S-425**).
6. **The cost floor and the horizon are not the binding constraints, and this is recorded so that a later session does not over-read the rejection.** The construction's cost-floor argument is **moot** rather than failed: the spread's *level* is a published, large, observable number (the ERS report records "**The spread between the U.S. refined beet sugar and world refined sugar prices was around 28 cents per pound … then fell sharply to less than 20 cents per pound**", **S-418**), and the round trip on two listed contracts is small relative to it. The rejection is **structural**, not quantitative, and reasons 1–5 are each independent of any cost or data-availability consideration.
7. **The false kill that was caught is itself a register-level finding.** The interrupted session's own partial ledger had already begun to build a tradability kill on a false premise (**S-418**'s "both listed futures references are world raw-sugar contracts"; **S-419**'s "the absence of a US domestic contract … is established jointly by this entry and by the price-series rows in S-418"). The direct read of the exchange's own specification (**S-427**) disproves it. **The register's rule that a wrong kill is as much a defect as a missed candidate was applied to the register's own work, in place, with the withdrawal recorded.**

### The measurement — the register's open question 5, answered

The Cycle-113 handoff asked five things of a construction-first method test. Here is the answer, in the register's own terms, from this cycle.

**(a) How far the mechanism could be pushed blind.** **All five steps**, and it could be pushed *further* than the register expected. The Phase-0 construction was completed **before any fetch**, from the register's own memory alone, and it was not merely a plausible sketch: it had a named institutional feature, a named flow, a named state, a named sign, a dated event structure, and three falsifiable predictions with a cost-floor argument. The register's own memory was sufficient to produce it. It was *wrong* in three specific ways, and each error is now a rule: (i) it assumed the tariff step was the price-determining feature (it is not — reason 4); (ii) it assumed the exhaustion event was a live, recurring occasion with a meaningful base rate (it is not — reason 3); and (iii) it assumed the domestic leg might not be tradable (it is — Route 4). **None of these three errors is of a kind the register's own memory could have caught.** They are all *institutional-fact* errors, and the register's memory contains no institutional facts.

**(b) The first screen limb the construction failed.** **Two, and they were the first two routes executed.** Route 1 (the publication) failed **G-13 and the third test**; Route 3 (the literature) failed the **rediscovery guard** on the mechanism. Both failed on routes executed early. Routes 2, 4 and 5 then produced *support* for parts of the construction (the step is real and large; the domestic leg is listed; the state generalises), and the two failures that remained were the ones that had to be found in the **external** literature and in the **regulator's own publication mechanics** rather than in the register's tables. **The decisive contrast with the register's ordinary screens:** R-039 through R-062 killed families **from register memory** — a family was killed because its state *kind* was already in the table, or its constraint template was already closed. **This construction could not have been killed that way, because nothing about it was in the register.** That is the real, and unwelcome, cost of the method.

**(c) Whether the constructed state satisfies G-13 and the third test on its own terms.** **No — and, uniquely in this register's history, the construction diagnosed its own failure before a single document was read.** Phase 1 step 5 of the pre-registered construction stated, in the register's own hand, that the accounting residual "IS the flow" and that the ledger therefore "prints exhaustion only after the volume has entered". The regulator's own FAQ then confirmed it verbatim (**S-420**). **This is the method's one clean, positive result:** a construction-first test, run honestly, catches the G-13 and third-test failures *in the construction itself* at the design stage, and does so without paying for the literature. It is a *design-time* screen, not an evidence-time screen, and it is cheaper than everything else the register does.

**(d) External sources required.** **Nineteen** new source identifiers (S-409 … S-427 and S-434 … S-438), of which **fourteen** are Cycle-114's own (S-420 … S-433) and **five** (S-434 … S-438) serve Priority 2 and Priority 3. Of the fourteen Cycle-114 sugar entries, **thirteen are line-verified** (fetched directly and text-extracted) and **one** is a **metadata-only** record (**S-425**). The **cost** of this screen in sources is roughly **four times** the register's typical family screen (R-062 consumed eleven for two sub-families; this consumed fourteen for one instance). The **cost** in *irreducible institutional facts* is the number that matters: **six** documents were needed that the register could not have known to need — the CBP FAQ, 7 CFR Part 1435, the CCC OAQ notice, the FSA loan-rate release, 15 CFR Part 2011 and the ERS TB-1893B model — and **each of them carries a limb that kills the construction**. **A construction-first screen is expensive in a way a family screen is not: the family screen knows in advance which six documents it needs, and the construction-first screen discovers them only by failing.**

**(e) Prior expectation against outcome.** The register's prior, as recorded in the Cycle-113 handoff, was that the construction would fail on "**the rediscovery guard or on the G-13/third test**". **It failed on both, and on two further limbs the prior did not name**: the **base rate** of the event (reason 3) and the **mis-identification of the price-determining mechanism** (reason 4). The mis-identification limb is the interesting one, because it is the limb a *family* screen would never have reached: a family screen asks whether a state can be found, and stops at "no published state of this kind" or "the state is already published"; it never asks *which instrument actually sets this market's price*. That question is a **mechanism** question, and it is the one that produced the deepest kill.

**(f) The recommendation on open question 5.** **The family screen remains the better unit of work, but not for the reason the register assumed, and the construction-first method is worth running under a narrower rule than "any occasion".**
- **What the family screen is better at:** cheap kills from register memory. R-039–R-062 were killed in minutes by matching a *state kind* or a *constraint template* already in the table. A construction-first screen cannot be killed that way, because by construction it is not yet in the table.
- **What the construction-first method is better at:** (i) it produces **a falsifiable mechanism with dated predictions**, not a list of families; (ii) it **diagnoses its own G-13 and third-test failures at design time** (finding (c) above), which is a screen the register does not otherwise have; and (iii) it produces a **measurement** — this entry — rather than a family-list, and the measurement is now a reusable register asset.
- **The recommended pre-registered rule, derived from this cycle:** *the construction-first method is worth running only when both of the following hold: (i) the constructed state is **not** an accounting by-product of the flow it would trade, and (ii) the constructed price wedge is **not** already in the known-baseline table.* Condition (i) is the design-time G-13/third-test check, which this cycle showed can be run on the construction alone, before any fetch. Condition (ii) is the family screen, used as a **cheap prior** on the construction — and it is exactly the check this cycle's construction failed on Route 3, having been run *after* the expensive part. **Run the family screen against the construction first, and it would have cost nothing.** The register's *hybrid* rule is therefore: **construct, then run the register's own state-kind and constraint templates against the construction, and only then go to the literature.** Under that rule this construction would have been stopped at zero source cost on the state-kind and template match, and the *deeper* finding — that the tariff step is not the price-determining feature of the US sugar market — would still have been lost. **That residual is the price of the cheap kill, and the register records it as such rather than pretending the cheap kill was complete.**

**Routes tried, and their failure modes.** (1) **Federal Register API** (`api/v1/documents.json` with `conditions[term]`, and `documents/{doc}.json` for metadata) and the **GPO full-text service** (`/documents/full_text/text/YYYY/MM/DD/{doc}.txt`) — **HTTP 200** throughout; used for the FY2026 and FY2027 TRQ determinations, the USTR allocations notice and the FY2027 OAQ notice. (2) **CBP** — the programme landing page and the weekly report page were fetched directly (**HTTP 200**); the **Quota FAQs** page was fetched directly through the web fetch tool (**HTTP 200**); **plain `curl` to `cbp.gov` is refused with HTTP 403 by the edge**, and the Commodity Status Report's **attached PDF could not be text-extracted** in this environment, so the ledger's *column schema* remains **index-rendered** while its *existence, cadence and lag* are line-verified. (3) **USDA FAS / ESMIS** — the FY2025 monthly sugar report PDF was fetched directly (**HTTP 200, 261,502 bytes**) and text-extracted. (4) **USDA ERS** — TB-1893B fetched directly (**HTTP 200, 33,676 bytes**); the Sugar and Sweeteners Outlook PDFs (S-417, S-418) were fetched directly by the interrupted session. (5) **eCFR versioner API** (`/api/versioner/v1/full/{date}/title-{N}.xml?part={P}`) — 7 CFR 1435 and 15 CFR 2011 both **HTTP 200**; the first attempt with the current date (`2026-10-21`) returned **HTTP 400** and was re-run with the last available issue date (`2026-10-19`). (6) **USDA FSA** news release — **HTTP 200**. (7) **ICE** contract specifications (`status.ice.com/api/productguide/spec/{id}/pdf`) — No. 16 fetched directly (**HTTP 200**); the ICE *price* page rendered with no chart data and no volume for the listed No. 16 contracts, so **liquidity is unestablished** and no claim is made. (8) **European Commission** taxation-and-customs page — **HTTP 200**. (9) **Crossref API** (`api.crossref.org/works`), **OpenAlex API**, **Semantic Scholar API** and the **EDGAR full-text search API** (`efts.sec.gov`) — all **HTTP 200** except Semantic Scholar, which returned **HTTP 404** for one DOI, and `papers.ssrn.com`, which returned **HTTP 403**; `sciencedirect.com` returned **HTTP 403**; `viewpoint.pwc.com` returned a **transport error**. (10) **Literature searches** on the TRQ family and on the published price effect of the sugar TRQ, located through the search index on 21 October 2026 and **not registered, not fetched, and no claim rests on any of them**: the EU steel-safeguard TRQ literature and the Commission's own safeguard notices; the US–Mexico sugar programme's import-licence regime; the RFS and ethanol-fuel standards' treatment of import-licence rationing; and the OPEC-style quota-allocation literature. **The route list is recorded so that a later session does not repeat it, and so that it is honest about what was verified and what was not.**

**Dated re-screen condition.** Re-screen the *import-licence rationing* family only if **(a)** a rationing authority publishes a **decision-time forward-looking** exhaustion indicator — a forecast or a pro-rata schedule — rather than a retrospective entries-to-date residual, at a documented cadence and before the decision; **(b)** the over-quota step is **demonstrated to be the price-determining feature of that market**, by the agency's own model or the exchange's own documentation, rather than a duty wedge alongside a pre-announced quantity constraint; **(c)** the exhaustion event has a **measurable base rate** over a documented multi-year window in which the residual actually reached its limit; and **(d)** a published test exists that conditions the price effect on the exhaustion state, with a sign that no held source already claims. **The US sugar TRQ does not satisfy any of (a), (b) or (c), and (d) is the one limb on which the literature is genuinely thin** — the honest residual, and it is recorded as a residual and not as a candidate.

**Decision:** **bounded negative (R-063).** The family is recorded in the known-baseline table (**row 97**); no dossier was created; no `IDEA-` identifier was consumed; **fourteen `S-` identifiers were consumed by this screen (S-420 … S-433)**, with five more (S-434 … S-438) serving Priorities 2 and 3; no score changed; no validation specification was added; and **no new gap is opened**, because the reasons are independent of data availability — the construction failed on the **publication ordering** (reason 1), the **third test** (reason 2), the **base rate** (reason 3), the **mis-identification of the price-determining mechanism** (reason 4) and the **published-baseline** limb (reason 5). The screen's product is a **rediscovery guard** (row 97), a **registered precedent** on what a construction-first screen costs and what it buys, **a caught false kill** (Route 4) recorded and withdrawn in place at S-418 and S-419, and **the register's answer to its own open question 5**, in the six-part measurement above.

---

## R-064 — The published-state-kind coverage map, and the Cycle-123 construction-first screen in three new state kinds (bounded negative; three pre-registered candidates; zero fetches)

**What this entry is, and why it is written as a coverage record rather than a family screen.** Every other entry in this log is a *family* screen: the register starts from a published state kind and asks whether an instance of it can be turned into a candidate. This entry is different in kind, and it is the register's **third** construction-first screen (after R-063 and the Cycles 115–121 screens), but it is written as a **map** because the register's own method has now reached the point where the binding constraint is no longer "which mechanism might work" but "**which kinds of published institutional state have not already been closed**". The nine state-kind and constraint templates (A–I), the sixty-three family rejections and the ninety-seven known-baseline rows together enumerate a **finite space of published state kinds**; a construction-first attempt can only start inside that space. This entry (i) records the enumeration, (ii) runs the **three state kinds pre-registered in `cycle_notes.md` before the cycle's first fetch** against the narrowed rule and the template programme, and (iii) states the residual honestly.

### The narrowed rule being applied, and the order of the screen

The rule is R-063's, unchanged: **(i)** the constructed state must **not** be an accounting by-product of the flow it would trade; **(ii)** the constructed price wedge must **not** already be in the known-baseline table; **(iii)** the register's own **state-kind and constraint templates must be run against the construction before the literature**; and the strict bar applies throughout — a **published decision-time state**, **sign-determining rather than magnitude**, with an **unclaimed sign**, no **published baseline**, and a **feasible clean test**, plus the Cycle-52 third test (the state must not be a public real-time by-product of the flow). Under rule (iii) the literature is reached **only** by a survivor; all three candidates below were killed **before** any fetch, and **Priority 1 consumed zero source identifiers**.

### D1 — the published statutory budget-transfer state

- **The construction, as pre-registered.** *State:* the published annual transfer amount of a sovereign wealth fund to the national budget, defined by statute as a **fixed fraction of the fund's published portfolio valuation**. *Flow:* the dated annual transfer executed by the fund's manager on the state's behalf. *Instance candidate (named in advance, to be verified only if the construction survived the screen):* a national sovereign wealth fund operating under a statutory transfer rule.
- **Register-memory check (before any literature).** Not in the 97-row known-baseline table; not in R-001 … R-063; **not matched by templates A–I** — the state is not the price of a constraint (A), not a fee or toll (C), not a count or queue (D), not a reserve or headroom (E), not a breach or waiver (F, G), not a change of law (H), and not a clamp or cap band on a continuously published state (I). The *rule* behind the transfer is statutory (an H-adjacent object), but the *state* the construction conditions on is a published **transfer amount**, which none of the nine templates describes. **The construction is therefore admissible and must be tested on the narrowed rule's own clauses.**
- **Kill 1 — the accounting-by-product clause (narrowed rule (i)).** On the state as the **transfer amount**, the amount is *defined* as a fraction of the fund's published portfolio valuation, so the state **is** the flow's own accounting quantity: the flow's size and the state's value are the same number, scaled by a statutory coefficient. This is the sugar TRQ's own Phase-0 diagnosis in a different garment — there, "the accounting quantity **IS** the flow"; here, the accounting quantity **is** the flow's size, and the flow is the only thing the amount refers to. A state that is a scaled copy of the flow cannot be an *exogenous* conditioning variable, and the register's third test fails with it: the state is a public real-time by-product of the flow it would trade.
- **Kill 2 — the two-branch reading, and G-12/G-13 on the surviving branch.** If the state is instead taken to be the **published portfolio valuation** — which is not by-product of the flow — the construction still fails, on two further grounds. (a) **Magnitude, not sign:** the valuation's *level* determines how big the dated transfer is; it does not determine a *direction* in any price. A rule that conditions the size of a flow is a magnitude rule, and the register's bar requires a sign-determining state. (b) **Anticipation:** the amount of a statutory annual transfer is a **budget figure**, published in advance of the transfer date, and the transfer date itself is fixed by the statute. A state that is published in advance and attached to a pre-announced dated flow is the definition of an anticipated flow; G-13 and G-12 fail together, and no surprise can attach to the event.
- **Verdict: bounded negative at the design stage; zero fetches.** The kill is structural and does not depend on any institutional fact about any particular fund; the instance was never verified, and the register records that it was not needed.

### D2 — the published remaining-capacity state for a constrained participant class

- **The construction, as pre-registered.** *State:* the published daily balance of a third party's remaining capacity under a published cap (for example a foreign-participant investment or ownership capacity balance published by the exchange or the regulator). *Flow:* the constrained class's rationed trades at exhaustion. *Instance candidate (named in advance, to be verified only if the construction survived the screen):* a daily-published foreign-participant capacity balance at an Asian or emerging-market exchange.
- **Register-memory check (before any literature).** Not in the known-baseline table; not in R-001 … R-063; **matched by template (I)** — a published clamp or cap band of a continuously published state (**R-046**, known-baseline **row 75**). The state *is* a published band on a continuously published quantity, and the event the construction trades is the **crossing** of that band.
- **Kill — template (I), with R-046's own reasons applied.** The band crossing is a **deterministic function of an already-published number**; the band's role in the construction is a **filter** (rationing binds) rather than an **edge** (the state determines a sign); the effective sample is the **number of band exits**, which is small and discrete. R-046's recorded reasons — "published clamps, caps and bands on published states are ubiquitous", the crossing is deterministic, and the role is a filter — apply without modification. **The template match alone is sufficient**; the register does not need to reach the literature, and the construction is not carried further.
- **Second, independent kill — the obligor's own flow.** The constrained party is the party whose trades are rationed; it sells or buys *for its own account* because a published cap binds it. That is the **S-151 analogue** the register has recorded repeatedly (R-020's third reason, R-027's third reason): the constrained seller is the obligor, and its own flow is not a third-party concession another arbitrageur can capture.
- **Verdict: bounded negative at the design stage; zero fetches.**

### D3 — the published pending-release queue state

- **The construction, as pre-registered.** *State:* the published queue (length × per-unit size) of pending, non-discretionary releases of an asset. *Flow:* the future dated release of that supply into the liquid market. *Instance candidate (named in advance, to be verified only if the construction survived the screen):* a public blockchain's published validator-exit queue.
- **Register-memory check (before any literature).** Not in the known-baseline table; not in R-001 … R-063; **matched by template (D)** — a published count or queue state at decision date (**R-041**, known-baseline **row 70**). The construction's state is a *published queue*, which is template (D)'s object exactly.
- **Kill — template (D), with R-041's own reasons applied.** R-041's recorded reasons are that published counts and queues are ubiquitous, that the count is a **level** rather than a sign-determiner, and that the state therefore conditions **magnitude**; the register's construction adds a per-unit size so that the product is a *scheduled* supply, which moves the state from a level to a **calendar** — and the calendar of dated third-party releases is a **published baseline** (the crypto token-unlock schedule family, known-baseline **row 45** — *corrected in Cycle 125; the original text of this kill read "row 17", which is the CIP-bases family* — and R-014).
- **Verdict: bounded negative at the design stage; zero fetches.**

### The coverage map — the register's published-state-kind space, as it now stands

This is the reusable product of the cycle. Each row is a **kind of published institutional state**; "closed by" names the entry that closes it. The map is the register's *lower bound* on the space, not a proof of exhaustiveness, and the cycle states that limit rather than asserting closure.

| # | Published state kind | Closed by |
| --- | --- | --- |
| A | The **price** of a funding, balance-sheet or capacity constraint (repo specialness, lending fee curves, CDS financing spreads, standing-repo award rates) | R-039 (row 68) |
| B | **Pair-level netted positions** (a convergence crowd) | G-28 structural null |

**Cycle-125 correction to this map (read before using it).** The **known-baseline row citations in rows 10–20 above do not reconcile with the baseline table's own numbering**, and the map is now superseded by the **corrected, extended map in R-066**, which states the numbering convention, its three anchors (R-063 → row 97; templates A–I → rows 68–75; Cycle 123 → row 98) and the corrected row sets for every family row. Two specific errors are corrected in place here: **row 10's citation "rows 21, 30, 51, 57, 82"** is replaced by **rows 15, 19, 21, 45, 50, 54, 58**, and **row 11's citation "rows 5, 6, 23, 25, 27, 97"** by **rows 22, 23, 43, 53, 55, 97** (RIN, EU ETS, California cap-and-trade, RFS, CBAM and the sugar TRQ — the families row 11 actually names). The **D-3 kill below** cites the crypto token-unlock schedule as "known-baseline **row 17**"; that is wrong — row 17 is the CIP-bases family and the token-unlock family is **row 45**. The kill itself is unaffected (it rests on template D and on R-041's recorded reasons); only the citation was wrong. **Rows 1–9's template citations (68–75) and row 21's row 98 are correct.** R-066 adds rows 22–24 and the three new rediscovery guards (rows 100–102).

### The residual, stated honestly

- **Rows 1–9** are the **template programme**; they are closed *as templates*, meaning that any construction whose state is one of those kinds is stopped at zero cost. Rows 10–20 are the **family screens**, closed on published-baseline and rediscovery grounds. **Row 21 is the only state kind this cycle added**, and it was killed by the narrowed rule's own accounting-by-product clause and by anticipation, before any literature was consulted.
- **The honest limit of the map.** The map is derived from the register's own memory of what it has screened; it is a **lower bound** on the space of published state kinds, not a proof that no twenty-second kind exists. A kind outside the register's prior art would be, by construction, invisible to a register-memory check, and the register has no instrument that enumerates the space of published institutional records. **This is the structural reason the register's own open question 1 ("can the register produce a candidate at all?") remains open**, and it is a *coverage* problem, not a *method* problem: the method works (R-063 measured it); the *provenance of new state kinds* is what the register lacks.
- **What would change the answer.** A construction-first attempt becomes admissible again only when its state kind is **outside** rows 1–21 **and** survives the accounting-by-product clause, the sign-versus-magnitude test, the anticipation test and the template screen. The register's own best route to a new state kind is therefore not another re-run of a known domain but a **record-first** scan: enumerate the *published institutional records* themselves (a regulator's or venue's continuous publications), derive the state kinds from them, and screen the derived kinds against the map above. That route is the register's recommended Priority 1 for the next cycle, and it is named here so that the next session inherits it rather than re-deriving it.

**Decision:** **bounded negative (R-064).** The three pre-registered constructions are killed at the design stage, with the killing clause or template named in each case; **no dossier was created**; **no `IDEA-` identifier was consumed**; **no `S-` identifier was consumed by this screen (zero fetches)**; no score changed; no validation specification was added; and **no new gap is opened**, because each kill is structural and independent of data availability. The screen's product is the **coverage map** above, the **row-98 rediscovery guard** for the one new state kind, and a **named replacement route** (the record-first scan) for the next construction-first attempt.
